# 01 EXECUTIVE SUMMARY – MESSY MIDDLE POWERTRAIN SERVICE & MAINTENANCE

No portion of this report or accompanying materials may be copied, reproduced, or distributed in any manner without express attribution to the North American Council for Freight Efficiency.

Vehicle servicing and maintenance are critical factors to successful, profitable and safe freight operations. Uptime is crucial to fleet economics, as the saying goes, "if the wheels ain't turnin', fleets ain't earnin'." Keeping trucks, trailers, drivers and loads continuously moving requires skilled technicians in well-equipped shops staying on top of the ever-evolving vehicle technologies. This report is a guidebook for fleets to understand service and maintenance ramifications of heavy-duty trucks in the Messy Middle of rapidly evolving new truck technologies.

Vehicle complexity has increased since the first true freight trucks replaced horse drawn wagons in the early 1900s, but the industry has seen an explosion of new technology choices since 2020. Those choices now include:

* Increasingly more efficient versions of diesel engines with ever improving emission reduction systems.
* New alternative fuels in various forms and blends including renewable diesels and renewable natural gases.
* Battery electric heavy-duty trucks with high power charging infrastructure.
* Hydrogen fuel cell trucks with high-pressure fueling infrastructure.
* Autonomous vehicles and their support infrastructure.

NACFE has labeled this period the Messy Middle reflecting an abundance of viable technology choices in a rapidly evolving and increasingly competitive freight marketplace. The pace of innovation has increased, requiring technicians and drivers to continuously learn about new functionalities and systems. This increasingly complex world is happening in parallel to the burgeoning use of artificial intelligence, AI, to facilitate troubleshooting and streamline operations.

02 EXECUTIVE SUMMARY – MESSY MIDDLE POWERTRAIN SERVICE & MAINTENANCE

For fleet maintenance managers, all these alternative trucks must seem like icebergs, their true magnitude is much larger than just the visible trucks. These new technologies often require new:

* Maintenance procedures
* Safety protocols
* Building equipment
* Shop infrastructure
* Specialized training
* Service tools
* Service software
* Tracking procedures
* Fueling infrastructure
* Auditing
* Certifications and recertifications
* Record keeping systems
* Much more

There are no free rides. Operating freight trucks has always required attention to service and maintenance. These new vehicles will also be operating in parallel with a wide range of older, traditional fossil diesel trucks, that will continue to need attention for years to come. The service life of heavy-duty trucks can exceed 20 years. Vehicle geriatrics, the support of aging vehicle platforms, will become increasingly challenging as newer technologies are not backwards compatible with older vehicles.

Keeping trucks running requires well-trained technicians supported with appropriate tools in shop facilities capable of dealing with the breadth of technologies. See Figure ES1. Support will require growing expertise that may necessitate specialists in key systems like sensors, computers and high-voltage systems alongside traditional expertise in mechanical systems like brakes, suspensions, frames, interior trim, HVAC, tires, air systems, etc.

Demand for trained service technicians has never been higher than today. The future will require significant efforts to entice and keep talented technicians. On-going training, certification and recertification will increase as well as the pace of innovation continues to accelerate.

Truck and trailer manufacturers create new technologies. They also provide support for them in the field with computer based service tools, over-the-air software updates, databases of failure histories, and factory training for service technicians. OEMs are critical to arming service technicians with current information, tools and parts to manage the new vehicles entering the market. They are also crucial to providing information and parts to keep those vehicles running over their long service life.

Fleets that operate their own shops need to encourage hiring, training, and motivating technicians. The shortage

of adequately trained technicians receives much less attention than potential shortages of drivers, but is equally concerning to successful operations. See Figure ES2. The demands of supporting a growing mix of technologies in the Messy Middle will challenge fleet investment choices.

Fleets that use third-party support services need to press their providers to stay ahead of the technology demands. The key metric for service is downtime, i.e., how long it takes to diagnose and repair a vehicle. Fleets will encounter delays due to supply chain part shortages for proprietary critical components. Fleets may see repeat visits to service the same issue when inexperienced technicians do not solve the root cause of problems. Some vehicles may get tied up for excessive time due to inadequate and inexperienced troubleshooting. Third-party service centers will need to bring their A-game to attract and retain fleets or lose those accounts to more capable competitors.

In the past, the path for training technicians has seen growth in technical schools focused on heavy-duty truck curricula. This trend will continue as new technologies demand greater focus. Complex technologies will require certification and on-going recertification to ensure quality maintenance and repair, and to maintain safety.

Shops will need to follow new protocols including increasingly critical lockout/tagout procedures, secure areas for authorized work with high voltage systems, sensors and safety systems for dealing with dangerous and potentially explosive gases, fire suppression methodologies, and more.

The development and updating of industry standards will facilitate safe operations and designs. Those standards will cover infrastructure like fueling and charging, as well as shop design and construction requirements and will influence vehicle system designers. The rapid pace of innovation means standards that take years to evolve often lag technology deployments. Industry standards' organizations will need to accelerate processes to get standards in place more rapidly.

Since the dawn of commercial trucking, a good deal of servicing could be done by backyard mechanics. Necessity often also required drivers to do impromptu servicing in the field to get their trucks to their deliveries on time or to get to the next service center. Today, technologies are becoming so complex that simple diagnostic and repair are no longer possible. OEMs will need to provide sufficient system redundancies to help drivers in the field safely limp vehicles and loads to the next service center.

OEMs, fleets, shops and third-party developers have growing opportunities to mine extensive data now available on vehicles and operations using AI tools to improve predictive maintenance and reduce lost time during reactive maintenance and troubleshooting. Call centers, warranty centers, distribution centers, load

<!-- page: 14 (unnumbered) -->

boards, telematics systems, supply chains, and more are collecting massive amounts of digital data that can be employed to efficiently improve fleet uptime and reduce maintenance and service downtime. Technicians and service centers at the cutting edge of AI use will discover competitive advantages over traditional approaches.

Parts availability and distribution will be challenging in the Messy Middle. Many pieces of modern vehicles are proprietary in nature and early production is very limited. Spares and replacement parts will be competing with OEM factories that are building complete new vehicles. The availability of OEM- specific high-capacity batteries, high-pressure carbon fiber tanks and associated plumbing, unique sensors, etc. will be limited during the early years of market penetration. These parts likely will carry cost premiums as well, which may limit the ability to parts distribution centers to stock any significant volume of spares. Many parts may not be serviceable on site in repair bays and will need to be sent to special factories for repair. A "core" system where new parts are exchanged for failed ones will likely be needed, with the faulty parts being sent to distant specialists for repair.

Software has become integral to vehicles and infrastructure. See Figure ES3. The nature of software updates are nearly continuous. This will be true for vehicles, for infrastructure and especially for digital-based service tools. Where a toolbox full of wrenches and sockets used to be the go-to equipment for technicians, today's technicians start with digital-based service tools controlled by software that use programmed diagnostics to isolate issues.

While a great deal of focus to streamline servicing multiple vehicles in the field is on over-the-air update capabilities, physically touching trucks will be necessary in order to service other parts of the vehicle. Traditional skills for non-software based diagnostics will be needed. Listening to drivers will also be critical, as they have first-hand experience with the vehicles in use.

One last significant element of service and maintenance in the Messy Middle will be including infrastructure service and maintenance as a crucial part of keeping trucks moving. Uptime and downtime metrics apply equally to battery charging systems, hydrogen and natural gas fueling systems, and renewable diesel tanks and pumps. If the trucks can't fuel, they are no better than trucks stuck in a service bay. Many fleets and service facilities will have on-site fueling for these alternative powertrains, adding additional layers to resiliency planning. Back-up systems may also be necessary such as on-site generators running on a variety of power sources, on-site micro-grids and battery storage to help balance loads on grids, and micro.

This report was developed as part of a cooperative class project between NACFE and the University of California at Riverside. The primary author, Marcos Alvarez, conducted extensive interviews with OEMs, fleet service managers, subject matter experts, and NACFE personnel over the course of nearly a year under the direction of NACFE. Alvarez participated in NACFE's Run on Less – Messy Middle including site visits to a participating fleet, and attended the ACT Expo 2025 event including NACFE's introduction session on Run on Less. His research also included an extensive literature search on relevant topic material.

This report is a guidebook for OEMs, fleets, drivers, dealerships, shops and technicians on the breadth of complexity facing truck and trailer maintenance and service in the Messy Middle. The report highlights the growing resources available to help the industry improve technician training and help prepare service center operations to better serve fleet needs.

Important findings in the report, as illustrated in Figure ES4, are:

1. Maintenance costs are a significant factor in investing in new trucks. Those costs also often include facility infrastructure maintenance, special equipment and training for technicians, and on-going auditing and record keeping.
2. The skills of the technicians and the tools available to them will continue to be key to high uptime. Intelligent OEM service tools and fault-diagnosis systems will be critical to reducing downtime.
3. Service of natural gas, hydrogen, battery electric, and bio-fuel systems demand greater attention to safety and equipment protocols than traditional diesels.

<!-- page: 15 -->

<!-- page: 15 (unnumbered) -->
05 EXECUTIVE SUMMARY – MESSY MIDDLE POWERTRAIN SERVICE & MAINTENANCE

4. Training technicians and keeping that training current will need to be a priority for service managers, including tracking and auditing skills and certifications. The pace of technology change will require continuous training and recertifications.
5. Computers will likely be the first tools needed to diagnose and service vehicles. They also will need to be kept current with appropriate software licensing and regular hardware updating that will need to be factored into costs.
6. OEM warranties will become even more critical as the complexity of vehicles increases with new technologies.
7. For the foreseeable future, standards and procedures will be rapidly evolving as fleets gain experience and provide feedback to OEMs. Staying aware of changing standards and staying compliant may be an additional labor cost to factor into investment planning.
8. Behind the fence fueling infrastructure maintenance will be as challenging and may be necessary to keep trucks running. Public infrastructure maintenance or lack thereof may also factor into vehicle uptime.
9. Predictive maintenance enhanced by artificial intelligence systems will greatly aid technicians.
10. Specialization in new technologies is inevitable for service technicians as the complexity of vehicles continues to grow. This may require additional staffing to cover all the critical new technologies to keep fleets moving.

Service and maintenance in the Messy Middle presents challenges and opportunities. The growing list of choices for heavy-duty truck powertrains means greater diversity of technologies will need to be supported by trained technicians operating with the most current digital tools. The industry will need to encourage new technicians to enter the field and will need to help current ones stay up to date on new technologies. It's an exciting time as the industry transitions to newer systems.

# 1 INTRODUCTION

Vehicle servicing and maintenance are critical factors to successful, profitable and safe freight operations. Uptime is crucial to fleet economics and keeping trucks, trailers, drivers and loads continuously moving requires skilled technicians in well-equipped shops staying on top of the ever-evolving vehicle technologies.

Vehicle complexity has increased with an explosion of new technology choices since 2020. The new technologies include alternative-fueled vehicles — those powered by renewable diesel, biodiesel, compressed natural gas, battery electric and hydrogen fuel cells. The vehicles are going to require new maintenance procedures, shop infrastructure changes, service tools, specialized training for technicians, service software and more.

Uptime is key regardless of powertrain choice, so maintenance and repair will continue to be a critical factor in the successful operation of a fleet.

The future will require significant efforts to entice and keep talented technicians. On-going training, certification and recertification will increase as well as the pace of innovation continues to accelerate. The demands of supporting a growing mix of technologies in the Messy Middle will challenge fleet investment choices.

# 2 SCOPE

This report is a guidebook for OEMs, fleets, drivers, dealerships, shops and technicians on the breadth of complexity facing truck and trailer maintenance and service in the Messy Middle. The report highlights the growing resources available to help the industry improve technician training and help prepare service center operations to better serve fleet needs.

It outlines not only how each propulsion technology works but also what is required to successfully deploy, maintain, and sustain it in real-world fleet operations. From workforce development and safety training to diagnostic infrastructure and data-driven maintenance, the focuses is on turning technical possibilities into operational realities.

# 3 NACFE'S MISSION

NACFE's overriding principle in reporting on technologies is to provide an unbiased perspective. NACFE recognizes that it also has vested interests and an agenda. NACFE's mission is to improve the efficiency of North American goods movement. NACFE pursues this goal in two ways: By improving the quality of the information flow and by highlighting successful adoption of technologies. NACFE's Confidence Reports assist in information flow, while the Run on Less demonstrations and Fleet Fuel Studies highlight adoption success and provide benchmarks for fleets.

NACFE also recognizes that emerging and future technologies need an unbiased review to help fleets consider them and for manufacturers to learn and adapt their products and solutions to better meet fleets' needs. To that end, NACFE started publishing Guidance Reports on emerging and future technologies. As of mid-2025, NACFE has published 11 Guidance Reports on electric and hydrogen fuel cell trucks.

NACFE shares its research free of charge to the industry, conducting and participating in information-sharing events including workshops, panel discussions, and conferences.

# 4 REPORT METHODOLOGY

NACFE used its proven methodology of investigating available prior research and information on the topic and then contacting tractor, truck and trailer manufacturers, fleets, and trucking industry organizations to gather information on maintenance practices, technician training and certification, and tools and equipment that will be needed to successful maintain vehicles in trucking's Messy Middle.

# 5 THE STATE OF THE ART

The era of an all-purpose general mechanic capable of fixing any truck with wrenches, sockets, and incredible skill is fading. The latest, modern machines require technicians with specialized expertise tailored to each platform. Today's essential tools are no longer limited to a wrench and a socket; they also include diagnostic laptops, high-voltage safety and diagnostic equipment, secure connectivity, and the ability to integrate software, hardware, and mechanical knowledge to effectively repair a vehicle.

We are living and working in a time that the North American Council for Freight Efficiency (NACFE) has dubbed the Messy Middle — a period of transition before the trucking industry reaches the carbon-neutral movement of freight, which is marked by an inefficient scattering of developing truck technologies and public policy shifts [120]. The recission of California's Advanced Clean Fleets (AFC) regulation has kept the door open to continue to develop technologies other than battery electric vehicles (BEVs) like renewable diesel (RD), renewable natural gas (RNG), hydrogen (H2 or H2 internal combustion engine (H2ICE), hybrid, and other solutions. The industry's goal is to make these low- and zero-emission vehicles the best and most efficient choice for a fleet. Although achieving 100% freight efficiency and business utilization is currently beyond our reach, pursuing freight efficiency through technological advancements drives innovation in trucking.

Working on these trucks is different from operating them, and knowledge of operational safety is important for all invested parties. Each technology requires its own set of standard operating procedures, maintenance philosophy, safety protocols, and diagnostic tools. This report examines the evolving service requirements for advanced technology trucks, beginning with conventional internal combustion engines (ICE) and the implications of renewable fuels, then progressing to BEVs, fuel cell electric vehicles (FCEVs), and H2ICE vehicles.

A description of the technology would be useless without an implementation plan — one that connects innovation to the technicians, fleets, and service networks responsible for keeping these advanced trucks on the road.

This report seeks to lay the foundation for bridging that gap by outlining not only how each propulsion technology works but also what is required to successfully deploy, maintain, and sustain it in real-world fleet operations. From workforce development and safety training to diagnostic infrastructure and data-driven maintenance, the focuses is on turning technical possibilities into operational realities. This bridge is only being planned; it is not complete. We are currently generating the data for these techniques.

# 6 ADVANCED ICE

Internal combustion engines remain the backbone of North American freight operations, but their characteristics today differs profoundly from those of the mechanical workhorses of the 1990s. Where performance was once dominated by displacement and turbocharger sizing, today's ICE platforms are digitally

<!-- page: 18 -->
Messy Middle Powertrain Service & Maintenance
June 25, 2026 Page 18

governed machines where uptime, emissions compliance, and durability are inseparably tied to software calibration, aftertreatment health, and over-the-air diagnostics.

The communication between systems on these modern trucks are codified in SAE International J1979/ISO 15031-5, which sets the diagnostic messaging standard for heavy-duty vehicles, requiring that engines demonstrate not only mechanical reliability but also clear, interoperable diagnostic reporting across electronic control modules and CAN bus networks. For fleet managers, ICE uptime is no longer solely a function of rotating parts but also of software release governance, parts compatibility, and diagnostic compliance, all of which directly impact operations.

The long arc of ICE development is illustrated in Figure 1, which charts OEMs' maximum horsepower from 1991 through 2024 against regulatory milestones. The sharp drop in horsepower during the Environmental Protection Agency's (EPA) regulatory window from 2004 to 2007 reflects the compromises required to meet early emissions targets, followed by recovery and eventual performance gains through the adoption of selective catalytic reduction (SCR), high-pressure fuel injection, and advanced combustion geometries. Executives can interpret this history as evidence that compliance-driven R&D delivers productivity dividends, while technicians must recognize that aftertreatment integration is no longer peripheral; it is central to performance and durability.

## 6.1 ICE Advantages

One of the most profound shifts in ICE technology has been the restructuring of emissions equipment from a compliance burden into an enabler of efficiency. As Arvind Thiruvengadam, principal engineer for Advanced Engines at Paccar Inc., explained, "If you can get the calibration and the refinement of SCR to be highly efficient,

<!-- page: 19 -->
Messy Middle Powertrain Service & Maintenance
June 25, 2026 Page 19

that means it allows the engine to go into a mode where you can technically emit higher NOx [114]." So as engineer started developing SCR, engine efficiency increased, according to a UC Riverside CE-CERT Seminar in 2024 [79]. Investment in aftertreatment calibration unlocks not only cleaner operation but also improved fuel economy and power density because of SCR's ability to defeat this increased NOx created by the engine. Executives and technicians therefore learned that aftertreatment systems should be maintained with care and monitored appropriately.

Recent OEM platforms reflect the enhanced abilities of modern engines. The Cummins X15 Efficiency Series delivers up to 605 hp while meeting EPA 2027 emissions standards and achieving a 4% base-engine fuel-economy gain in 2024 models compared to previous versions [27]. (See Figure 2.)

Car and semi-truck Cd are not directly comparable, even though they are affected by trailer length [117]. Ultimately, these changes impact how efficiently the truck slices through the air.

## 6.2 ICE Challenges

While modern ICE platforms offer unprecedented integration of power, emissions control, and diagnostics, these benefits come with new vulnerabilities. The NOx–soot trade off remains a central issue; while optimizing combustion for lower soot production you increase NOx, and vice versa. As PACCAR's Thiruvengadam observed, "There is always a soot-to-NOx trade off. You must play this game between not regenerating your diesel particulate filter (DPF) versus controlling your NOx. The problem arises when tweaking those knobs. You are invariably going to affect the durability of the engine [114]." For decision-makers choosing a powertrain, these trade-offs when balancing compliance costs against the risk of downtime. Meanwhile, technicians face the challenge of maintaining DPF regeneration frequency, SCR catalyst performance, and turbocharger health within narrow tolerances or manipulating the emissions system.

Another challenge ICE platforms face lies in the durability of high-pressure fuel and air management systems. Modern engines such as the Cummins X15 and PACCAR MX-13 operate at injection pressures up to 2,500 bar and rely on variable-geometry turbochargers (VGTs) to balance diverse duty cycles [27]. As Thiruvengadam explained, "A company like PACCAR would use a specific type of turbo, which is the variable geometry turbo. We do about 2000 to 2500 bar injection pressures. The injectors are so advanced that we can do about 10 split injections, but we do not do that. It all comes down to durability [114]." For technicians, this underscores the importance of precise calibration and verification after servicing. For executives choosing a platform, this insight may highlight the trade-off between a high-performance model with lots of power and a less robust but more reliable unit.

<!-- page: 24 -->

## 6.3 ICE Maintenance

Maintenance of advanced ICE vehicles begins with oil governance because oil chemistry dictates both component longevity and regulatory compliance. Unlike earlier eras when API CI-4 oils offered wide latitude across fleets, today's high-output engines are calibrated for low-viscosity FA-4 lubricants, which are more specialized [123].

According to Fullbay, these oils are designed for engines built in and after 2017. The API description states that FA4 refers to XW-30 oils meant for select diesel engines that comply with 2017 standards governing on-highway greenhouse gas emissions. The oils must be suitable for highway use, with diesel fuel sulfur content up to 15 ppm.

The CK4 description is broader. Also suitable for use in four-stroke-cycle high-speed diesel engines, these oils meet the 2017 non-road Tier 4 exhaust emission standards. However, this oil category replaces CJ4 lube technology. The formulation makes CK4 suitable for diesel fuels with sulfur content up to 500 ppm. The API notes, though, that using this category of oil with fuel that has more than 15 ppm sulfur could affect the oil drain frequency as well as the exhaust after-treatment system durability.

That only presents a problem if you do not mix oil types [124].

OEMs such as Cummins, PACCAR, and Daimler Truck explicitly warn that substituting CK-4 oils outside FA-4 envelopes risks voiding the warranty due to shearing, viscosity breakdown, and inadequate high-temperature protection. Shell emphasizes the ability of its Rotella FA-4 line, to maintain protective film strength at 100°C while reducing parasitic drag in rotating assemblies such as crankshafts, camshafts, and turbocharger bearings [125]. For executives, the implication is that oil procurement contracts must align with OEM specifications. At the same time, technicians must ensure the correct grade and specific oil types are consistently and appropriately used, especially across mixed fleets.

Oil drain intervals have likewise transformed from "one-size-fits-all" to digitally validated programs. Cummins' Oil Guard sampling system enables approved fleets to extend oil drain intervals up to 80,000 miles (about 128,748 km), provided laboratory analysis confirms acceptable viscosity retention and oxidation stability [126]. This represents a 60% increase over the 50,000-mile baseline still enforced for conventional CK-4 oils in heavy-duty applications. PACCAR's MX-13 maintenance guidelines recommend 75,000 miles (about 120,701 km) under regular duty cycles and 35,000 miles (about 56,327.04 km) under severe duty, explicitly warning that failure to use FA-4 oils or to sample at 10,000-mile increments voids the oil-drain extension [62]. Daimler Truck's Detroit DD15 aligns with this, offering 75,000-mile drain intervals paired with 500,000-mile valve lash checks, underscoring the interdependence between lubrication management and valvetrain stability [28]. Together, these OEM schedules demonstrate that oil governance has become a digitally validated compliance process.

Maintenance schedules, though codified in OEM guides, must be adapted to real-world duty cycles. NACFE's Mihelic emphasizes that most fleets haul 65,000 lbs. (about 29,483 kg) gross or less, with payloads averaging 35,000 lbs. (about 15,876 kg) and highway speeds of 60 to 70 mph. In such applications, horsepower demand rarely exceeds 250 hp, making oil drain extensions feasible if operators adhere to proper sampling and maintain aerodynamic discipline [117]. Conversely, fleets operating under high loads or demanding conditions, such as construction, logging, or mountainous terrain, should have shorter change intervals and increase sampling frequency, as elevated soot levels and thermal stress accelerate oil degradation. Specifying discipline involves not only matching horsepower and oil intervals to the duty cycle but also balancing these factors against efficiency objectives.

<!-- page: 25 -->
Messy Middle Powertrain Service & Maintenance
June 25, 2026 Page 25

Duty cycle variations present opportunities for optimization. Connected diagnostics have changed maintenance programs, integrating oil sampling programs with over-the-air (OTA) systems such as Volvo Remote Programming, Kenworth TruckTech+, Peterbilt SmartLINQ, and Cummins Connected Solutions [127] [128] [129] [130]. These platforms enable technicians and fleet managers to validate ECU updates, monitor diagnostic trouble codes, and check calibration integrity before a vehicle enters service [127]. More information on this topic is in Section 11 of this paper.

In summary, oil sampling programs establish a baseline for fleet and for a mixed-fleet representative sample, enabling precise assessments. This oil sampling program is coupled with a digital and connected OTA maintenance program.

In maintaining ICE vehicles, the last task is to understand the truck's communication networks per SAE J1939-73, which defines standardized diagnostic messaging across modules and sensors, also known as the CAN network. This CAN network will transmit a stream of data from hundreds of sensors and modules that control and communicate within the truck, as well as back to headquarters. More information on the CAN network can be found in Section 14 and Appendix C of this paper.

Another important consideration in advanced ICE maintenance is coolant management is. Cummins' Service Bulletin 3666132 specifies that fully formulated coolants must contain balanced antifreeze, supplemental coolant additives (SCA), and buffering compounds premixed 50/50 with deionized water, ensuring electrochemical stability across aluminum, iron, and mixed-metal engine architectures [373]. Failure to use deionized or demineralized water accelerates electrolysis, which manifests not as visible corrosion but as voltage-driven pitting and cavitation inside radiators, heater cores, and cylinder liners.

John Frala, professor emeritus of Advanced Transportation at Rio Hondo College, emphasizes that electrolysis is often misdiagnosed as corrosion but, in reality, represents a systemic electrical failure. "Electrolysis attacks not only radiators and heaters but can destroy an engine. Although a small voltage can be detected in most engine cooling systems, as a result of a reaction between the coolant and metals, the voltage should never exceed 0.1 V in vehicles with aluminum engine blocks or cylinder heads. Most radiator failures diagnosed as corrosion are in fact electrolysis-related, making technician training critical in preventing repeat failures [131]."

This testimony reinforces that coolant governance is not merely a matter of chemistry but also of electrical diagnostics. Training curricula must therefore equip technicians to pair chemical checks (pH, nitrite/molybdate levels) with electrical monitoring, ensuring systems are stabilized before irreversible pitting occurs. At the same time, we are discussing deionized water for ICE, deionized water and exact fluid specifications are critical for use in BEVs because if a fluid is not deionized it can conduct electricity, disrupting grounding, creating issues and potentially frying electronics.

Finally, condition-based, predictive- and prescriptive-maintenance are increasingly layered on top of the oil and diagnostics foundation. At the condition-based level, fleets integrate oil analysis, soot loading, and coolant chemistry reports to schedule interventions only when thresholds are crossed. Predictive systems ingest these data streams into machine-learning models to forecast part-wear trajectories. Prescriptive maintenance closes the loop by issuing automated recommendations tied to OEM service intervals.

More on these maintenance strategies, detailed workflows, and case studies are discussed in Section 9 of this paper. Here, their mention reinforces that oil governance and testing, diagnostics, and connected services are the ground floor for advanced maintenance strategies.

## 6.4 Future ICE

Looking well beyond conventional diesel ICE, experimental architectures such as Reactively Controlled Compression Ignition (RCCI) demonstrate how heavy-duty ICE might evolve. While out in the distant future, RCCI uses dual fuels with different reactivities — typically a low-reactivity premix (natural gas, gasoline, ethanol) and a high-reactivity pilot (diesel), to precisely control ignition timing and reduce soot and NOx simultaneously [132]. In these engines, the squish zone — the area between the piston head and the cylinder at top dead center, where the fuel is mixed in a turbulent action by the movement of the piston — is modified to improve fuel mixing. Deere's patent on an RCCI engine describes the squish zone as "a specially contoured recess in the piston crown designed to enhance fuel–air mixing and reduce soot formation [133]." A cross-sectional view of this specialized piston crown geometry, designed to enhance mixing, is provided in Figure 8.

> **Figure 8: Squish zone piston crown geometry [133]**
> Type: Schematic / Technical drawing
> Axes: None
> Series: None
> Values: Pip +0.14 mm, Bowl depth -8.7 mm, Squish height +3.3 mm, Bowl radius +12.3 mm
> Notes: Cross-sectional view of modified (extended bowl) piston crown geometry.

These modified piston bowls reduce flame "quenching," a critical phenomenon in combustion, involving the extinction of flames when they come into contact with a cooler surface or enter a narrow passage leading to a lower flame temperature and decreasing reaction rate, and of course, soot formation [374].

## 7.1 Renewable Diesel

Renewable diesel (RD), also known as hydrotreated vegetable oil (HVO), is produced by reacting vegetable oils, animal fats, or recycled cooking oils with hydrogen at a high temperature (300°C to 400°C, 30 to 80 bar) over nickel-molybdenum (NiMo) or cobalt-molybdenum (CoMo) catalysts in a hydrotreating unit. (See Figure 10 for a diagram of an HVO plant.) This process removes oxygen and saturates the hydrocarbons, yielding a fuel that is chemically identical to petroleum diesel. Because RD meets ASTM D975 specifications, fleets can use it at up to 100% concentration as a drop-in replacement without modifying engines, fueling infrastructure, or storage systems [98].

Jeff Seger, NACFE's clean energy consultant, said "Renewable diesel meets ASTM D975 petroleum diesel specifications, enabling seamless fleet adoption, while biodiesel is governed under ASTM D6751 and is blended in accordance with manufacturer guidelines to ensure performance and warranty compliance." This alignment with the same standard used for ultra-low sulfur diesel (ULSD) allows fleets to integrate RD without risk of warranty conflicts or misfuelling. This distinction makes RD more operationally flexible than biodiesel.

<!-- page: 28 -->
Messy Middle Powertrain Service & Maintenance
June 25, 2026 Page 28

> **Figure 10: Hydrotreated vegetable oil production [135]**
> Type: Schematic / Process flow diagram
> Axes: None
> Series: None
> Values: None
> Notes: Diagram showing hydrotreated vegetable oil (HVO) production plant steps including Vegetable Oil (P1), Hydrogen, R1, C1, S1.1, S1.2, S1.3, S1.4, R2, S2, S3, Effluent gas, Purge, LPG, Naphtha, Kerosene, Diesel, Water-phase, NaOH solution.

Again, the primary advantage of RD lies in its drop-in capability. This compatibility minimizes disruption for mixed fleets, where vehicles of varying age and specification must share fueling infrastructure.

RD also delivers measurable performance improvements. Its cetane number typically ranges from 65 to 80, far exceeding the ASTM minimum of 40. A higher cetane number improves ignition quality, enabling faster starts, reducing the risk of misfire, and improving combustion [45].

Cold-flow performance is another differentiator; RD has a pour point near -14°C, making it suitable for most climates. When blended with ethanol or winterization additives, operability can be extended to colder regions, with studies reporting blend pour points as low as -51°C [376]. This weather-reliability feature eliminates the need for extensive seasonal storage management, a significant concern for fleets operating in northern regions.

To conclude, Matt McLelland, vice president of sustainability and innovation at Covenant Logistics, emphasizes its near-term value. "This is available today. This is affordable. And just because it is not a zero-emission solution does not mean that it is not a great thing we should start doing right now [136]." For fleets, balancing cost, regulatory compliance, and uptime, RD offers a ready lever for decarbonization without the infrastructure burden of electrification.

RD offers excellent storage stability and remains stable in storage for up to 12 months without significant degradation [98]. For fleets with seasonal equipment or backup generators, this property significantly reduces maintenance costs associated with clogged filters and microbial growth. Fleet executives have described RD as a pragmatic and scalable bridge solution.

The lubricity and other benefits of Chevron renewable diesel and high-quality biodiesel that deliver a lower-carbon, high-performing, 100% renewable fuel, are illustrated in Figure 11 [16]. Small additions of

<!-- page: 29 -->
Messy Middle Powertrain Service & Maintenance
June 25, 2026 Page 29

biodiesel to renewable diesel rapidly reduce wear scar diameter, allowing BD/RD blends to meet the ASTM lubricity requirement with as little as 2% biodiesel, while higher biodiesel blend levels further decrease wear toward the B100 value. This enhanced lubricity helps limit wear on engine components and fuel pumps, supporting smoother, cooler operation and potentially reducing downtime and maintenance compared with RD.

### 7.1.1 Renewable Diesel Challenges

Even with its high cetane number, SCR systems require exhaust gas temperatures above 200°C for urea injection to effectively reduce NOx. A concern for fleets using aggressive idle-reduction strategies is that such strategies can inadvertently lower exhaust temperatures below the 200°C threshold, thereby compromising NOx conversion. This would be true for all fuels, internal combustion engines, and aftertreatments to different degrees; however, it is mentioned here because RD also has the added property of creating more ultra-fine particles.

Material compatibility presents another challenge. Chevron Renewable Energy Group (REG) notes that elastomers such as nitrile rubber and certain gasket compounds can swell or shrink with changes in the RD blend ratio, requiring fleets to schedule proactive inspection and replacement cycles [15]. For technicians, this means that post-switch seal checks must be logged accurately in maintenance management systems and that the correct seal or part is used; an incorrect part may be installed regardless of fit.

Finally, climate limitations remain under-documented. While performance has been validated down to -14°C, there is insufficient Tier-1 data confirming operability at extreme arctic temperatures (below -20°C). Until more

<!-- page: 30 -->
Messy Middle Powertrain Service & Maintenance
June 25, 2026 Page 30

comprehensive testing is published, fleets in cold climates should exercise caution with RD and maintain winterization protocols [98].

### 7.1.2 Renewable Diesel Safety

From a safety and compliance perspective, RD is treated identically to petroleum diesel. It is governed by NFPA 30: Flammable and Combustible Liquids Code, which sets requirements for storage, transfer, and spill control [137]. For executives, this means no new fire-suppression or containment systems are required when switching to RD.

At the technician level, handling protocols remain the same as for ULSD — grounding during transfers, standard PPE, and Occupational Health & Safety Administration (OSHA) compliance for exposure limits. Unlike CNG or hydrogen, RD poses no new high-pressure or cryogenic hazards. This simplifies fleet training because safety briefings need not be rewritten beyond existing diesel fuel-handling standards.

### 7.1.3 Renewable Diesel Maintenance

Maintenance outcomes with RD are positive but require attention to detail. Cleaner combustion results in lower soot accumulation in DPFs, reducing backpressure, and extending regeneration intervals. The SAE paper Effects of Fuels on Compression Ignition Engine Particle Size Distribution and DPF Filtration Efficiency confirmed that "the use of renewable fuels may be beneficial in terms of reduced soot loading, leading to reduced backpressure and less frequent regeneration of the DPF [138]."

The report said that while "neat" renewable diesel (neat signifying 100% B100 biodiesel fuel) produced moderately low soot, it ranked third after ULSD and biodiesel in terms of particle emissions. This suggests only a mild improvement in DPF soot burden relative to ULSD [138]. This means operators can expect longer regeneration intervals, but not eliminate filter service. According to the paper, there is also a difference in how a DPF loads because of the microfine particles RD creates. The DPF does not have sufficiently small filter holes to capture soot when fresh. It only begins to work more effectively once the DPF is "pre-loaded" or in operation.

Finally, RD's superior storage stability reduces maintenance costs associated with clogged filters and microbial growth. Whereas biodiesel requires aggressive tank management, renewable diesel can remain stable for a year or longer, simplifying logistics for fleets with seasonal or backup assets [98]. This translates into reduced downtime and fewer unplanned maintenance events, improving tractor productivity per service hour.

## 7.2 Biodiesel

Biodiesel is a renewable fuel produced from triglyceride-rich feedstocks such as vegetable oils, animal fats, or recycled cooking oils. The process used to create biodiesel is described in Figure 12. Unlike renewable diesel, which is hydrocarbon-based, biodiesel consists of fatty acid methyl esters (FAMEs) created through transesterification. In this chemical process triglycerides react with methanol in the presence of a base catalyst such as sodium hydroxide, yielding biodiesel and glycerin as a byproduct. [139]. Because of its ester chemistry, biodiesel has different material compatibility and combustion properties compared to petroleum diesel.

<!-- page: 31 -->
Messy Middle Powertrain Service & Maintenance
June 25, 2026 Page 31

> **Figure 12: Flow chart of converting vegetable seeds to biodiesel [377]**
> Type: Flow chart / Diagram
> Axes: None
> Series: None
> Values: None
> Notes: Flow chart showing Step 1 (Preparation of seeds, Sun dried and decorticated, Roasted for 10mins or solar heated, Extraction (mechanically/solvent)), Step 2 (Purification of oil, Sedimentation/boiling with water/filtration), Step 3 (Processing of oil -> Pyrolysis (absence of air and nitrogen -> Liquid fractions), Micro-emulsification -> Micro-emulsions, Transesterification -> Biodiesel).

The production of biodiesel relies on a fundamental chemical process known as transesterification. In this reaction, triacylglycerols — the primary component of vegetable oils and animal fats — are reacted with a type of alcohol (typically methanol) in the presence of a catalyst. This process cleaves the glycerol backbone of the fat molecule. It replaces it with alkyl groups, yielding two distinct products: alkyl esters, which serve as biodiesel, and glycerol, a byproduct. This chemical transformation lowers the viscosity of the original oil to a level comparable to petroleum diesel, making it suitable for use in compression-ignition engines. The molecular mechanism of this reaction is detailed in Figure 13.

<!-- page: 32 -->
Messy Middle Powertrain Service & Maintenance
June 25, 2026 Page 32

> **Figure 13: The transesterification reaction of triacylglycerol with alcohol [140]**
> Type: Chemical reaction diagram / Schematic
> Axes: None
> Series: None
> Values: None
> Notes: Shows Triacylglycerol (Vegetable Oil) + Alcohol in the presence of Catalyst yielding Alkyl ester (Biodiesel) + Glycerol.

ASTM International regulates biodiesel under ASTM D6751: Standard Specification for Biodiesel Fuel Blend Stock. This standard ensures fuel quality and defines requirements for oxidative stability, acid number, and cold-flow properties. In commercial use, biodiesel is most often blended with petroleum diesel to balance performance and durability. These blends are labeled with a "B" number, such as B10 (10% biodiesel, 90% diesel) or B20 (20% biodiesel, 80% diesel). Higher blends, such as B50 to B100, are restricted to niche or captive fleets with modified equipment.

Biodiesel is governed under ASTM D6751 and is blended in accordance with manufacturer guidelines to ensure performance and warranty compliance, unlike renewable diesel, which conforms directly to ASTM D975. This means that biodiesel adoption carries OEM-specific blend limits, whereas renewable diesel adoption can be seamless.

### 7.2.1 Biodiesel Advantages

Biodiesel offers a range of operational, economic, and environmental advantages that make it an attractive near-term decarbonization strategy for commercial fleets. Its most fundamental benefit is lubricity enhancement. The desulfurization process that produces ULSD also removes many of the polar compounds responsible for natural lubricity. As a result, ULSD without additives often fails to meet ASTM D6079, which limits the high-frequency reciprocating rig (HFRR) wear scar diameter to 520 micrometers. Adding as little as 2% B2 restores lubricity and reduces wear scar diameters to well below this limit, thereby protecting high-pressure pumps, injectors, and other precision components ([83]. This is comparable to the renewable diesel lubricity characteristics. For technicians, this improvement means reduced wear rates, fewer injector replacements, and longer fuel-system life without reliance on aftermarket additives or proprietary lubricity improvers.

Beyond protection, biodiesel contributes directly to environmental stewardship. It is both non-toxic and biodegradable, with a half-life in soil of less than 30 days, dramatically shorter than that of petroleum diesel. According to the U.S. Department of Energy's Alternative Fuels Data Center, spills of neat biodiesel or low-level blends pose significantly lower risks to soil and groundwater quality than comparable petroleum spills [141].

<!-- page: 33 -->
Messy Middle Powertrain Service & Maintenance
June 25, 2026 Page 33

These characteristics lower exposure under environmental liability and remediation frameworks, particularly for fleets operating in coastal, agricultural, or wetland areas.

From a combustion perspective, biodiesel promotes more complete combustion and reduces tailpipe emissions of CO, HC and PM because of its higher oxygen content. Peer-reviewed studies demonstrate that B20 blends reduce CO and PM emissions by 16% to 17% and HC emissions by 12% compared with petroleum diesel, while NOx emissions on average show no net change (0.6% ± 1.8%) [142]. Biodiesel also offers operational flexibility and energy security advantages. Blends such as B5 and B20 can be used interchangeably with ULSD in most OEM-approved engines, enabling fleets to reduce lifecycle greenhouse-gas emissions by 10% to 16% at B20 and up to 74% at B100, depending on feedstock source and production pathway [141]. Unlike many alternative fuels, biodiesel can be stored and dispensed through existing diesel infrastructure with only minor procedural adjustments. This translates to a low-risk, low-investment pathway to emissions reduction and Renewable Fuel Standard (RFS) compliance.

Additionally, biodiesel can improve fire safety because its flash point typically exceeds 130°C, which is higher than that of petroleum diesel, reducing the likelihood of accidental ignition during handling or storage. The Association Quality Management Biodiesel's (AGQM) European fleet data confirm that biodiesel's higher flash point and lower volatility make it inherently safer for on-site storage, particularly in enclosed or high-temperature facilities [143]. This is especially relevant for large depots and municipal fleet yards where aboveground tanks are used near maintenance operations.

Finally, biodiesel strengthens domestic and regional fuel security by being produced from renewable agricultural or waste feedstocks, diversifying fuel supply chains, and supporting local economic development. The US Energy Information Administration notes that incorporating biodiesel into regional diesel pools can offset imported petroleum volumes and stabilize fuel availability during supply disruptions. This enhances procurement resilience and helps meet corporate sustainability goals through measurable Scope 1 emissions reductions.

Biodiesel's combination of lubricity enhancement, emission reduction, safety, and supply-chain resilience makes it a practical transitional fuel for fleets pursuing decarbonization without the infrastructure and cost burdens associated with zero-emission technologies.

### 7.2.2 Biodiesel Disadvantages

While biodiesel provides measurable sustainability and lubricity benefits, it also introduces technical challenges that must be carefully managed in fleet operations. The most pressing issue is cold-weather operability. High-blend and neat biodiesel (B50 to B100) solidify between -3°C and 0°C depending on feedstock composition, forming wax crystals that restrict flow and clog filters. Once gelled, engines can experience fuel starvation or stall. Fleets in northern climates must use heated storage, insulated delivery lines, or winter blending with ULSD to maintain operability. AGQM specifies that winter-grade blends be stored at least 5°C above their cloud point and recommends B5 or B10 for sub-freezing operation [143]. ASTM International defines cold-flow metrics such as cloud point, pour point, and cold filter plugging point (CFPP) for grades No. 1-B S15 and S500 to ensure consistent winter performance [4].

Another critical limitation is oxidative stability. Biodiesel oxidizes faster than petroleum diesel when exposed to heat, oxygen, or light, leading to gum, sediment, and acid formation that can foul injectors and corrode tanks. ASTM D6751 specifies minimum induction periods and acid number limits to ensure stability during storage [4]. AGQM adds that biodiesel should be stored in dark, dry, and sealed tanks, rotated every three months, and never blended with residual fuel to prevent peroxide buildup [143]. This requires disciplined inventory control; for technicians, it demands vigilant tank inspection and sampling schedules.

<!-- page: 34 -->
Messy Middle Powertrain Service & Maintenance
June 25, 2026 Page 34

Material compatibility presents another concern. The ester chemistry of biodiesel can soften or swell natural rubber, polyurethane, and some nitrile components, while copper, brass, and zinc surfaces accelerate fuel degradation. AGQM and the Biodiesel Handbook recommend stainless steel, Viton, fluoropolymer, or Teflon for all wetted components [140] [143]. Technicians converting legacy systems must therefore audit all seals, gaskets, and fittings to verify compatibility before introducing high-blend biodiesel.

A final limitation involves engine oil dilution. Because biodiesel volatility is lower than that of ULSD, small quantities of unburned fuel can migrate past piston rings into the crankcase. This dilution lowers lubricant viscosity and can accelerate wear if drain intervals are not shortened. The DOE recommends shortening oil-change intervals by 20% to 30% for B20 and higher blends and monitoring oil dilution via routine analysis [144]. Executives should incorporate these maintenance adjustments into life-cycle cost models and preventive maintenance budgets.

When fleets adhere to ASTM D6751 and D7467 standards, maintain strict storage controls, and follow OEM blend limits, biodiesel can deliver reliable service while minimizing the risk of fuel instability or component wear.

### 7.2.3 Biodiesel Safety

From a safety and environmental perspective, biodiesel exhibits clear advantages over petroleum diesel. It is non-toxic, biodegradable, and possesses a high flash point, making it inherently safer to handle, store, and transport. The DOE's AFDC classifies biodiesel as having low acute toxicity and a flash point exceeding 130°C, reducing the likelihood of accidental ignition [141]. This safety margin translates into lower insurance premiums and reduced regulatory exposure under environmental liability laws.

However, biodiesel biodegradability introduces risks of microbial contamination. Microbes thrive at the fuel-water interface, forming organic acids and sludge that corrode tanks and plug filters. AGQM identifies water management as the single most important preventive measure. Operators must regularly drain tank bottoms, install desiccant breathers, and apply biocides when contamination is detected [143]. Technicians should test water and microbial growth quarterly using ASTM D6974 methods.

Spill and exposure safety also favor biodiesel. Biodiesel contains minimal aromatics and evaporates slowly, lowering inhalation and fire risks during cleanup. DOE data confirm that neat biodiesel biodegrades 98% within 28 days under aerobic conditions [144].

Regarding fire safety, while biodiesel's flash point is significantly higher than that of diesel, fleets must still comply with OSHA and NFPA storage regulations for combustible liquids. Aboveground tanks should include flame arrestors and grounding systems, particularly in hot-weather regions where static discharge is a concern [143]. By combining lower environmental toxicity with high flash-point stability and manageable microbial-control practices, biodiesel provides fleets with a safer, more compliant operational fuel platform.

### 7.2.4 Biodiesel Maintenance

In addition to the oil dilution and freezing-weather considerations, successful biodiesel adoption depends on disciplined maintenance and quality control procedures.

During initial conversion, biodiesel's natural solvent properties can clean existing deposits from storage tanks and fuel systems. This cleaning effect loosens accumulated varnish and particulates that may clog filters during the first 30 to 60 days of use. Technicians should increase filter-inspection frequency and keep spare elements on hand until system equilibrium is reached [143]. Following stabilization, filter-change intervals typically return to normal.

<!-- page: 35 -->
Messy Middle Powertrain Service & Maintenance
June 25, 2026 Page 35

Storage systems require periodic inspection for water, corrosion, and microbial contamination. Tanks should be sampled at multiple points, especially at low points where water accumulates. AGQM recommends quarterly inspections and annual cleaning for large-fleet tanks, including internal coating checks and gasket replacement [143]. For aboveground tanks, paint or wrap in white to minimize solar heating and prevent fuel oxidation.

When fleets implement these procedures — accelerated early-stage filtration, shortened oil intervals, seasonal blending, and disciplined tank management — biodiesel delivers stable performance, consistent emissions reductions, and long-term engine durability equivalent to conventional diesel. Current OEM positions regarding biodiesel and renewable diesel compatibility are summarized in Figure 14.

# 8 RENEWABLE NATURAL GAS

RNG also called biomethane, is chemically identical to fossil-fuel CNG and consists entirely of methane (CH4). Unlike fossil natural gas, RNG is produced by capturing biogenic methane from landfills, wastewater treatment plants, and agricultural digesters, then upgrading it through scrubbing, dehydration, and CO2 removal to pipeline-quality fuel [98]. The RNG production and treatment lifecycle is depicted in Figure 15. Because the upgraded fuel meets the exact specifications of fossil CNG, it can be stored at the same pressures (up to 3,600

<!-- page: 36 -->
Messy Middle Powertrain Service & Maintenance
June 25, 2026 Page 36

psi), distributed through existing natural gas pipelines, and used in conventional natural gas engines without modification.

> **Figure 15: RNG/LNG Treatment Stages and Biogas End Uses [216]**
> Type: Schematic / Flow diagram
> Axes: None
> Series: None
> Values: None
> Notes: Diagram showing anaerobic digestion products, biogas treatment (moisture removal, flare, siloxane/sulfur removal, compressor, CO2 removal), and biogas end uses (heating, electricity, other industrial uses, fertilizer, soil amendment, animal bedding, other innovative uses).

NACFE's Mihelic explains there is no difference between methane from the ground versus landfill. "The engine does not know or care if it is fossil or renewable gas, it is all the same. What changes are the carbon intensity of the fuel and the upstream environmental impact [117]." This makes RNG a renewable, drop-in option for fleets already using CNG, unlocking significant environmental benefits for organizations.

## 8.1 RNG Advantages

One of the most significant advantages of RNG lies in its emissions profile. When RNG is combusted in modern natural gas engines, it produces exceptionally low emissions, achieving near-zero NOx levels. NOx emissions reductions can reach up to 90% below current EPA limits [145]. Additionally, RNG virtually eliminates PM emissions, making it a cleaner alternative to conventional fossil fuels.

Operationally, RNG simplifies aftertreatment. Unlike diesel, RNG engines require neither DPFs nor SCR. Paul Sandsted, director of technology & sustainability at TTP (formerly NGVAmerica), said, "An NGV exhaust system consists only of a simple three-way passive catalyst that does not require urea and performs well in any vehicle

<!-- page: 37 -->
Messy Middle Powertrain Service & Maintenance
June 25, 2026 Page 37

application or duty cycle [378]." However, ammonia formation can still occur under specific rich operating conditions, as shown in Figure 16.

> **Figure 16: Ammonia formation mechanism over Pd/Rh [379]**
> Type: Schematic / Diagram
> Axes: None
> Series: None
> Values: None
> Notes: Diagram illustrating exhaust temperature (250 ~ 550° C), aging induced OSC loss, low air-to-fuel ratios, high durations in rich operation leading to High NH3 emissions with a catalytic converter with a TWC (Three-Way Catalyst).

These three-way catalysts convert CO, HC, and NOx. While the aftertreatment system's primary function is to convert NOx into CO2, water, and nitrogen (N2), it is important to be aware that ammonia (NH3) can form as a by-product during transitions between lean and rich combustion. This is particularly true under high load or transient fueling events. Understanding how this occurs, and how to identify and correct it in the field, is not just a matter of emissions compliance and aftertreatment hardware protection, it is a critical step in mitigating potential risks [146].

During lean operation (air-fuel equivalence ratio lambda > 1.0), the exhaust contains excess oxygen. Here, the catalyst plays a crucial role: it actively oxidizes CO and unburned hydrocarbons and stores oxygen within its ceria-zirconia oxygen-storage component (OSC). This "oxygen buffering" capability is the catalyst's way of maintaining balance, keeping it near the stoichiometric point. When the engine transitions to rich operation (lambda < 1.0), the stored oxygen is released, and the catalyst environment becomes fuel rich. In this phase, NOx species are reduced over palladium and rhodium sites, often forming N2 as the desired product. However, if CO and H2 concentrations remain high, part of the NOx reduction proceeds via an alternative pathway that produces NH3. The catalytic mechanism driving this reaction is shown in Figure 17 [146].

<!-- page: 38 -->
Messy Middle Powertrain Service & Maintenance
June 25, 2026 Page 38

> **Figure 17: Active sites for CH4 catalytic oxidation over Pd/CeO2 [146]**
> Type: 3D molecular / chemical structure diagram
> Axes: None
> Series: None
> Values: None
> Notes: Shows molecular representation of CH4, O vacancy atom, Pd atom, CeO2, showing catalytic oxidation mechanisms.

Research shows that ammonia (NH3) formation in heavy-duty CNG exhaust systems is most pronounced when exhaust temperatures range from 250°C to 550°C [146]. This temperature range is typical for cruise and mid-load vehicle operating conditions in these vehicles. At lower temperatures, the catalyst reaction is limited because of slow kinetics; conversely, when temperatures exceed approximately 550°C, the ammonia produced is rapidly decomposed back to nitrogen and hydrogen.

Furthermore, the study highlights the impact of catalyst thermal aging, which frequently occurs in fleet service environments. As the oxygen storage component (OSC) of the catalyst degrades over time, its capacity to store and release oxygen diminishes. This deterioration causes the exhaust mixture to oscillate more dramatically between oxidized and reduced states, leading to the persistence of richer gas pockets within the catalyst. As a result, NH3 selectivity increases significantly, with aged catalyst units producing several times as much ammonia as newer ones, even under identical operating conditions.

The current regulatory frameworks leaves both methane and ammonia slip unresolved. SAE studies have shown that unburned methane, a highly potent greenhouse gas, can escape through catalysts, especially at low exhaust temperatures. Existing EPA standards focus exclusively on non-methane hydrocarbons, leaving a gap in how methane emissions from these sources are regulated and accounted for in lifecycle assessments. For executives tasked with environmental, social, and governance (ESG) reporting, it is important to acknowledge this regulatory blind spot when presenting the carbon benefits of RNG technologies.

Methane slip in CNG-fueled heavy-duty engines occurs when unburned CH4 passes through the combustion chamber and catalyst without complete oxidation. The phenomenon is most pronounced during cold starts, extended idle, and rapid transitions between load states, when exhaust temperature remains below the catalyst's effective "light-off" range of 350°C to 400°C [147] [148]. Within this range, palladium-based oxidation

<!-- page: 39 -->

sites begin to activate, but methane’s strong C–H bond limits conversion efficiency until temperatures exceed
roughly 450°C — consequently, any exhaust outlet temperature sustained below 350°C guarantees partial
methane breakthrough [146]. Field diagnostics should therefore include continuous exhaust-temperature
monitoring; a prolonged sub-light-off condition during idle or low-load operation indicates CH4 slip and
incomplete catalyst warm-up.

Sensor behavior is equally critical to controlling and diagnosing methane slips. Wideband lambda sensors must
respond within ≤100 milliseconds to maintain stoichiometric balance; values are confirmed for Bosch LSU 4-
series sensors and comparable devices [149] [150]. A lean bias (lambda > 1.05) cools exhaust gases and suppresses
oxidation, while a rich bias (lambda < 0.98) limits available oxygen — both sharply reducing CH4 conversion efficiency
[380]. During visual inspections, technicians should examine the catalyst housing for thermal distress or
contamination. Chalk-white or bluish hues often indicate overheating. In contrast, oily or glassy films indicate
siloxane or lubricant poisoning of the palladium active sites [381]. A healthy catalyst operating above 450°C,
with accurate sensor feedback, will sustain near-complete methane oxidation, keeping both greenhouse gas
emissions and downstream ammonia formation within regulated fleet limits [151]. To read the sensor signal, a
diagnostic laptop, software, and a vehicle-computer interface device, such as a Nexiq, are required.

A modern wideband oxygen (UEGO) sensor measures mixture strength not by “switching” across stoichiometric
as a narrowband sensor does, but by maintaining a constant oxygen balance within a microcavity and reporting
the effort required to hold that balance [217]. Exhaust gas is admitted through a diffusion gap into a small
chamber that faces two zirconia elements — a measurement cell (the Nernst cell) and an electrochemical
oxygen pump. The Nernst element develops a potential that depends on the oxygen partial pressure difference
between the cavity and the reference air. The controller treats ~0.45 volts as its stoichiometric setpoint. The
internal structure of a planar wideband oxygen sensor is detailed in Figure 18. Any deviation — up or down —
means the oxygen fraction in the cavity no longer matches stoichiometric conditions.

> **Figure 18 — Planar wideband oxygen sensor structure [217]**
> Type: schematic
> Axes: none
> Series: none
> Values: none
> Notes: Diagram showing R_cal, I_PUMP, V_S I_PUMP, V_S, H+, H-, exhaust gas, diffusion gap, pump cell, Nernst cell, reference cell, heater element, air.

The sensor’s internal design combines two electrochemical cells, a Nernst cell and an oxygen pump cell, that
work together to keep the oxygen level inside a small diffusion chamber at a steady balance point.

<!-- page: 40 -->

As the exhaust passes the sensor, the Nernst cell electrochemically measures the oxygen concentration and
outputs a small voltage. When the voltage deviates from the nominal reference of about 0.45 V, the controller
drives current through the pump cell. If the mixture is lean (too much air), the pump cell removes oxygen from
the chamber to restore balance. If the mixture is rich (too much fuel), the current reverses, and the pump
generates oxygen by splitting water vapor and carbon dioxide within the chamber. The current required to
maintain this balance indicates to the controller how rich or lean the mixture is. That current is then translated
into a digital air-fuel ratio signal for the engine control unit.

In natural gas engines, this continuous, proportional feedback is critical, unlike narrowband sensors, which
toggle between “rich” and “lean.” A wideband sensor measures the full range of air-fuel ratios in real time. This
is especially important because natural gas engines often run in lean-burn mode, using more air than
stoichiometric to improve fuel economy and reduce NOx emissions. Some engines operate at ratios as high as
22:1, far leaner than gasoline systems can tolerate. To diagnose these systems, technicians follow the sensor
verification procedure outlined in Figure 19.

### Catalyst Inlet and Outlet Oxygen Sensors

1. Cummins Troubleshooting and Repair Manual
2. DVOM
3. INSITETM
4. Propane Enrichment Tool

| 12v heater supply | Wire terminal _________ | Wire terminal _________ |
| 12v heater return | Wire terminal _________ | Wire terminal _________ |

**Step 2:**

**Step 3:**

> **Figure 19 — Catalyst inlet and outlet oxygen sensor diagnostic procedure [152]**
> Type: schematic / text procedure
> Axes: none
> Series: none
> Values: none
> Notes: Figure 19: Catalyst inlet and outlet oxygen sensor diagnostic procedure [152]

During a propane enrichment test like the one shown in Figure 20, the oxygen sensor voltage response provides
a fast, reliable diagnostic snapshot of both sensor health and catalyst function. When propane is introduced into
the intake, the engine temporarily runs rich, causing the healthy upstream sensor voltage to rise to around 0.9

<!-- page: 41 -->

V. As the propane is cut off, the air-fuel mixture becomes lean, causing the voltage to drop below 0.1 V before
returning to regular oscillation. A properly functioning catalyst and downstream sensor exhibit dampened
voltage swings, showing that oxygen storage in the catalyst smooths fluctuations. By contrast, a faulty or
contaminated upstream sensor responds sluggishly to changes in the mixture, producing shallower, delayed
peaks.

In contrast, a dead sensor remains fixed near 0.45 V, indicating loss of electrochemical activity. If the
downstream trace mimics the upstream curve, the catalyst is no longer storing oxygen effectively — a signature
of catalyst failure. This waveform, measured across rich and lean phases within a 20-second window, validates
the upstream sensor’s switching speed and the catalyst’s ability to buffer oxygen, both of which are critical to
maintaining closed-loop combustion efficiency and emissions control as seen in Figure 20 [390].

> **Figure 20 — Oxygen sensor voltage response Veteran Fleet Dynamics-Data Analytics [390]**
> Type: line chart
> Axes: Time (seconds) vs O2 Sensor Voltage (V)
> Series: Healthy Upstream (Pre-Cat), LAZY Upstream (Contaminated), DEAD Upstream (Stuck 0.45V), Healthy Downstream (Post-Cat), Failed Catalyst (Downstream Mimics)
> Values: 
> Healthy Upstream (Pre-Cat): starts at approx 0.45V at 0s, drops to approx 0.1V at 1s, peaks at approx 0.75V at 2s, drops to approx 0.2V at 4s, rises to approx 0.75V at 6s, stays high to 10s, drops sharply to approx 0.1V at 11s, stays low to 14s, rises to approx 0.75V at 17s, stays high to 20s
> LAZY Upstream (Contaminated): shallower peaks and delays across the 20s window
> DEAD Upstream (Stuck 0.45V): flat line at approx 0.45V
> Healthy Downstream (Post-Cat): dampened voltage swings around 0.5V - 0.7V
> Failed Catalyst (Downstream Mimics): follows upstream curve closely
> Notes: Timeline bars at bottom: 0-5s: BASELINE (Normal closed-loop), 5-10s: RICH (Propane injection ON), 10-15s: LEAN (Propane valve CLOSED), 15-20s: SNAP (Throttle snap test)

## 8.2 RNG Challenges

RNG also carries critical operational constraints. The U.S. Department of Energy states that CNG cylinders have a
finite service life of 15 to 20 years, depending on construction, and must be retired at the end of life, with no
requalification option [92]. NFPA 52 mandates visual inspections every 36 months or 36,000 miles. Mihelic said,

<!-- page: 42 -->

“Carbon fiber tanks have cycle life limits, like all pressure vessels. A challenge is making maintenance people
observe and ensure tank lifetimes and replacement occurs as required to avoid accidents [117].”

Figure 21 is an example of an RNG tank expiration sticker. This expiration date introduces a fixed-capital
replacement liability that must be budgeted for alongside truck lifecycle planning.

> **Figure 21 — Fuel tank expiration sticker [92]**
> Type: photograph
> Axes: none
> Series: none
> Values: none
> Notes: WIRE TOUGH, CNG ONLY, US DOT Type-2 & NGV-2-2007, EXP. 6/2033, Serial No. WE-42154-029, Manufactured: 6/2013, DO NOT USE AFTER: 6/2033, Service Pressure: 24800 KPa (3600 PSI) at 21 C (70 F), Vol: 154 L, Dia. 15.9"/404mm, Length- 59"/1499mm, Thd. 3/4-14 NGT, Wiretough Cylinders LLC, Bristol, Virginia 24202

Another limitation is fuel supply scaling. The US EPA estimated that in the 2035-2050 timeframe, RNG
production might be as high as 40 billion gasoline gallon equivalents, but in 2024 the Coalition for Renewable
Natural Gas estimated production at ~900 million gallons [391] [392]. For fleet managers, RNG should be viewed
as a targeted solution for return-to-base duty cycles and compliance corridors, not a universal diesel
replacement.

Economics remain mixed. PITT Ohio’s Darakos said, “We had a fleet of 34 CNG units. We ran into some
challenges. The maintenance cost was three to four times that of conventional diesel. We were rebuilding
engines at 300,000 and 400,000 miles. The diesel maintenance intervals are 50,000 miles. The natural gas at
20,000 miles, you are doing spark plugs [119].” DOE/NREL cost models confirm this trend. While RNG fuel itself
can reduce per-mile cost by 15% to 25% compared to diesel, maintenance costs rise by 20% to 40%, creating a
trade-off that must be carefully managed [153]. A comparative analysis of operation and maintenance costs
across different powertrain platforms is provided in Figure 22.
