## 8.3 RNG Safety

RNG fleet safety revolves around high-pressure fuel integrity and methane-hazard management. RNG and CNG
are stored at up to 3,600 psi; improper handling can cause catastrophic rupture. NFPA 52 establishes inspection
intervals; OSHA 29 CFR 1910.110 governs compressed gas storage; and CSA B108-18 sets Canadian requirements
for natural gas vehicle maintenance facilities. The DOE AFDC warns, “Missed inspections or delayed tank
replacement can result in catastrophic failure [96].”

Methane’s characteristics add complexity. It is odorless, lighter than air, and flammable in air at 5% to 15% by
volume, making leaks difficult to detect without sensors. Yadav’s SAE study demonstrated that integrated CAN-based leak detection can identify releases in under one second, significantly reducing hazard exposure [393].

Industry leaders highlight the human side of safety. Packard noted, “With natural gas and hydrogen, you need to
teach new safety protocols. Diesel has been around forever but with these gases, you’ve got to worry about
emergency response personnel [118].” For executives, this requires investment in technician and first-responder
training, in addition to compliance with technical codes. For technicians, this means training surrounding high-pressure protocols, methane hazard zones, and rapid shutdown procedures.

## 8.4 RNG Maintenance

RNG engines have a distinct maintenance schedule compared to diesel engines, affecting uptime, cost per mile,
and technician workload. Spark plugs in heavy-duty compressed natural gas (CNG/RNG) engines typically require
replacement every 750 to 1,500 engine hours, which translates to approximately 25,000 to 45,000 miles
depending on the engine model and duty cycle. In contrast, diesel glow plugs can last between 80,000 and
100,000 miles. For example, the Cummins B6.7N requires its spark plugs to be changed every 750 hours, around
25,000 miles for high-duty applications, whereas the L9N can extend the interval to 1,500 hours, about 45,000
miles [154].

The per-unit cost of spark plugs is higher than that of diesel glow plugs, leading to an increase in maintenance
costs per mile by 15% to 20% when factoring in labor. Jason Bauer, technical support manager at Cummins,
explains, “Every fleet that has 'premature' spark plug replacement is caused by having the wrong average vehicle
speed when calculating the interval or is just missing the interval. Our preventive maintenance intervals are

<!-- page: 44 -->

designed such that the total usable life of the spark plug is utilized, yet the spark plug is changed prior to misfire
occurring [155].”

Additionally, NACFE’s Otto notes that CNG powertrains require oil changes every 20,000 to 30,000 miles,
compared to the 50,000-mile intervals typical for diesel fleets [115]. This need arises because of increased blow-by contamination and the requirement for low-ash oil to protect natural gas engines [394]. Technicians caution
that using unapproved oils may cause valve recession and fouling, particularly in fleets with longer service
intervals. Executives should weigh potential fuel savings against these additional maintenance costs when
evaluating overall ownership expenses.

High-pressure fuel systems, regulators, lines, and valves operate at up to 3,600 psi, creating safety-critical
service and danger points. The TTP-CNG Refuse Recommended Practices stress that regulators rely on coolant
circulation for temperature control; if flow is blocked, regulators can freeze, causing restricted flow or hazardous
cryogenic conditions [156]. Technicians in the field have adapted by adding coolant temperature checks at every
PM interval to ensure stable regulator performance. Missed inspections or deferred service can result in sudden
regulator failure, engine cut-outs, or technician injury during line depressurization. From an executive
perspective, these maintenance practices are not optional add-ons but core compliance measures under NFPA
52 and OSHA 1910.110.

The transition between diesel-era maintenance and compressed natural gas operations requires a fundamental
shift in workforce certification. Unlike diesel technicians, who may gain competency through incremental
experience, CNG fleets demand that every employee — from drivers to emergency responders — receive role-specific training before interacting with high-pressure fuel systems. The Transport Project's Recommended
Practices for CNG-Powered Refuse Trucks establishes a tiered certification framework that maps training
requirements to operational responsibilities. Figure 23 summarizes these critical training tiers.

<!-- page: 45 -->

> **Figure 23 — Recommended CNG training matrix identifying certification [156]**
> Type: table / matrix
> Axes: none
> Series: Vehicle Drivers and Fuelers, Routine Vehicle Maintenance Technicians, CNG Fuel Systems Technicians, CNG Fuel System Repair Technicians, CNG Fuel System Inspectors, Technicians that Service All Aspects Except CNG Fuel Systems, Dealership Maintenance Teams, CNG Fueling Station Support Teams, Designated Emergency Contact for the Fleet, Firefighters/First Responders/Fire Marshalls/Code Officials
> Values: checkmarks indicated across training courses (NGV Driver and Fueler Training, Fundamental NGV Technician Safety Training, CNG Fuel System Inspector Training, CNG Fuel System Inspector Certification, HD Truck NVG Maintenance and Diagnostics Training, HD NGV Fuel System Repair and Diagnostic Training, Defueling, Decommissioning, or Disposal of CNG Fuel Tanks, CNG Fueling Station Operation & Maintenance Training, Firefighter/First-responder Training, Fire Marshal/Code Official Training)
> Notes: Recommended CNG training matrix identifying certification [156]

With personnel properly trained, technicians must understand the physical architecture of the CNG fuel system.
Unlike diesel tanks, CNG storage involves high-pressure vessels equipped with specific safety mechanisms. The
system relies on a series of rigid cylinders, typically mounted behind the cab or on the frame rails, integrated
with manual isolation valves and thermally activated pressure relief devices (PRDs) that vent gas during a fire to
prevent catastrophic rupture. The core components of this high-pressure storage network are detailed in Figure
24.

<!-- page: 46 -->

> **Figure 24 — Vehicular CNG fuel systems [156]**
> Type: schematic
> Axes: none
> Series: Low-Pressure Side, High-Pressure Side
> Values: none
> Notes: Diagram showing Vent Cap, Vent Line, Defueling Receptacle, Defueling Valve, Fuel Receptacle, Secondary Check Valve, Cylinder Valve, Fuel System Isolation Solenoid Valve, Fuel System Isolation Manual Valve, Remote PRO, Transducer, Mechanical Pressure Gauge, Pressure Regulator, High-Pressure Coalescing Filter, Fuel Shutoff Solenoid Valve

Beyond the storage vessels, the fuel delivery architecture reduces pressure from storage levels (up to 3,600 psi)
to engine operating parameters. This is achieved through a complex network of high-pressure regulators,
coalescing filters, and solenoid valves that control the flow during both fueling and operation. Understanding
the interplay among the fill circuit, the defueling path, and the pressure-regulation stages is essential for safe
diagnostics. Figure 25 provides a schematic overview of these critical fluid control circuits.

<!-- page: 47 -->

> **Figure 25 — CNG fuel system [157]**
> Type: schematic
> Axes: none

RNG fleets cannot rely on diesel-era knowledge alone. As PITT Ohio’s Darakos said, “We have three technicians
that were certified to do the tank inspections here. The technicians are a challenging piece. We have partnered
with high school vo-tech programs and started co-op programs. We push online training, hands-on training, and
OEM classes [119].” Packard echoed the workforce challenge, “With natural gas and hydrogen, you need to
teach new safety protocols. Diesel has been around forever. However, with these gasses; you have got to worry
about emergency response [118].” This means budgeting for technician certification (CSA B108 in Canada,
NGV2/NFPA 52 in the US), co-op recruitment, and refresher training every two to three years as technology and
codes evolve. For technicians, it means gaining confidence in high-pressure procedures, methane hazard
detection, and tank integrity checks, skills that go well beyond diesel PMs.

## 9 BEVs

Battery-electric vehicles sit at the center of one of the most consequential transitions in modern freight
transportation. For decades, the North American trucking industry has relied on ICE vehicle as its workhorse,
supported by a vast ecosystem of fueling, maintenance, and service practices. BEVs disrupt this paradigm by
replacing the diesel engine and fuel system with an integrated high-voltage (HV) propulsion platform that
includes traction batteries, inverters, drive motors or e-axles, orange-jacketed high voltage (HV) cabling, and a
manual service disconnect (MSD). This reconfiguration shifts the center of fleet maintenance away from oil, fuel,
and aftertreatment systems toward diagnostics, software calibration, and HV electrical safety protocols [158].

## 9.1 BEV Architecture

When it comes to BEVs, Dr. Mark Quarto, of Quarto Technical Services, said, "First, any workflow efficiency or
effectiveness is going to be mediocre at best without some understanding of the underlying technologies — and

<!-- page: 48 -->

this is really the key point. Education and training are at the forefront: education tells us the why, and training
tells us how. You must have both. From a safety-protocol perspective, knowing when to use approach
boundaries, PPE, and lockout/tagout needs to be fully understood — and these procedures can be radically
different between OEMs [159].”

During motor operation, an inverter inverts direct current (DC) from a hybrid or electric vehicle's battery pack
into alternating current (AC). The inverter uses high-voltage, high-current MOSFETs (metal-oxide-semiconductor
field-effect transistors) to switch current back and forth, producing the alternating current that powers the
vehicle's AC motor-generator(s). The inverter modulates the alternating current to control motor operation by:
- Increasing or decreasing AC frequency, to control motor speed,
- Increasing or decreasing AC amplitude to control motor torque (Advanced Electric Drive Vehicle
Education Program, NAFTC, 2013, Section 9.1.1 [395]).

The BEV eliminates the engine and transmission in favor of a high-voltage DC bus that powers an inverter-driven
traction motor. This simplified architecture reduces moving parts but introduces complex thermal and electrical
management subsystems. Figure 26 illustrates the fundamental DC powertrain layout that underpins modern
electric commercial vehicles.

> **Figure 26 — Simplified DC powertrain architecture for BEVs [396]**
> Type: schematic
> Axes: none
> Series: none
> Values: none
> Notes: Diagram showing Battery, Boost Converter, L1, DC-Link, Inverter, Motor

In this electric drive system, energy management is bidirectional and governed by sophisticated power
electronics. Energy flows from the high-voltage battery pack through the inverter to drive the motor, while
regenerative braking reverses this flow to recharge the battery. Simultaneously, a DC-DC converter steps down
the high voltage to supply the vehicle’s 12V accessories, ensuring that lights, ECUs, and cabin controls remain
operational. The complete power-flow ecosystem, linking high-voltage storage to tractive effort and auxiliary
loads, is shown in Figure 27.

<!-- page: 49 -->

> **Figure 27 — Simplified power flow electric drive system [395]**
> Type: schematic
> Axes: none
> Series: none
> Values: none
> Notes: Diagram showing Battery pack, Inverter, Motor, DC-DC converter, 12V battery, with DC and AC directional arrows.

The inverter's core function is to convert the battery's direct current into the three-phase alternating current
required by traction motors to deliver smooth, controllable torque across all speeds. This conversion process
employs pulse-width modulation (PWM), a technique that rapidly switches high-voltage transistors on and off to
create waveforms that emulate sinusoidal AC output. Modern electric truck inverters operate at 10,000 Hz to
20,000 Hz, frequencies beyond human perception and are precisely controlled by microcontrollers to adjust
both motor torque and speed.

By modulating the amplitude and pulse duration of these switching cycles, the inverter achieves smooth power
application, eliminating the whining noise characteristic of less sophisticated BEV control systems. Figure 28
demonstrates how transistor PWM creates higher amplitude sine wave regions through wider PWM pulses,
corresponding to narrower off-periods that generate the time-averaged voltage producing speed and torque
through current flow [160].

To better understand how the inverter controls speed and torque, imagine the sine wave representing the
inverter giving power in response to a throttle signal, below. The speed of the revolutions of the motor would
correspond to the closeness of the sine wave peaks, which corresponds to the speed of transistor switching in
the inverter array, and also corresponds to the timing in between PWM signals. The torque corresponds to the
width of the actual PWM signal and the height of the sine wave peak.

<!-- page: 50 -->

> **Figure 28 — Sample points sine wave with corresponding modulated PWM [161]**
> Type: line chart / schematic
> Axes: duty % vs time / phase angle
> Series: Sine wave, PWM pulses
> Values: duty % values: 75%, 93.3%, 100%, 93.3%, 75%, 50%, 25%, 6.6%, 0.0%, 6.6%, 25%, 50%
> Notes: Figure 28: Sample points sine wave with corresponding modulated PWM [161]

There is one caveat: different battery chemistries will perform differently. Therefore, according to Quarto,
"Knowing how to interpret the battery-pack data or the parameter IDs is important, and this is based on the
chemistry. Several chemistries are in use, but technicians must know which chemistry they are working with, for
example, NMC (Lithium Nickel manganese Cobalt Oxide (LiNiMnCoO2) or LFP (Lithium Iron Phosphate, LiFePO4),
because LFP and NMC will display differently. When you get into battery packs, you need a deep level of
understanding of what you are working with [159].”

The inverter uses transistors arranged in three pairs to generate the AC power for one motor phase. These
switches switch on and off between 10,000 and 20,000 times per second, and the motor coils smooth these
rapid pulses into a rotating magnetic field that spins the wheels [162]. When diagnosing inverter problems,
technicians need oscilloscopes that capture all three phases simultaneously to spot issues such as dead switches,
phase-to-phase timing mismatches, or the catastrophic device failure known as “shoot-through,” in which both
switches conduct simultaneously m blowing the inverter [161]. Another way to check for a dead leg is to isolate
the motor and perform resistance testing. More information on this can be found in Section 9.5.

The transistors enabling PWM switching are metal-oxide-semiconductor field-effect transistors (MOSFETs),
arranged in three pairs within the inverter — one pair for each AC phase. These semiconductor switches
represent the highest-stress components in the inverter, operating at extreme switching speeds with minimal
timing tolerances. The critical safety margin between top and bottom switch firing must be maintained between
0.0005 and 0.002 milliseconds to prevent shoot-through. This timing gap narrows under thermal load, meaning
inverters that test flawlessly on a cold bench can fail during high-power highway operation. Diagnostic
technicians must understand that inverter performance is temperature-dependent and cannot be validated by
cold testing alone. Figure 29 presents the cross-sectional structure and bias configuration of an N-channel

<!-- page: 51 -->

enhancement MOSFET, revealing the semiconductor layers and electrical pathways that enable precision
switching [161].

> **Figure 29 — The metal-oxide-semiconductor field-effect transistor [162]**
> Type: schematic
> Axes: none
> Series: none
> Values: none
> Notes: Diagram showing Source (S), Gate (G), Drain (D), VDS, VGS, ID, SiO2, Metal Electrode, Metal Oxide Insulator, N-type Channel, P-type Substrate, Depletion Layer, Substrate.

Fleet managers comparing electric powertrains to diesel systems require standardized power metrics to
evaluate parity. A common question emerges: how does a 400-hp diesel engine compare to an electric motor in
kilowatt output? This conversion requires understanding the relationship between mechanical horsepower (a
legacy unit of steam engine performance) and the watt, the direct unit of electrical power. The conversion is
straightforward: 1 kW equals 1.34102 hp, so a 300 kW electric motor produces approximately 402 hp. This
metric is critical to fleet decision-making, enabling technicians and engineers to translate historical diesel
specifications into electric equivalents. Figure 30 provides a quick-reference table that converts kilowatts to
horsepower across the operating range of Class 8 electric trucks, enabling rapid specification comparisons.

<!-- page: 52 -->

## Kilowatts to Horsepower

| 50 | 67 |
| 75 | 101 |
| 100 | 134 |
| 150 | 201 |
| 200 | 268 |
| 250 | 335 |
| 300 | 402 |
| 350 | 469 |
| 400 | 536 |
| 450 | 603 |
| 500 | 671 |

1 kW = 1.34102 HP • 1 HP = 0.7457 kW
Conversion formulas: KW to HP: HP = kW × 1.34102 • HP to kW: kW = HP × 0.7457

## 9.2 BEV Advantages

One of the most consistently documented advantages of BEVs is their lower maintenance costs compared to
internal-combustion vehicles. Comparative fleet modeling indicates that BEV maintenance costs range are 30%
to 50% less than ICE vehicles. The mechanics of this advantage are straightforward; fleets eliminate recurring
items such as engine oil changes, DEF replenishment, friction brakes, and fuel filter replacements. These
consumables, which have long defined service intervals, do not exist in BEV platforms.

According to an interview with NACFE’s Otto, in practical terms, most of the “dirty work” of lubricants and
exhaust aftertreatment gives way to advanced electrical troubleshooting, over-the-air software updates, and
sensor diagnostics [115]. Tasks such as verifying motor winding integrity with a digital multimeter (DMM),
replacing failed current sensors, or applying OEM service software for inverter calibration now replace draining
fluids or swapping filters.

<!-- page: 53 -->

Volvo Group CTO Lars Stenqvist reinforced this shift at ACT Expo 2025, stating, "From the Volvo perspective,
efficiency is no longer a 'nice to have' feature — it is the product. When you electrify a truck, every kilowatt-hour becomes sacred energy. Our aerodynamic work and software optimization are now done together,
because you cannot separate the physical truck from its digital control system [163].”

The reduction in brake wear is another significant advantage of BEVs, made possible by regenerative braking. In
traditional diesel fleets, brake pads and drums are subject to constant mechanical stress, often requiring
replacement at regular intervals. BEVs, by contrast, rely on their electric motors to recapture kinetic energy
during deceleration, converting it back into stored electrical energy. This process not only extends the driving
range but also minimizes mechanical brake use, resulting in measurable reductions in parts costs and technician
labor. A NACFE field study documented substantial decreases in brake service demand at the fleet level,
confirming the economic and uptime advantages [158].

Wind tunnel simulations demonstrate that coordinated tractor–trailer aerodynamic packages can achieve
between 4% to 27% energy-rate savings and extend BEV range by 5% to 37%, depending on duty cycle and
speed profile [398].

This finding supports the business case for BEVs in high-mileage applications, where fleets use return-to-base
(RTB) or return-to-origin (RTO) charging, such as regional delivery fleets. Salim Youssefzadeh, CEO of WattEV,
reinforced this optimized setup during an ACT Expo 2025 shipper panel. “What we have been able to do is
create tours that optimize electric vehicles. In many cases, we are getting close to 500 miles per day per truck.
That really increases utilization and makes TCO make sense [164].”

## 9.3 BEV Challenges

Fleets must also confront BEVs operational and economic disadvantages. These limitations are not theoretical;
they manifest daily in TCO calculations, shop planning, and long-term investment risks.

The first and most critical disadvantage is the total cost of transport (TCT). Although BEVs reduce maintenance
costs compared to diesel, the holistic economics of freight movement remain challenging. When equipment
costs, labor inputs, infrastructure requirements, payload impacts, and dwell times are factored into the
equation, BEVs present a higher per-mile cost today. A Ryder analysis published in 2024 concluded that “There
are specific applications where EV adoption makes sense today, but the use cases are still limited. Yet we’re
facing regulations aimed at accelerating broader EV adoption when the technology and infrastructure are still
developing,” according to Karen Jones, EVP and head of new product development for Ryder. “Until the gap in
TCT for heavier duty vehicles is narrowed or closed, we cannot expect many companies to make the transition;
and, if required to convert in today’s market, we face more supply chain disruptions, and transportation cost
increases [66].”

Infrastructure development has emerged as the pacing factor for BEV adoption. Utility lead times for service
upgrades, transformer installation, and high-voltage interconnects can span multiple years, particularly in dense
freight corridors. A recent study by the Clean Freight Coalition outlines the great need for infrastructure
development. Based on this methodology, utilities will need to invest around $370 billion nationally on
distribution grid upgrades over the next 15 years and new construction to meet local charging demand from
Class 3 to 8 trucks. In comparison, utilities invested roughly $450 billion in distribution across the US over the
last 15 years [165].

However, past utility infrastructure investment is not predictive of the future, as rapid expansion is underway in
electric power generation and distribution to serve new users, such as data centers, and as electrification trends

<!-- page: 54 -->

accelerate. Such a significant investment need indicates infrastructure aging and immaturity. More on
infrastructure can be found in Section 12.

Battery-electric platforms require large battery packs to deliver an acceptable range. These packs add several
thousand pounds to curb weight, eroding payload capacity in weight-limited applications. While federal
allowances provide a 2,000-lbs. exemption for zero-emission Class 8 tractors under the FAST Act, this margin is
insufficient for some freight classes [34]. For shippers and carriers in bulk commodities or on very long-haul
routes, where perhaps 10% of the freight carried has payload capacity that explicitly defines revenue per load,
this trade-off is a significant barrier.

Another disadvantage stems from technology risk and stranded assets. The rapid pace of OEM innovation,
coupled with the volatility of start-ups in the zero-emission space, creates uncertainty about long-term support.
Jim Gillis, president of IMC, put this bluntly at ACT Expo 2025, “I’ve bet on two OEMs in the zero-emissions space
that went out of business. I’m stuck with equipment that doesn’t work. Shippers aren’t coming to me saying
they will help cover that cost [166].” This statement underscores the risk fleets face when investing in new
platforms without proven service networks, established parts pipelines, or residual value guarantees.

The combined effect of these disadvantages is a landscape in which BEV deployment requires careful duty-cycle
analysis. Fleets must model not only range and charging but also payload tradeoffs, customer tolerance for
longer dwell times, and the risk profile of OEM selection. For high-mileage regional fleets with consistent lanes
and strong shipper partnerships, BEVs can make sense today. For operators in bulk, long-haul, or variable-route
markets, the disadvantages may still outweigh the advantages until costs normalize and infrastructure matures.

## 9.4 BEV Safety

Safety considerations for BEVs represent a foundational shift in fleet service practices. Unlike internal
combustion platforms, where hazards primarily involve fuels, exhaust, and mechanical energy, BEVs’ risk profile
involves HV circuits, battery chemistry, and unique fire behaviors. For this reason, BEV safety must be addressed
not as an “add-on” but as a core competency within any shop or depot that services electrified fleets.

OSHA requires lockout/tagout (LOTO) procedures for hazardous energy under 29 CFR 1910.147 [59]. In practical
terms, this means all BEV HV circuits must be treated as live until they have been verified as de-energized. The
National Institute for Automotive Service Excellence (ASE) extends this principle by defining an “electrically safe
work condition” as one in which HV conductors are disconnected, isolated, secured via LOTO, and tested for the
absence of voltage. Until that condition is established, technicians must assume lethal voltage is present [2] [3].
This is reinforced by Otto, who said, “With battery electric trucks, voltages — heavy-duty, 800, 900 volts — can
kill people. You really need a certification of some kind. Anything above 50 volts is killing material if there is
enough energy behind it [115].” Otto’s remarks highlight the distinction between nominal voltage values and
available fault energy; BEV packs typically operate at 600 to 900 V DC, with fault currents exceeding 1,000 amps,
making arc flash and electrocution a constant risk.

Instrument safety is as critical as LOTO. Technicians must use only CAT III/1000 V or CAT IV/600 V-rated
multimeters and leads, ensuring the test equipment is rated at or above the maximum anticipated voltage [35].
Misuse of instruments can result in catastrophic meter failure and arc flash. As Otto emphasized, “You need CAT
III 1000-volt meters, insulation testers, thermal cameras — this is not optional. If you bring in a BEV without the
right diagnostic tools, you are blind and putting your technicians at risk [115].”

Instrument safety presents a subtle but critical vulnerability in BEV service environments. Technicians often
believe that higher voltage ratings on multimeters and test leads provide better protection, but the actual

<!-- page: 55 -->

relationship between category rating and overvoltage protection is counterintuitive. A CAT IV 600 V meter
experiences higher transient stress than a CAT I 1000 V meter because the CAT rating reflects assumptions about
source impedance, not absolute voltage limits. CAT I circuits assume a 12-ohm source impedance — typical of
highly protected laboratory environments — allowing controlled surge currents during fault conditions.

CAT IV circuits assume only a 2-ohm source impedance — typical of utility service entrance points — thereby
enabling dramatically higher transient surge currents, sometimes exceeding 3,000 A. For a BEV technician
working on a 600 V battery pack, using a CAT IV 600 V meter instead of a CAT III 1000 V meter concentrates
more energy in the meter during a fault condition. Figure 31 illustrates the relationship between CAT rating,
source impedance, and resulting transient overvoltage stress, clarifying why technicians must select instruments
based on both voltage and category rating.

## Voltage Testing Categories and Transient Surge Comparison

| Measurement Category | Working Voltage (DC or AC-RMS to Ground) | Peak Impulse Transient (20 Repetitons) | Test Source ($= V/A$) |
|---|---|---|---|
| CAT I | 600 V | 2500 V | 30 Ohm Source |
| CAT I | 1000 V | 4000 V | 30 Ohm Source |
| CAT II | 600 V | 4000 V | 12 Ohm Source |
| CAT II | 1000 V | 6000 V | 12 Ohm Source |
| CAT III | 600 V | 6000 V | 2 Ohm Source |
| CAT III | 1000 V | 8000 V | 2 Ohm Source |
| CAT IV | 600 V | 8000 V | 2 Ohm Source |

> **Figure 31 — CAT III vs. CAT IV voltage testing categories and transient surge comparison [397]**
> Type: table
> Axes: none
> Series: Measurement Category, Working Voltage, Peak Impulse Transient, Test Source
> Values: See table above.
> Notes: Figure 31: CAT III vs. CAT IV voltage testing categories and transient surge comparison [397]

PPE requirements scale accordingly. NFPA 70E specifies arc-rated gloves (Class 0/00), face shields, and electrical
hazard (EH) boots as baseline PPE for HV service, establishing a mandatory, non-negotiable regulatory
framework. Visual examples reinforce these standards, and PPE constitutes the final, non-negotiable layer of the
shop toolkit. Honeywell offers PPE ensembles specifically designed for electric-vehicle service, including
infrared-protected face shields and voltage-rated gloves tested at 1,000 volts [399].

Figure 32 shows the proper Class 0 electrical glove with leather protector ensemble, including the construction
and layering that enable safe high-voltage work. Electrical hazard footwear rounds out the PPE ensemble for
high-voltage work environments. MEH-rated boots — meeting ASTM F2413 and F2892 standards for electrical
hazard protection — provide secondary underfoot protection against energized conductors or conductive floors
in maintenance bays.

footwear specification that protects technicians during entire shift lengths in high-voltage environments.
Thermal protection for the face and eyes ranks alongside hands and feet in HV safety protocols.

Figure 33 and Figure 34 show boots, EH-rated Timberland PRO Endurance 8" Composite Toe Work Boot. Electric
Hazard Protection. These provide secondary underfoot protection against live electrical circuits, electrically
energized conductors, parts, or apparatus for less than 600 volts. They meet the ASTM standards for F2413 and
F2892 requirements when tested to F2412.

Arc-flash face shields protect against three simultaneous hazards — ultraviolet radiation (from arc plasma),
infrared thermal energy (from intense heat), and direct mechanical impact from arc blow (rapid expansion of
air). Figure 35, an arc-flash face shield designed for EV service technicians, illustrates a professional arc-flash
face shield specifically for BEV service, featuring dual-lens construction and a frame design that enables multi-hour wear without eye fatigue [169]. Shops also must maintain rescue equipment. Insulated "hot sticks" or
rescue shepherds' hooks allow a technician to pull a colleague away from an energized circuit during
emergencies. Industry supplier Hubbell notes that these epoxy-fiberglass rods are designed to withstand contact
with HV busbars while maintaining dielectric isolation.

<!-- page: 58 -->

> **Figure 35 — Arc-flash face shield designed for BEV service technicians [169]**
> Type: photograph
> Axes: none
> Series: none
> Values: none
> Notes: White hard hat with blue arc-flash face shield and headlamp.

> **Figure 36 — Insulated high-voltage rescue hook for technician safety [168] [400]**
> Type: photograph
> Axes: none
> Series: none
> Values: none
> Notes: Red and black insulated rescue hook.

<!-- page: 59 -->

Safety procedures extend beyond tools and PPE into repeatable service checklists. A standard BEV de-energization protocol should include:
- Securing the vehicle and applying wheel chocks.
- Powering down using the OEM shutdown sequence.
- Donning PPE (Class 0 gloves, arc-rated face shield, EH boots).
- Removing the manual service disconnect (MSD).
- Applying LOTO devices at designated points.
- Observing capacitor dwell time to allow pre-charge circuits to discharge.
- Performing “live–dead–live” verification with a CAT-rated meter.
- Conducting insulation resistance testing before re-energization [35].

Beyond HV isolation, the High-Voltage Interlock Loop (HVIL) is a mechanical safety measure. The HVIL is a 5 to 12
V low-voltage circuit spanning connectors and enclosures. If continuity is lost, the battery management system
(BMS) commands the contactors to open and initiates bus discharge, typically within 1s [402]. Tesla technical
notes describe HVIL operation as maintaining ~20 mA current at ~4.8 V across loop resistance [170]. However,
ASE warns explicitly, “Do NOT back-probe high-voltage connectors.” Intrusive probing risks water ingress and
compromises insulation. Instead, OEM-approved breakout leads, or scan tools, must be used for verification [3]
[4].

Fire safety poses another unique hazard for BEVs. The International Association of Fire Chiefs (IAFC) Energy
Storage Systems Bulletin advises that lithium-ion pack fires may require sustained water application directly to
the battery enclosure [171].

Volvo, Freightliner, Tesla, and other OEMs provide Emergency Response Guides (ERGs) — laminated placards
affixed to vehicle cab doors — that specify exact procedures for the specific vehicle model. These guides identify
battery access points, cooling line locations, mechanical disconnect points, and water application procedures.
First responders often lack BEV experience; an ERG placard may be the only technical guidance available during
incident response. Fleets must maintain ERG copies both in binders accessible to technicians and as loose cards
in service vehicle cabs for roadside emergencies. Digital ERG storage through mobile apps (Tesla app, Volvo
Connected Services, Freightliner SmartLINQ) provides a backup reference when physical placards are damaged
or obscured. Figure 37 shows an example of a Volvo Emergency Response Guide placard, demonstrating the
critical information layout that enables rapid, informed response during thermal events.

<!-- page: 60 -->

> **Figure 37 — Volvo Trucks emergency response guide placard [111]**
> Type: schematic / illustration
> Axes: none
> Series: none
> Values: none
> Notes: Volvo Trucks emergency response guide placard [111]

Shops must maintain ERGs in both in-bay binders and in-cab packets to ensure accessibility for technicians and
first responders. As Mihelic said, “At this point, no one really knows what a pack replacement cycle is going to
look like for fleets. OEMs have projections, but they depend on duty cycle, charging speed, and environmental
conditions. That’s a big gap we need real-world data to close [117].” While Mihelic’s point addressed
degradation, it applies equally to fire safety; real-world conditions will determine best practices.

In summary, BEV safety protocols demand a culture shift in shops. Every technician needs to become part of a
high-voltage workforce, PPE compliance is mandatory, and emergency planning extends beyond diesel norms.
Without this foundation, BEV deployments risk both technician safety and fleet liability.

## 9.5 BEV Maintenance

Maintenance for BEVs always should be performed according to OEM instructions by specifically trained
technicians. This required technicians to shift from wrench wrenches to laptops. As the industry transitions to
battery-electric and software-defined vehicles, technician roles are evolving from traditional mechanical skills
toward digital diagnostics and software management capabilities. The future technician must possess both
electrical expertise and data analysis competencies to maintain and troubleshoot these complex systems [163].
Maintenance practices for BEVs diverge sharply from diesel fleets, reflecting both the absence of combustion
systems and the introduction of complex HV and software-driven components. While BEVs eliminate entire
classes of PM tasks such as oil changes, aftertreatment service, and DEF refills, they impose new diagnostic
requirements that demand advanced instrumentation and trained technicians.

<!-- page: 61 -->

BEV preventive maintenance centers less on fluids and more on electronics, thermal management, and HV
system integrity. Core PM tasks include inspections of:
- Inverters and DC-to-DC converters regulate energy flows.
- HV orange cabling and connectors, requiring checks for insulation wear and chafing.
- Low-voltage support batteries, which power safety-critical systems.
- Thermal loops that manage pack, e-axle, and inverter cooling.

Unlike diesel, where PM tasks rely on fluid analysis and filter swaps, BEV service relies heavily on inspecting
heavy-duty components. See Figure 38, Figure 39, and Figure 40 for an eCascadia truck. Otto summarized the
shift, “You are no longer dealing with oil changes or aftertreatment, but you are dealing with interlocks, thermal
systems, and high-voltage service [115].”

> **Figure 38 — HV cable routing [28]**
> Type: schematic
> Axes: none
> Series: 1. Frontbox, 2. Charging Fuse, 3. High Voltage Batteries, 4. Inverters, 5. eAxles, 6. Charge Ports, 7. DC Boxes
> Values: See labels 1 through 7 above.
> Notes: Figure 38: HV cable routing [28]

Freightliner's eCascadia establishes four primary maintenance milestones: M1 at - 10,000 miles (routine
inspections), M2 at 50,000 miles (electrical component assessment), M3 at 100,000 miles (coolant and filter
replacement), and M4 at 150,000 miles (drivetrain fluid service). Each tier escalates diagnostic depth, moving
from visual checks to instrument-verified electrical parameters to thermal imaging of power electronics. The
schedule balances operational realities — a 10,000-mile inspection frequency aligns with regional pickup points
for many fleets — with failure probability modeling. Figure 39 excerpts the eCascadia maintenance matrix,
showing task assignments across mileage intervals and identifying the transition from routine to specialized
service requirements.

<!-- page: 62 -->

> **Figure 39 — Freightliner eCascadia maintenance interval schedule [28]**
> Type: table
> Axes: none
> Series: Schedule I (Short-Haul Transport), Frequency, Miles, km
> Values: Initial Maintenance (IM): first, 10,000 mi, 16,000 km; Maintenance 1 (M1): every, 10,000 mi, 16,000 km; Maintenance 2 (M2): every, 50,000 mi, 80,000 km; Maintenance 3 (M3): every, 100,000 mi, 160,000 km; Maintenance 4 (M4): every, 150,000 mi, 240,000 km
> Notes: Figure 39: Freightliner eCascadia maintenance interval schedule [28]

Freightliner's eCascadia inspection checklist categorizes tasks by system: cooling system (radiator, pump, hose
integrity), electrical system (connector corrosion, cable chafing, terminal torque), brake system (hydraulic fluid
condition, ABS function), and safety systems (arc-flash PPE availability, LOTO device staging). Checklists also
identify pass/fail decision criteria, preventing technician interpretation from introducing variability. For instance,
the coolant inspection specifies "check coolant level with engine cold; mark minimum and maximum sight glass;
if below minimum after top-off, investigate for leaks before returning to service." This removes ambiguity and
creates a defensible service record. Figure 40 presents an excerpt from the eCascadia inspection-maintenance
checklist, showing how comprehensive task lists translate milestones into actionable work orders.

> **Figure 40 — Freightliner eCascadia detailed inspection-maintenance checklist [28]**
> Type: table
> Axes: none
> Series: Maint. No., Operation Description, Service Schedule I (IM, M1, M2, M3, M4)
> Values: 08-01 High Voltage Electrical Component Inspection (M2, M3, M4); 08-02 High Voltage Wiring Check (M2, M3, M4); 08-03 Cable Gland Protection Inspection (M2, M3, M4); 13-01 Air Compressor Inspection (IM, M1, M2, M3, M4); etc.
> Notes: Figure 40: Freightliner eCascadia detailed inspection-maintenance checklist [28]

Testing HVIL continuity requires precision. ASE guidance stresses “Do NOT back-probe high-voltage connectors,”
as this risks moisture ingress and long-term insulation degradation [2] [3]. Instead, technicians must use OEM-approved breakout leads or test adapters to interrupt and monitor the circuit safely.

Figure 41 illustrates the High-Voltage Interlock Loop (HVIL) schematic, highlighting the 5 to 12V monitoring
circuit and the contactor control logic essential for diagnosis.

<!-- page: 63 -->

> **Figure 41 — High-voltage interlock loop diagnostic schematic [170]**
> Type: schematic / table
> Axes: none
> Series: Connector, Pin, Expected Voltage (HVIL On), Resistance to GND (HVIL Off)
> Values: Detailed pinout voltages (4.8V, 3.6V, 2.4V, 1.2V) and resistances (240 ohm, 180 ohm, 1200 ohm, 600 ohm) shown in table.
> Notes: Figure 41: High-voltage interlock loop diagnostic schematic [170]

Because HVIL diagnostics involve live circuits, artistry is paramount. Technicians must apply live–dead–live
verification, use insulated tools, and document readings with screenshots to confirm safe completion. Otto
again framed the technician’s role: “If you bring in a BEV without the right diagnostic tools, you are blind and
putting your technicians at risk [115].”

Daily operator inspections also form an essential part of the BEV maintenance culture. FMCSA requires drivers
to perform daily vehicle inspection reports (DVIRs) under 49 CFR §396.11 (FMCSA 2017). For BEVs, these
inspections must include:
- Check HV orange cabling for chafing, insecure covers, or corrosion.
- Inspect coolant hoses and fittings for leaks.
- Confirm regenerative braking function.
- Check hydraulic brake fluid levels and parking brake engagement.

Monitoring for visible pack leakage or unusual odors indicating thermal risk. DOT Mobile Express Service
intervals remain mileage-based but focus more on electronics than combustion. At 10,000 miles (M1 interval),
the Freightliner eCascadia service schedule includes air compressor inspection, brake inspection, gas detection
system sensor testing, wheel nut checks, and tire inspection. See Figure 42 for an excerpt from the eCascadia
service schedule table [28]. At 50,000 miles (M2 interval), recommended tasks expand to include high-voltage

<!-- page: 64 -->

electrical component inspection, HV wiring check, cable gland protection inspection, air compressor oil and filter
change, and power steering fluid level inspection [28]. At 100,000 miles (M3 interval), complete BEV inspections
should include coolant replacement (or every 200,000 miles/two years). At 150,000 miles (M4 interval), the axle
lubricant and filter, and the power steering fluid and filter, must be inspected [28].

> **Figure 42 — Freightliner eCascadia maintenance interval schedule [28]**
> Type: table
> Axes: none
> Series: Maint. No., Operation Description, IM, M1, M2, M3, M4
> Values: Complete maintenance operation schedule continuation from page 62.
> Notes: Figure 42: Freightliner eCascadia maintenance interval schedule [28]

In comparison, Volvo specifies extended coolant intervals of 300,000 miles or 48 months and synthetic rear axle
oil replacement at 250,000 miles. This demonstrates how service cadences are evolving with electrification
experience, shifting from frequent fluid changes to annual comprehensive inspections. The Volvo VNR Electric
also requires an annual BEV inspection, including a comprehensive battery inspection and electric air
compressor service. Figure 43 outlines the Volvo VNR Electric maintenance summary, highlighting the shift
toward longer-duration service intervals [172].

> Notes: Figure 43: Volvo VNR Electric OEM maintenance interval summary [172]

The cultural shift is clear: whereas diesel service relied heavily on consumables and mechanical replacement,
BEV service requires diagnostic precision, data literacy, and discipline with HV protocols. Fleets must train
technicians not only on procedures but also on mindset, replacing the “mechanic touch” with a “technician’s
touch,” in which restraint and measurement take precedence over force.

## 9.6 Battery Health & Range

Battery utilization and degradation have proven to be less of a fleet liability than early skeptics suggested.
Research shows that high operational utilization — measured in miles per day or hours in use — does not
necessarily accelerate degradation if packs are kept within thermal windows and depth-of-discharge (DoD)
limits. Preger et al. showed that data-driven operation can ensure long-term pack health and preserve residual
asset value [402]. Otto captured this point in practical fleet terms, “Battery health is not about mileage alone —
it is about how you use it. If you run the pack outside its thermal window or deep cycle it too often, you’ll
shorten its life. Fleets need to train operators to stay inside the safe zone [115].”

Comprehensive Techno-Economic Analysis of Battery-Electric Trucks: Evaluating Battery Aging Impact For
Regional Delivery Missions 20025-01-8591 contains a techno-economic analysis of a regional delivery VECTO
Group 9 BEV truck, combining a detailed powertrain model with an empirical battery-aging model to evaluate
multiple battery sizes, payloads, and two battery end-of-life thresholds at 80% and 70% state of health (SOH)
[173]. The simulations show that capacity fade shortens battery life, increases the number of replacements, and
shifts more energy to public fast charging as the pack ages. The raises energy carrier costs because fast-charge
electricity (0.52 €/kWh) is substantially more expensive than depot slow-charging (0.30 €/kWh). The authors
also link residual value directly to remaining battery life, using an aging model to estimate SOH at vehicle end-of-life and thus the pack's resale value.

<!-- page: 66 -->

For a fixed 300 km daily regional delivery mission, the TCO-optimal battery size depends on the payload. In the
80% SOH case, the minimum TCO shifts from about 376 kWh at empty to about 564 kWh at full payload. In
contrast, larger packs (≈658 kWh and above) primarily increase purchase cost without meaningful energy-carrier
savings because they do not materially change the required fast-charging share. Changing the SOH thresholds,
setting the battery end-of-life at 70% instead of 80% reduces replacement frequency and lowers total TCO, even
though the model predicts a modest increase in energy-carrier cost because of slightly more frequent fast
charging.

Overall, the study concludes that fleet-optimal BEV truck specifications must co-optimize battery size, duty
cycle, payload profile, and an explicit aging model, and that policy comparisons across powertrains should use
consistent, standardized battery end-of-life (EoL), “particularly payload and battery degradation over time, to
compare configurations [173].”

The most important part of our battery design philosophy is managing temperature gradients across modules. A
5.4°F (3°C) difference between cells can double the degradation rate over a thousand cycles. That is why we use
liquid-cooling plates, advanced BMS balancing, and predictive algorithms to keep everything within a 2° C
window [163].

Figure 44 maps the typical operational ranges of varying electric truck classes, comparing standard platforms
against long-range variants like the Tesla Semi.

> **Figure 44 — Typical operational ranges of current technology BEV [165]**
> Type: table
> Axes: none
> Series: Class, Example vehicles, Mileage efficiency (kWh/mi), Battery capacity [kWh], OEM spec range [mi], Usable range [mi]
> Values: Class 3: Rivian, Ford eTransit, MB eSprinter, 0.7 kWh/mi, 100 kWh, 150 mi, 90 mi; Class 4: Workhorse W4CC, 0.7 kWh/mi, 100 kWh, 150 mi, 90 mi; Class 5: Freightliner Mt50e, Workhorse W56, 1.5 kWh/mi, 100 kWh, 150 mi, 90 mi; Class 6: Kenworth, Navistar eMV, Freightliner eM2, 1.3 kWh/mi, 218 kWh, 163 mi, 98 mi; Class 7: Kenworth, Navistar eMV, Freightliner eM2, 1.3 kWh/mi, 218 kWh, 163 mi, 98 mi; Class 8: Freightliner eCascadia, Volvo VNR, 2.0 kWh/mi, 440 kWh, 220 mi, 132 mi; Long-haul: No electric long-haul truck in series production today, 2.0 kWh/mi, 600 kWh, 300 mi, 180 mi.
> Notes: Figure 44: Typical operational ranges of current technology BEVs [165]

The health of a BEV’s battery system defines its reliability, uptime, and TCO. More than any other subsystem,
the traction battery determines whether the business case for fleet electrification holds. Unlike diesel engines,

<!-- page: 67 -->

where life cycles are well established across millions of operating hours, the battery health curve for heavy-duty
BEVs still is being written in real time.

Recent research has validated the accuracy of advanced diagnostic methods. A 2024 study by Wen et al
confirmed that average SOH error of 3% could be achieved using modeling approaches [404]. These techniques
enable technicians to perform bay-length assessments without extended downtime, providing fleets with
actionable insights into pack condition during routine service windows. By comparison, traditional open-circuit
voltage (OCV) methods required rest periods to stabilize cell voltages, limiting their utility in operational
environments.

Instead, monitoring SOC for most fleets relies on coulomb counting, integrating current over time to estimate
charge. As PowerTech Systems explains, “The most intuitive method is to follow the current by integrating it
during cell use [174].” Coulomb counting allows real-time monitoring without rest intervals, making it better
suited for heavy-duty cycles where vehicles cannot afford extended idle time.

SOH, by contrast, is more complex. It represents the battery's usable state relative to its rated new capacity.
OEM definitions often include multiple dimensions: full-charge capacity vs. rated capacity, internal resistance
growth, and cell voltage imbalance. The State of Health Estimation of Lithium-Ion Batteries: A Review of Health
Indicators, Estimation Method, Development Trends and Challenges formalized this approach, noting that
“internal resistance is a crucial SOH indicator. As SOH declines, internal resistance increases,” which in turn
reduces both energy capacity and maximum power [175]. See Figure 45.

SOH = fracRE - RrealRE - RN × 100%

> **Figure 45 — State of health equation [175]**
> Type: equation / schematic
> Axes: none
> Series: none
> Values: Formula: SOH = fracRE - RrealRE - RN × 100%
> Notes: Figure 45: State of health equation [175]

This expression enables fleets and service providers to trend the effects of power fade, ensuring drivability and
peak-power maintenance triggers are addressed before catastrophic degradation.

These resistance increases carry fleet-level implications. As internal resistance rises, packs run hotter under
load; thermal loops work harder, and available power declines. This cascades into operations: range shortens,
acceleration weakens, and reliability drops. Fleets that monitor SOH trends proactively can schedule pack
refurbishments before vehicles fall below operational thresholds, reducing unplanned downtime.

For example, charging practices significantly influence long-term battery health. OEM advisories commonly
recommend limiting daily charge limits to 85% to 90% SoC, reserving 100% charge for duty cycles that require
maximum range. Similarly, avoiding deep discharges below 20% of SoC reduces stress on the cells. Various
sources have demonstrated that operating at 20% to 80% DoD and maintaining battery temperatures between
15°C to 35°C can extend pack life beyond 3,000 cycles [404]. Otto reinforced this operational reality: “Battery
health is not about mileage alone — it is about how you use it. If you run the pack outside its thermal window or
deep cycle it too often, you’ll shorten its life. Fleets need to train operators to stay inside the safe zone [115].” It
also should be noted that the vehicle manufacturer sets the accessible capacity of a battery pack, which may be
limited to 80% to 90% of the pack’s full capacity.

<!-- page: 68 -->

To deepen understanding of battery packs, multiple battery chemistries are used for different reasons and
exhibit different performance across climates. Figure 46 shows a practical application of these technologies in a
heavy-duty platform. The Windrose Standard Range truck uses NCN Ternary battery technology to balance
energy density with thermal stability, demonstrating how chemistry choices directly influence vehicle
architecture and range capabilities [176].

> **Figure 46 — Windrose standard-range truck [176]**
> Type: photograph
> Axes: none
> Series: none
> Values: none
> Notes: Windrose Standard Range truck, 295 miles of range, NCM Ternary Lithium, 486 kWh

Commercial electric vehicle fleets must choose between five major battery chemistries, each with distinct
performance characteristics. Lithium Iron Phosphate (LFP) uses an iron-based cathode that provides excellent
safety and longevity. Nickel Manganese Cobalt 811 (NMC811) combines 80% nickel with 10% manganese and
10% cobalt for high energy storage. Nickel Cobalt Aluminum (NCA) uses aluminum-stabilized cathodes for
maximum energy density. Lithium Titanate Oxide (LTO) uses a titanium-based anode for ultra-long life and
extreme-temperature performance. Sodium-ion (Na-ion) replaces lithium with abundant sodium for lower cost
[405]. Each chemistry excels in different areas — no single option wins across all performance metrics, making
proper selection critical for operational success and TCO.

Note that the levers may be in different positions, but they share similar chemistries. In reality, the numbers
mean more because they represent the percentages of metals in the regions, and manufacturers use different
letter codes. However, they are referring to the same cathode chemistry. In a McKinsey 2024 battery analysis,
they used NMC811 in accordance with the North American industry convention [177].

Figure 47 uses a spider chart to compare of the five chemistries across seven critical performance areas, with
scores normalized on a 0 to 100 scale for direct comparison. Energy density measures how much energy fits in
each kilogram — higher scores mean a more extended range without weight penalties. Cycle life indicates how

<!-- page: 69 -->

many charge-discharge cycles the battery survives before replacement — LTO dominates at 150/100 (exceeding
the baseline), while NCA manages only 10/100. Cost efficiency uses inverse scoring, where a lower price equals a
higher score — Na-ion leads at 95/100 ($90/kWh) while LTO scores 50/100 ($200/kWh). Safety rating reflects
thermal stability and fire risk based on Underwriters Laboratories testing — LTO and LFP score 95-100/100,
while NCA manages only 50/100 [177]. Fast-charging capability measures how quickly batteries accept power —
LTO's 6C+ rating earns 100/100, enabling a 10-minute recharge. Temperature performance evaluates cold- and
hot-weather operation: LTO's -30°C to +60°C range scores 100/100, while NMC811 and NCA's -10°C to +45°C
ranges score 60/100. Power density indicates acceleration and hill-climbing capability — NCA leads at 95/100,
which is essential for loaded trucks on mountain grades [178].

> **Figure 47 — Spider web comparison of five heavy-duty battery chemistries**
> Type: radar chart / spider chart
> Axes: 0 to 100 scale across 7 axes
> Series: LFP, NMC811, NCA, LTO, Na-ion
> Values: Normalized scores across Cycle Life, Energy Density, Power Density, Temperature Performance, Fast Charge Capability, Safety Rating, Cost Efficiency.
> Notes: Figure 47: Spider web comparison of five heavy-duty battery chemistries

Visualizing these trade-offs is essential for specification. The spider chart presents a comparison of five major
heavy-duty battery chemistries across seven normalized performance metrics. This chart allows fleet managers
to assess the strengths and weaknesses of each option instantly — whether prioritizing the extreme cycle life of
LTO (150/100) against the superior energy density of NCA (100/100) — to match the correct battery technology
to their specific duty cycles.

<!-- page: 70 -->

Long-haul trucking needs maximum range, making NMC811 or NCA the right choice despite their shorter
lifespan. These batteries pack 250 to 300 Wh/kg, enabling 300 to 500-mile trips between charges — critical
when every pound of battery weight reduces revenue-generating cargo capacity [177]. Fleet managers accept
replacing batteries every 1,000 to 2,300 cycles because depot charging and predictable routes make
maintenance scheduling straightforward.

Urban delivery and last-mile routes favor LFP batteries through their exceptional 3,000 to 10,000 cycle lifespan
and $115/kWh cost. Yes, LFP batteries weigh 30% more for equivalent range, but daily routes under 150 miles
make this acceptable [179]. Multiple short trips per day take advantage of LFP's longevity — these batteries last
3 to 5 times longer than NMC811 in stop-and-go delivery work, dramatically lowering replacement costs [180].

Cold-climate operations require LTO or Na-ion batteries that operate reliably in extreme cold. Conventional
lithium-ion batteries lose 20% to 30% of their range below freezing and waste energy heating themselves before
the truck can move. LTO maintains full performance down to -30°C, eliminating range anxiety and wasted
energy in northern territories [178]. The premium price becomes worthwhile when it prevents trucks from being
stranded in winter conditions.

Opportunity charging scenarios — such as buses, port trucks, and warehouse shuttles — benefit from LTO's 10-
minute fast-charging capability. These vehicles can fully recharge during loading, unloading, or driver breaks. The
$200/kWh premium pays for itself through operational flexibility and 10,000 to 20,000 cycle durability that
extends battery life to 7 to 10 years, even with multiple daily charges [179].

Battery health diagnostics are becoming increasingly integrated with telematics platforms. Aftermarket
providers such as Geotab now offer analytics that track SOC and SOH over time, correlating degradation with
charging profiles and duty-cycle data [38]. This predictive maintenance approach allows fleets to plan pack
replacements years, minimizing capital shocks and improving residual value forecasting.

Taken together, these studies show that battery life depends far more on how the pack is used and managed
than on simple odometer miles, reinforcing the need for data-driven health monitoring and standardized
reporting across fleets. The field results in Figure 48 Illustrate this point. Even in hot-climate duty cycles, high-use vehicles that stay within temperature and charging limits do not exhibit dramatically faster SOH loss than
lower-use peers, highlighting the value of disciplined thermal management and charging practices.

<!-- page: 71 -->

> **Figure 48 — Battery state of health vs. time for varying use levels [38]**
> Type: line chart
> Axes: Vehicle Age [Months] vs SOH [%]
> Series: High Use >20000 km per year, Low Use < 8000 km per year
> Values: SOH ranges from 1.0 down to approx 0.85 over 48 months for both series in hot climates.
> Notes: Exclusively vehicles operating in hot climates, No DCFC use, Primary charge Level 2. Figure 48 Battery state of health vs. time for varying use levels [38]

Battery health is, therefore, not only a technical matter but a strategic determinant of BEV viability. Fleets that
implement robust SOC/SOH diagnostics, enforce best-practice charging policies, and integrate telematics-based
predictive maintenance can significantly extend usable pack life. Conversely, fleets that neglect these measures
risk premature pack replacements and higher overall costs.

To begin a battery evaluation program and predictive/prescriptive diagnostic protocols, the fleet must establish
baseline power measurements under standardized load conditions, typically 25%, 50%, 75%, and 100% rated
torque at constant speed, to create reference signatures that reveal efficiency degradation, thermal derating, or
control algorithm drift during subsequent service intervals.

The Tools section details equipment and procedures for in-house baseline measurement implementation. For
fleets requiring certified third-party validation, comprehensive duty-cycle analysis, or complex powertrain
investigations beyond standard diagnostics, UC Riverside's CE-CERT provides professional testing services
tailored to heavy-duty battery-electric vehicles [79].

CE-CERT operates an advanced heavy-duty chassis dynamometer facility with testing capabilities spanning
10,000 to 80,000-lbs. vehicle configurations across BEV, plug-in hybrid (PHEV), hybrid electric (HEV), and fuel cell
electric vehicle (FCEV) platforms, as well as conventional fuel trucks [79]. Their 48" dual-roll electric
dynamometer system delivers 600 hp of continuous testing capacity and 1,200 hp peak capability, enabling
complete baseline characterization across 17 standardized drive cycles, including UDDS, HFEDS, and custom
fleet duty cycles, with NIST-traceable measurement accuracy.

As a California Energy Commission Cal Testbed partner, CE-CERT develops and executes comprehensive test
plans that integrate SAE J1939 diagnostic data acquisition with chassis-dynamometer road-load simulation to
capture motor efficiency, inverter thermal performance, battery state-of-health correlation, and regenerative
braking effectiveness, using statistically validated measurement protocols. Figure 49 depicts a Class 8 truck

> Values: none
> Notes: Kenworth truck undergoing dynamometer testing at CE-CERT.

undergoing testing on a dynamometer, illustrating the specialized testing necessary to certify and “support the
evaluation of battery electric (BEV), plug-in hybrid (PHEV), hybrid electric (HEV), and fuel cell electric vehicles
(FCEV). CE-CERT’s testbeds enable comprehensive performance and emissions analysis across a range of duty
cycles and powertrain [79].”

Professional power investigations at CE-CERT support warranty validation, OEM performance-claim verification,
regulatory-compliance demonstration, and root-cause analysis of field-reported efficiency anomalies that
require controlled testing environments beyond fleet maintenance capabilities [79]. Their Mobile Emissions
Laboratory integration enables hybrid vehicle certification when emissions characterization is required alongside
electric powertrain analysis.

Fleet managers gain access to comprehensive test reports that include efficiency maps, thermal performance
validation, power-curve comparisons against baseline specifications, and diagnostic data exports compatible
with fleet management systems. Measurement consistency is recognized across EPA, CARB, and SAE testing
standards for multi-site validation programs or comparative technology assessments.

Performance metrics quantified by advanced testing facilities provide insight into the fundamental
electrochemical differences between battery types — a factor that directly shapes real-world outcomes for
commercial fleets. One of the most pronounced operational differences between battery technologies emerges
in their discharge characteristics under load.

Lead-acid batteries are prone to significant voltage sag under high current draw. This voltage drop limits their
ability to deliver consistent power, especially as the battery approaches depletion. In contrast, lithium-ion
chemistries exhibit a much flatter voltage profile throughout the majority of their discharge cycle. This stability

<!-- page: 73 -->

enables lithium-ion batteries to maintain a steady power output until they are nearly fully discharged, enhancing
the reliability and predictability of vehicle performance under demanding duty cycles.

Figure 50 contrasts the discharge curves of lead-acid and lithium-ion batteries. The illustration highlights why
lithium-ion technology provides fleets with a larger usable energy window, making it especially advantageous
for both auxiliary and primary traction applications. As a result, operators benefit from extended operational
range, increased efficiency, and reduced risk of performance degradation during critical operations.

> **Figure 50 — Lithium-Ion vs lead acid battery discharge curves [174]**
> Type: line chart
> Axes: Depth of Discharge (%) vs Battery Voltage (V)
> Series: Lead-Acid, Lithium-Ion
> Values: Lead-Acid voltage drops from approx 55V at 0% to approx 43V at 100%; Lithium-Ion voltage stays flat around 56V from 0% to approx 75% DoD, then drops steeply.
> Notes: Figure 50: Lithium-Ion vs lead acid battery discharge curves [174]

## 9.7 Essential Shop Tools

The transition from diesel-based fleet maintenance to full battery-electric service represents a profound cultural
and technical reformation. Diesel service bays are no longer compatible with the safety and precision demands
of modern HV powertrains. Instead, workshop environments must evolve into electrically safe ecosystems
where technicians interact daily with energized components capable of delivering 400 to 900 V direct current.
The National Fire Protection Association (NFPA) defines it in an emergency response guide [435]. This represents
not merely a procedural formality but a structural realignment of how fleet facilities define competency and
risk.

ASE Electrified Propulsion Vehicles (xEV) High-Voltage Electrical Safety Standards Version 2.1 builds upon this
principle, prescribing that all BEV-capable shops establish dedicated HV work zones, insulated flooring, and
controlled storage for damaged batteries [2] [3]. For fleet operators, the impact of these standards transcends
compliance — each procedural control directly reduces exposure to downtime, insurance disputes, and
technician attrition. In practice, applying NFPA and ASE protocols transforms the shop floor from a reactive
service bay into a managed electrical workspace, where risk becomes a quantifiable parameter rather than an
ambient hazard. Together, these frameworks define the foundation of safe BEV servicing.

<!-- page: 74 -->

In this new electric workshop, various diagnostic tools interface with the technician and the BEV. The ICE
measurement tools that once topped out at 30 V DC for sensor testing now must withstand spikes of several
kilovolts without loss of accuracy or insulation breakdown. High-voltage tooling experts advise that overvoltage
categories separate consumer-grade testers from professional-grade diagnostic systems, and that category III
equipment protects users during distribution-level measurements. At the same time, Category IV applies to
direct utility installations [181].

For fleet service operations, these categories directly define safe work practices. Selecting a Category III or IV
tester ensures that transient energy from traction batteries or power electronics cannot breach insulation or
cause arcing. Upgrading from a 300 V CAT III tool rated for 4 kV surges to a 600 V CAT III model capable of 6 kV
protection can increase both measurement reliability and technician safety.

Proper tool selection also encompasses contact-protected connectors, reinforced insulation, and strain-relieved
cables, all of which align with IEC 61010, NFPA 70E, and SAE J1766 standards for electrical safety. In practice,
compliance with overvoltage categories protects technicians, preserves measurement integrity, and anchors
fleet diagnostic operations within recognized high-voltage safety frameworks [181]. Fleets and technicians must
ensure they know exactly what tools they are working with, as meters are application-specific.

In BEV maintenance, only meters certified to at least CAT III 1000 V or CAT IV 600 V ratings meet the threshold
for safe operation. These tools, like the Fluke 87V Industrial Multimeter, combine transient suppression, double
insulation, and fused current ranges to withstand surges common in 800-volt traction architectures. Class 8
vehicles, such as the Freightliner eCascadia or Kenworth T880E, operate at nominal pack voltages up to 650 V,
supporting energy capacities up to 470 HP (350kW) consistent with DC fast charging for SAE J1772 CCS
connectors [407].

Insulation resistance verification is among the most critical tests in a BEV service, a direct analogue to
compression testing in the diesel era. The test validates that high-voltage circuits remain properly isolated from
vehicle chassis ground, preventing hazardous leakage currents or unintended conductive paths. Industry
practice requires that any circuit operating above 120 V DC exhibit at least 1 MΩ of resistance when subjected to
test voltages between 500 and 5,000 V [182].

Instrument safety is critical in high-voltage service environments. Technicians must use only CAT III 1000 V or
CAT IV 600 V-rated multimeters and leads, ensuring the test equipment is rated at or above the maximum
anticipated voltage. Figure 51 displays the Fluke 1550C FC insulation tester, a professional-grade instrument
designed for safe HV diagnostics during BEV service operations [35].

> **Figure 51 — Fluke 1550C FC 5 kV insulation tester [35]**
> Type: photograph
> Axes: none
> Series: none
> Values: none
> Notes: Fluke 1550C FC 5 kV insulation tester.

<!-- page: 75 -->

According to ABB E-mobility’s Engineering Group, “Insulation testing is where we see the most errors in field
service. People rush through tests without allowing discharge time, and that can lead to shock hazards or
equipment damage. We are deploying smart meters that automatically log live-dead-live verification to remove
human guesswork [183].”

Proper test-lead configuration prevents leakage current from compromising measurements. Guard wires detect
contamination pathways that otherwise would introduce errors into insulation resistance readings. Figure 52
illustrates how transient current can migrate along the surface of a test lead or HV wire if its insulation becomes
contaminated or mechanically degraded. Dirt, oil, and salts reduce surface resistance, allowing current leakage
that may distort readings or, in severe cases, cause electric shock or surface tracking. Professional-grade testers
incorporate a guard wire — typically a blue auxiliary lead — to sense and isolate surface leakage, thereby
distinguishing actual conductor faults from insulation breakdown. This principle underscores the need to use
insulation-resistance testers with integrated guard-detection circuits.

> **Figure 52 — Insulation resistance test leads showing leakage current path [182]**
> Type: schematic
> Axes: none
> Series: Test voltage (Tester's red lead), Guard wire (Tester's blue lead), Contamination (Dirt, oil, salts, etc.)
> Values: none
> Notes: Figure 52 Insulation resistance test leads showing leakage current path [182]

Traditional meters can be impractical and hazardous to use on a heavy-duty BEVs. Hall-effect clamp meters,
which allow for non-intrusive monitoring of DC and AC currents without disconnecting circuits, are more
practical. The Hall effect, identified by Edwin Hall, explains that when a conductor or semiconductor, with
current flowing in one direction, was introduced perpendicular to a magnetic field, voltage could be measured at
right angles to the current path [184]. In BEV diagnostics, this enables technicians to measure high currents in
traction or charging circuits by clamping around the conductor. See Figure 53 for a Fluke variety ammeter [35].

The non-invasive nature of the amp clamp reduces technician exposure while enabling continuous, real-time
data collection. Clamps must be zeroed prior to every measurement, so scaling factors for mV/A accessories can
be verified to avoid misinterpretation [35].

<!-- page: 76 -->

> **Figure 53 — Fluke 381 remote display true-RMS AC/DC clamp [35]**
> Type: photograph
> Axes: none
> Series: none
> Values: none
> Notes: Fluke 381 remote display true-RMS AC/DC clamp [35]

For a more detailed look at the operation of a BEV’s electrical system, the PicoScope Effective electric vehicle
diagnostics with the PicoScope 4425A system requires systematic waveform analysis against known reliable
reference patterns rather than relying solely on numeric fault codes [185]. Technicians must capture three-phase
motor current signatures using the 2000A clamps and compare the resulting waveforms to manufacturer
specifications or established baseline patterns to identify winding imbalances, phase dropout conditions, or
inverter switching anomalies that numeric diagnostics cannot detect [185].

Waveform capture across all three motor phases reveals asymmetries that indicate inverter malfunctions or
motor winding problems. Professional oscilloscopes with simultaneous multi-channel sampling enable
technicians to detect dead-leg failures, phase timing misalignment, and shoot-through conditions that would
otherwise remain invisible. Figure 54 shows a PicoScope BEV diagnostics kit configured for comprehensive
three-phase motor analysis during troubleshooting [185].

HV battery pack diagnostics require differential-voltage measurements up to 1400V, with careful attention to voltage patterns across cell groups, and reference to OEM service data for acceptable variance thresholds typically measured in millivolts per cell [185]. The CP/PP charging communication protocol analysis involves decoding pulse-width modulation signals between the vehicle and EVSE equipment, where technicians must cross-reference captured waveforms against SAE J1772 or IEC 62196 standards to verify proper handshaking sequences and current capacity negotiation [185]. This diagnostic approach mirrors traditional ignition or fuel injection waveform analysis. However, it requires building a reference library of known reliable BEV waveforms by documenting properly functioning systems, since many electric powertrain faults manifest as waveform distortions rather than discrete fault codes [185].

Thermal management has become an essential component for diagnosing and maintaining BEVs. Unlike diesel vehicles, which rely primarily on exhaust heat and simple coolant loops, BEVs use complex multi-loop liquid-cooling systems to keep batteries, inverters, and e-axles operating safely together. Today, technicians rely on thermal imaging cameras (see Figure 55) to spot trouble early; these cameras can detect hotspots in busbars, connectors, and coolant interfaces that might otherwise go unnoticed. Infrared thermography provides early warning of issues such as poor thermal conductivity or uneven current flow, which can lead to failures or shorten battery life if left unaddressed. In real-world practice, a thermal camera can reveal loose terminal lugs or temperature imbalances between modules well before they become significant problems.

Today, technicians rely on thermal imaging cameras to spot trouble early. These cameras can pick up hotspots in busbars, connectors, and coolant interfaces that might otherwise go unnoticed. Hot spots at busbar

---

<!-- page: 78 -->
Messy Middle Powertrain Service & Maintenance

June 25, 2026 Page 78

connections, insufficient coolant flow, or degraded pumps can create uneven aging between modules. In real-world practice, a thermal camera can reveal loose terminal lugs or temperature imbalances between modules well before they become significant problems [405]. “The most important part of our battery design philosophy is managing temperature gradients across modules. A 3°C difference between cells can double the degradation rate over a thousand cycles. That is why we use liquid-cooling plates, advanced BMS balancing, and predictive algorithms to keep everything within a 2°C window,” Stenqvist said [163].

However, Figure 55 illustrates the Fluke TiX580 infrared camera that can be used without the AI algorithm. In addition to thermal imaging of battery packs and high-voltage connections, it also can troubleshoot low-voltage relays and other components [35].

> **Figure 55 — Fluke TiX580 infrared camera used for thermal imaging [35]**
> Type: Photograph
> Notes: Decorative photograph of a Fluke TiX580 infrared thermal imaging camera.

Heat-exchange systems running through them are designed to maintain cell temperatures within narrow bands, but real-world duty cycles stress these systems. Hot spots at busbar connections, insufficient coolant flow, or degraded pumps can create uneven aging between modules. Thermal imaging during PM inspections can detect these anomalies before they cascade into accelerated degradation [35].

Phase angle is the electrical angle between the stator’s rotating magnetic field (set by the three-phase voltage from the inverter) and the rotor’s magnetic field (seen in the stator currents). This angle is created when the inverter drives three-phase AC with a 120° phase offset. As the software advances or retards the rotating field relative to the rotor, the measured phase difference between voltage and current changes. In field-oriented control, torque is essentially proportional to the component of stator current that is 90° electrical to the rotor flux, so commanding a specific phase angle and current gives precise torque on demand. With DC you only get a static field with no controlled rotating angle, so you cannot continuously modulate torque and speed in the same smooth way in a synchronous traction motor [186].

The same phase-angle information is used as an absolute position sensor. By observing the instantaneous electrical angle between stator quantities (voltages/currents or back-EMF) and mapping it through motor pole-pair data, the controller converts that electrical angle into a unique mechanical shaft position over 0° to 360°. Sensor-less algorithms inject test signals or analyze natural back-EMF to lock onto this phase angle, while other drives cross-check it with an encoder or resolver. In both cases, accurate absolute position lets a

---

<!-- page: 79 -->
Messy Middle Powertrain Service & Maintenance

June 25, 2026 Page 79

heavy-duty BEV motor produce high launch torque, coordinate multiple axles, and blend traction and regenerative braking without jerks or wheel slip [187].

Fleets need to ensure they work with the OEM to acquire a laptop or scan tool capable of analyzing enhanced vehicle CAN data. Quarto states, “the acquisition of enhanced diagnostic tools needs to be a part of the contractual agreements between the fleet and OEM. For example, utilizing the OEM serial data (scan) tool with products like BattScan SOH tester, DC-1000, and oscilloscope testing would permit broader and deeper testing and diagnostics. This will provide fleet diagnostics and services to be more thorough, flexible, and complete [159].”

The ALL-TEST PRO MD III motor testing assembly addresses this gap and is explicitly designed to test permanent magnet motors and motor-generators used in electric and hybrid vehicles. This instrument measures winding resistance balance, inductance imbalance, rotor flux integrity, and insulation quality at frequencies relevant to high-speed BEV applications. For fleets, this tool enables predictive maintenance, detecting incipient winding faults before they progress to open-circuit failures or thermal runaway. Figure 56 shows the ALL-TEST PRO MD III motor testing assembly, explicitly designed for permanent-magnet motors in electric and hybrid vehicles [188].

No modern BEV shop can function without OEM-approved scan tools. These platforms interface directly with the vehicle’s battery management system (BMS), inverter controllers, and thermal loops, providing real-time telemetry on SOC, SOH, HVIL continuity, and software fault codes. For advanced operations, OEM scan tools are

---
