required to conduct cell rebalancing, clear interlocks, initiate thermal purges, and install OTA software updates. Bosch Diagnostics explains that the difference between BEV and diesel scan work lies in the depth of electronic control: “Without the OEM-approved toolchain, fleets risk blind spots in monitoring HV safety and diagnostics [189].”

This tooling transformation reflects the broader cultural transition in fleet shops. Where diesel maintenance rewards mechanical aptitude, BEV readiness requires electrical literacy, diagnostic discipline, and strict adherence to safety. Shops that invest in high-voltage diagnostic tools, insulation testers, and PPE position themselves not only for regulatory compliance but also for fleet reliability and technician retention.

Key questions for fleets to ask when inquiring about a BEV include, “as the OEM developed the battery apck remanufacturing program to support the customer, does the OEM provide special hardware and software tools to perform any battery pack diagnostics and service? When you get into the contractual arrangements [ask] who is providing the special tools, and are the technicians being trained to use the special tools. It really becomes a very tiered type of program. You’ve got to know what quaestions to ask,” Quarto said [159].

The evolution from diesel to BEV maintenance requires not only diagnostic sophistication but a physical transformation of the service environment itself. Class 8 battery packs, weighing between 3,000 and 8,000 lbs., demand specialized handling systems — dual-post lifts with battery fixture jacks, 4,000-lbs. capacity transfer carts with roll decks and locking tabs, and overhead cranes rated to at least 5 tons for safe removal and installation [2] [3]. To comply with ASE xEV Safety Standards Version 2.1, BEV facilities also must maintain secured PPE storage zones, segregating clean and contaminated equipment, and decontamination rooms featuring a minimum six-foot transition path for technicians exiting "hot" areas [190].

The Hioki MR8848 Memory HiCorder addresses these requirements by simultaneously sampling multiple isolated channels, capturing voltage, current, and sensor waveforms at rates sufficient to resolve inverter switching behavior (typically 10 to 20 kHz). For BEV diagnostics, this capability enables technicians to correlate motor phase currents with inverter gate-drive signals, identify timing skew between phase legs, and document intermittent faults that appear only under load or under specific duty-cycle conditions. Unlike scan tools that report processed parameters, the HiCorder captures raw electrical signatures, which are essential for warranty claims and root cause analysis. The instrument's isolated inputs prevent ground loops that would otherwise corrupt measurements on high-voltage systems referenced to chassis ground. Figure 58 shows the Hioki MR8848 Memory HiCorder portable high-speed data acquisition system used for multichannel waveform capture during field testing [191].

Fire-resistant storage enclosures and ventilated containment zones are required for damaged or compromised battery packs, located no closer than 50’ to occupied structures, and equipped with continuous thermal monitoring and Class D fire-suppression systems [192]. Equally essential are vehicle exclusion barriers — red-and-white post systems with warning signage — to mark high-voltage work zones and restrict foot traffic. These measures elevate BEV service bays from conventional mechanical shops to electrically controlled environments where lifting, containment, and contamination control are integral to technician safety and regulatory compliance. Light-duty shop standards have been assumed in this paper because, to date, no official standards for heavy-duty have been established [193].

## 10 HYDROGEN

For Class 8 long-haul freight, hydrogen currently is regarded as a viable zero-emission alternative to diesel because it is expected to preserve range, minimize refueling time, and have less impact on payload than other alternatives. In practice, these expectations have yet to be proven. Battery-electric solutions remain well-suited to short-haul and regional duty cycles, where grid access and downtime constraints are less severe. As of now,

---

<!-- page: 83 -->
Messy Middle Powertrain Service & Maintenance

June 25, 2026 Page 83

“hydrogen is the only viable zero-emission solution currently proposed as one-for-one replacements for diesel in the future of long-haul heavy-duty trucks,” according to NACFE’s report on hydrogen trucks [194]. Three hydrogen use cases will be discussed in this paper: hydrogen-diesel co-combustion (HDC), the hydrogen internal combustion engine (H2ICE), and the hydrogen fuel cell vehicle (HCEV).

HDC offers a retrofit solution for fleets seeking to reduce emissions without extensive engine modifications. See Figure 59. This strategy uses a supplemental hydrogen injection alongside standard diesel operation, allowing existing Class 8 trucks to operate in HDC mode. Notably, this approach does not require a redesign of the base engine, offering a practical and cost-efficient pathway for operators interested in hydrogen adoption while maintaining their current diesel assets. North America OEMs currently offer no production HDC engines.

> **Figure 59 — Prior Power hydrogen injection diesel system [408]**
> Type: Photograph
> Notes: Photograph showing the rear cab area of a Dennis Eagle truck with Prior Power hydrogen injection equipment.

Research by Kheirkhah et al highlights the significant environmental benefits of HDC, demonstrating a 25% reduction in CO2 emissions (0.83 vs. 1.11 kg/kWh) for Class 8 trucks [409]. Additionally, engine-out nitrogen oxides (NOx) were reduced by 22%, and tailpipe NOx emissions saw reductions of up to 60% under medium load conditions. These findings underscore the potential of HDC as a transitional technology, enabling fleets to reduce emissions and comply with evolving regulations without requiring complete fleet overhauls.

H2-ICEs are a bigger commitment to the hydrogen ecosystem. Cummins notes that hydrogen ICE engines like the X15H “operate on the same cycle as regular natural gas engines and have almost the same components,” making them familiar to fleets already servicing diesel or natural gas platforms [195]. They are more tolerant of impurities, simpler to maintain, and less costly to produce than fuel cells. The H2ICE is expected to allow fleets to pursue decarbonization with minimal operational disruption. See Figure 60. There are currently no OEMs in production offering H2ICE engines in North America.

FCEVs convert hydrogen into electricity using a proton exchange membrane (PEM) fuel cell [197]. See Figure 61. These vehicles offer high drivetrain efficiency but require new technician competencies in stack diagnostics, high-voltage safety, and predictive maintenance. Their long-term value lies in zero tailpipe emissions and access to potentially abundant fuel, though training pipelines and infrastructure readiness remain limited. Another limiting factor is technical complexity. Not only do technicians have to be BEV-savvy, but they also have to be competent in hydrogen-related technicalities.

The actual fuel cell stack resembles a cube or a rectangle, as it is a series of stacked cells on top of one another. The fuel cell might be integrated with its support systems, as shown in the Ballard Heavy Duty FCmove-XD power plant, Figure 62. To learn more about the intricacies of hydrogen fuel cells, refer to the DOE Hydrogen Fuel Cell Engines and Related Technologies Course Manual which is based on the maintenance of a Ballard FCEV bus [86]. The manual details the significant technological differences between diesel and FCEV maintenance.

Understanding PEM fuel cell electrochemistry is vital, but technicians also must be familiar with the integrated systems supporting production modules. The Ballard FCmove-XD demonstrates this integration for Class 8 trucks by combining a 200+ kW stack with subsystems for air, hydrogen, thermal, and power management in a single unit. Unlike basic diagrams, real modules feature compressors for precise airflow, humidifiers to manage moisture, hydrogen recirculation pumps for high efficiency, coolant loops operating at 60 to 80°C, and DC-DC converters for voltage control [198]. Maintenance involves regular checks like air filter changes, coolant monitoring, and leak testing. Figure 62 shows the module's integrated design.

Hydrogen propulsion is expected to offer advantages that align well with the demands of heavy-duty trucking, beginning with refueling speed. The Port of Oakland, FirstElement Fuel’s True Zero station has demonstrated that a hydrogen truck can be refueled in under 10 minutes, dispensing 100 kilograms of hydrogen in a single session and serving up to 200 Class 8 trucks per day [410]. This puts hydrogen on par with CNG in terms of fast-fueling time. Timed fueling options exist for both H2 and CNG that can extend fueling sessions over hours to reduce costs.

---

<!-- page: 86 -->
Messy Middle Powertrain Service & Maintenance

June 25, 2026 Page 86

Hydrogen is expected to strengthen infrastructure resilience. In 2023, federal and private investments were pouring into hydrogen hubs and designated freight corridors, with billions of dollars committed [194]. However, in 2025, policy changes significantly reduced funding and rescinded many commitments.

Unlike battery-electric trucks, which largely depend on high-capacity grid connections, hydrogen can be transported and stored in trailers and delivered remotely to vehicles via specially configured compressor trailers that operate on a variety of fuels. This flexibility makes it worthwhile in ports, warehouses, and rural corridors where grid reliability can be uncertain.

Early-production North American heavy-duty fuel cell trucks will be comparable in weight to their BEV counterparts. As the Clean Air Task Force explains, hydrogen is expected to allow fleets to maintain gross vehicle weights close to today’s diesel standards at some point in the future [440].

Finally, hydrogen propulsion provides important environmental and community health benefits. Because hydrogen does not produce soot, sulfur oxides, or carbon monoxide when burned, and because it can be tuned for lean combustion that lowers NOx, it contributes to cleaner air [86]. At fleet scale, NACFE projects that hydrogen adoption could significantly reduce CO2, NOx, and particulate matter across freight corridors, helping fleets stay ahead of emissions regulations while improving conditions in communities near major transport routes.

Research by Dr. Johnson demonstrates why this matters. Emissions from ports and warehousing operations do not remain within the facility — they disperse into surrounding neighborhoods driven by wind patterns and atmospheric conditions. Using NOAA's HYSPLIT trajectory modeling, Johnson's team mapped how pollutants from freight operations in the Inland Empire travel up to 56 sq. mi. into residential communities, with exposure varying dramatically based on time of day (morning winds at 5 mph versus afternoon winds at 15 mph) and temperature differentials [79].

For fleets operating near disadvantaged communities identified under California's SB 535 CalEnviroScreen criteria, the transition to zero-emission propulsion represents not only regulatory compliance but also a measurable improvement in residential air quality [199]. Figure 63 illustrates the geographic dispersion of emissions from warehouse fleet operations into surrounding residential areas based on wind trajectory modeling.

Modeling of pollutant dispersion from Inland Empire freight operations into residential areas spanning up to 56 sq. mi., demonstrating how time-of-day wind patterns (4 a.m. at 5 mph vs. 5 p.m. at 15 mph) affect neighborhood exposure [79].

Despite these benefits, hydrogen adoption presents several challenges that cannot be ignored; the largest is the immaturity of the infrastructure. The scale of this challenge becomes clear when examining the current state of hydrogen fueling availability. California, the only state with a meaningful hydrogen network, has just 55 active, open-retail hydrogen fueling stations as of November 2024, concentrated almost entirely around the Los Angeles and San Francisco Bay Area metropolitan areas. The network contracted rather than expanded in the past year, with Shell closing seven major stations and supply disruptions limiting service hours across remaining facilities. Meeting a modest mid-decade target appears unrealistic.

California Air Resources Board projected only 87 hydrogen stations by the end of 2025, falling 60% short of the 200-station goal set by Governor Newsom's 2018 executive order [200]. DOE and NREL confirm that each hydrogen fueling station costs significantly more to build than an equivalent diesel or electric charging facility [201]. Producing and transporting hydrogen are currently prohibitively expensive.

The second major challenge is fuel cost. As Thomas Healy CEO and founder of Hyliion said, “Hydrogen is still too expensive for fleets to run profitably. Until we see prices come down closer to diesel, it is going to be hard to get adoption [202].” Hydrogen pricing in California often exceeds $30/kg at the vehicle pump. DOE has set a long-term goal of lowering the cost of dispensed hydrogen to under $7/kg, but current fleet costs remain above that level [87].

Fuel purity adds another layer of operational risk. Hydrogen used in fuel cells must meet the SAE J2719 quality standard, which requires a purity of 99.97% and imposes tight limits on contaminants such as carbon monoxide and ammonia [411]. Even trace impurities can damage stacks. To prevent this, SAE J3219 prescribes testing procedures at fueling stations to screen hydrogen before dispensing [203].

---

<!-- page: 88 -->
Messy Middle Powertrain Service & Maintenance

June 25, 2026 Page 88

In addition to purity risks, hydrogen use also generates excess heat that must be dissipated. Otto explained that while diesel engines release about half of their waste heat through exhaust, fuel cells reject all of it through cooling systems. As a result, fuel cell vehicles require much larger heat exchangers and more complex cooling loops, raising both engineering demands and maintenance burdens [115].

Material durability is a significant challenge for hydrogen-powered heavy-duty vehicles, particularly because of hydrogen embrittlement. Hydrogen molecules are tiny and diffuse readily into metals at elevated pressure and temperature, a process known as hydrogen permeation. Over time, this can introduce hydrogen into the metal microstructure, making it brittle and prone to crack initiation and growth under load. Such degradation can occur in critical high-pressure components such as storage tanks, lines, and fittings. See Figure 64 for an example. A compression or swage fitting may compromise vehicle reliability and safety if not properly managed [204].

> **Figure 64 — Compression fitting [86]**

> Notes: Technical drawing showing 1) Tube, Nut, Rear Ferrule, Front Ferrule, Fitting Body; 2) assembled tube inserted into fitting; 3) fully tightened fitting; and 4) Gap Inspection Gauge showing "Attempt to insert this (thinner) slot between nut and body".

Current research focuses on developing advanced coatings and specialized alloys to mitigate the risks of hydrogen embrittlement. However, long-term data from fleet operations remains limited, and ongoing durability testing is necessary to fully understand the lifespan and performance of these materials under real-world conditions. One study conducted by SAE highlighted that material degradation is a considerable maintenance concern that will persist until more comprehensive durability assessments are available [412].

Material durability also intersects with safety protocols. Otto described hydrogen as a particularly challenging element, noting that its small molecular size allows it to leak from containment systems readily. This characteristic, combined with the risk of embrittlement, underscores the importance of routine inspections. Technicians must remain vigilant during maintenance checks to identify early signs of material degradation, ensuring that any compromised components are addressed before they pose a risk to vehicle operation or safety [115].

Recent work in the UK’s APC-18 “Project Brunel” H2-ICE program has focused on addressing these issues in engine internals rather than in storage vessels [205]. As a consortium supplier, Zircotec developed and tested specialist hydrogen-barrier coatings “to combat the negative effect hydrogen can have on some metals,” working alongside project partners Cummins, Johnson Matthey, and PHINIA. These coatings are now described as “market ready.” Zircotec explains that its ceramic coating “acts as a hydrogen permeation barrier for H2ICE systems,” and that the high-density, controlled-porosity coating “can prevent hydrogen embrittlement” by minimizing hydrogen diffusion and leakage through coated surfaces. According to Zircotec Engineering Director Dominic Graham, “Our coatings [are] enabling the safe use of high-risk materials for combustion engine internals,” and the APC-18 development work has reinforced the company’s position “at the forefront of material science for future mobility solutions [204].”

It is important to note that these developments are targeted explicitly at hydrogen internal combustion engines and associated fuel-injection and exhaust components, not at PEM fuel cells. The APC-18/Project Brunel work is consistently described as a hydrogen combustion engine program for medium- and heavy-duty applications, with coatings applied to H2ICE and related hardware rather than to PEM stacks or their balance-of-plant components.

## 10.1 Hydrogen Safety

Hydrogen’s physical properties make safety management central to its adoption in heavy-duty fleets. Unlike diesel, hydrogen has a wide flammability range (4% to 75% in air) and a low ignition energy, meaning even small leaks can accumulate and become hazardous. Compounding the risk, hydrogen flames are invisible in daylight and odorless. Otto emphasized this distinction, “With hydrogen, the flame is invisible in daylight. You cannot just look and see it like you can with a diesel fire. That means you must rely on sensors, alarms, and shutoffs. If you don’t have those systems in place, you will not even know it is burning until it is too late [115].”

To address these risks, safety codes require engineered controls. NFPA 2 mandates robust ventilation, hazardous-area electrical classifications, minimum clearance distances, and emergency shutoffs in hydrogen service bays [206]. Vehicle storage systems must comply with SAE J2579, which governs tank construction and service guidelines, while fueling safeguards are defined in SAE J2601-2, including pressure and temperature limits and mandatory fail-safe communication between the vehicle and the dispenser [207] [208].

In practice, hydrogen safety is as much about procedures as it is about equipment. Dedicated hydrogen bays require explosion-proof systems, gas detectors, and flame-resistant PPE. NHTSA and SAE emphasize the importance of training technicians in venting procedures, purge protocols, and high-voltage lockouts when servicing FCEVs. Taken together, the engineered systems and strict procedural discipline form a layered defense, ensuring hazards are contained before incidents occur.

## 10.2 Hydrogen Maintenance

Maintaining hydrogen-powered trucks requires new technician skills, specialized tools, and compliance with evolving safety codes. Fleets cannot rely on conventional diesel practices alone; they must prepare their workforce to meet the unique requirements of hydrogen.

Regulatory codes are the foundation. The California Fire Code (§2309) requires repair garages for lighter-than-air vehicles to provide non-combustible walls, hydrogen-specific ventilation (six air changes per hour under normal conditions and 12 at 1% hydrogen concentration), and detectors connected to alarms and automatic shutoffs

---

<!-- page: 90 -->
Messy Middle Powertrain Service & Maintenance

June 25, 2026 Page 90

[209]. Canada is adopting CSA’s Hydrogen Gas Vehicle Maintenance Facilities Code, which mirrors NFPA 2 but adds explicit requirements for certified hydrogen technicians [210].

The complexity of systems is one of the most significant changes for technicians. Otto reported, “On the fuel cell trucks, you’ve got multiple coolant loops, not just one like a diesel. You must bleed them in a specific sequence, which is not intuitive. We have had technicians try to fill it with diesel, and the system locked out. You need training and special tools to purge the air correctly [115].” Fuel systems often require pre-pressurization with gases such as nitrogen. This demonstrates that hydrogen maintenance often requires more detailed procedures and specialized training than diesel maintenance.

Specialized tools are becoming standard. Ballard requires portable hydrogen leak detectors, calibration adapters, and deionized water conductivity meters for stack service [198]. Toyota/Kenworth fleets use tank-defueling scan tools, Hyundai specifies valve service tools, and DOE prescribes calibration gases for verifying leak detectors [86].

The ATEQ H6000 is production-grade hydrogen leak testing equipment, featuring a handheld probe with sensitivity to detect leaks as small as 5 to 100 parts per million (PPM) — enough to identify connector seal degradation, fitting looseness, or tank valve leakge before escalation into safety incidents [211]. Calibration against traceable standards per SAE J3219 is mandatory to ensure detection accuracy. Figure 65 shows the ATEQ H6000 hydrogen portable leak tester console with handheld probe.

> **Figure 65 — ATEQ H6000 hydrogen portable leak tester [211]**
> Type: Photograph
> Notes: Photograph of the ATEQ H6000 portable leak tester console and handheld probe.

Predictive diagnostics also are expanding. Various researchers have shown electrochemical impedance spectroscopy as a rapid method for assessing stack degradation [413]. IEEE researchers have shown that Kalman filters can predict stack health with 90% accuracy [212]. Ballard reports processing more than 50 terabytes of fleet data in real time through its Remote Diagnostics Unit to anticipate failures [198].

Real-world fleet experience illustrates the stakes. A Nikola Tre operator reported that the trucks “cost five to ten times that of a standard Class 8 drayage truck” and that one unit “was out of service for at least seven weeks out of the last nine months” because of cooling problems and mobile refueler downtime [414]. Otto noted one case where technicians had to connect remotely with engineers to purge a cooling system after repeated failures on the dyno [115].

Training modules emphasize hydrogen’s unique combustion characteristics, such as its broader flammability range, exceptionally low ignition energy, and a lean-to-stoichiometric ratio of about 34:1, compared with 14.7:1 for gasoline or 14.5:1 for diesel. These properties make engines more prone to knock and backfire, so technicians learn dual-spark ignition, exhaust gas recirculation, and injector calibration. Routine inspections of

---

<!-- page: 91 -->
Messy Middle Powertrain Service & Maintenance

June 25, 2026 Page 91

pressure relief devices and J2579-compliant fittings are added to otherwise familiar diesel-style practices. First responders also must be careful not to freeze pressure relief valves closed when spraying water.

Together, these examples show that hydrogen maintenance is not just an extension of diesel practice — it is a new discipline. Fleets must invest in training, equipment, and predictive analytics systems to maintain acceptable uptime while meeting safety and regulatory requirements.

### 10.2.1 H2ICE versus FCEV

Technician training and shop preparation diverge sharply depending on whether fleets adopt H2ICE or FCEV. Both platforms require expertise in high-pressure storage, leak detection, and fueling safety, but day-to-day workflows differ significantly.

For H2ICEs, training builds directly on experience with diesel and natural gas. Cummins explains, “A hydrogen engine can be installed in the same equipment as a diesel engine while also using the same transmission, cooling systems, and hydraulic systems. Maintenance practices and costs are comparable to those of diesel engines. The major difference is the onboard hydrogen storage system [21] [22].” Hydrogen systems operate at very high pressures of 10,000 psi or more, whereas natural gas operates at 3,600 psi and diesel at ambient pressure. Otto confirmed this point, “If you are working on a hydrogen ICE, it feels a lot like working on a natural gas engine [115].”

College of the Desert training modules emphasize hydrogen’s unique combustion characteristics, such as its broader flammability range, exceptionally low ignition energy, and a stoichiometric ratio of about 34.33:1, compared with 14.7:1 for gasoline [213]. These properties make engines more prone to knock and backfire, so technicians must learn dual-spark ignition, exhaust gas recirculation, and injector calibration. Routine inspections of pressure relief devices and J2579-compliant fittings are added to otherwise familiar diesel-style practices.

For FCEVs, training diverges more dramatically. Otto explained, “If you’re working on a fuel cell, that’s a whole new world. You are dealing with high voltage, membranes, and stack diagnostics. It’s not just a little bit different, it is a totally different career path for technicians [115].” FCEV training requires mastery of electrochemical systems, predictive diagnostics, and high-voltage lockout/tagout protocols. Service intervals differ as well. Fuel cells eliminate “oil change” type service, but require filter replacements every 30,000 to 40,000 miles, coolant changes every 150,000 miles, and stack replacements of about 400,000 to 700,000 miles depending on duty cycle [198]. Axles still require lube changes per OEM specifications.

Fleet leaders confirm the workforce's readiness gap. Hyliion’s Healy said, “We have seen firsthand that it is not easy to find techs who are certified to handle high-voltage and hydrogen at the same time. That’s slowing things down. We can train diesel guys on H2ICE fairly quickly, but fuel cells are a different story. You need new certifications [202].” As an example of these differences in maintenance, see Figure 66 for daily and weekly FCEV bus maintenance checks.

The fuel cell stack leak-down test exemplifies the procedural complexity that distinguishes FCEV maintenance from conventional diesel or even H2ICE service. Unlike engine compression tests that technicians routinely perform with handheld gauges, stack integrity verification requires pressurizing the hydrogen side of the membrane with inert nitrogen — never hydrogen — to a specified pressure (typically 0.5 to 1.0 bar above ambient) and monitoring the decay rate through a calibrated rotameter over a defined interval. The test confirms that neither the membrane electrode assembly (MEA) nor the bipolar plate gaskets exhibit crossover leaks that would allow hydrogen to migrate to the cathode side and escape through the exhaust. Such a crossover not only reduces fuel efficiency but creates a safety hazard: unburned hydrogen in the exhaust stream could accumulate in enclosed spaces.

The DOE Hydrogen Fuel Cell Engines Course Manual, developed through College of the Desert, specifies this procedure in detail — including acceptable decay rates, equipment calibration requirements, and failure criteria [86]. Figure 67 Illustrates the fuel cell leak-down test schematic showing a nitrogen bottle and a rotameter setup for stack integrity verification. Properly setting up the rotameter or pressure regulator is essential for not blowing out sensitive systems.

Academic pipelines are beginning to respond. US colleges such as Rio Hondo and Saddleback now offer certificates in hydrogen and fuel cell technician training, while in Europe, the IMI Level 3 Hydrogen FCEV Qualification has become a recognized standard entry credential [210]. In practice, H2ICE adoption enables incremental upskilling of existing diesel technicians. In contrast, FCEV adoption requires fleets to build a new class of cross-disciplinary specialists proficient in hydrogen handling, high-voltage safety, and digital diagnostics.

## 11 WHEELS, TIRES & BRAKES

The maintenance needs of wheels, tires and brakes on BEVs are different from those of diesel-powered vehicles.

## 11.1 Wheels

Wheel-end integrity remains foundational to safe commercial vehicle operation regardless of powertrain technology. Federal regulations mandate minimum tread depths of 4/32" on steer axles and 2/32" on all other positions, with daily Driver Vehicle Inspection Reports required under 49 CFR §396.11 [215]. These baseline requirements apply across diesel, natural gas, battery-electric, and fuel-cell platforms. However, BEV, H2ICE, HCNG, and FCEV, and other variants, introduce substantially higher wheel-end stresses that require maintenance and inspection protocols beyond conventional practices for several reasons.

---

<!-- page: 96 -->
Messy Middle Powertrain Service & Maintenance

June 25, 2026 Page 96

Battery electric vehicle curb weights exceed diesel equivalents by 8,000 to 12,000 lbs. due to battery pack mass. The Tesla Semi has a curb weight of more than 23,000 lbs., the Freightliner eCascadia of more than 21,000 lbs., and the Volvo VNR Electric of more than 22,000 lbs. [416]. Combined with instantaneous electric motor torque delivery, these elevated masses increase component loading by 15% to 25% compared to diesel baseline operations. This weight differential directly impacts wheel bearings, fastener systems, and structural components throughout the wheel assembly.

Natural gas vehicles present distinct considerations for wheel and truck stances. CNG and RNG tractors typically carry 2,500 to 4,000 lbs. of high-pressure cylinder storage mounted above the frame rails. This configuration raises the vehicle's center of gravity by 2" to 4" and increases front axle load by 5% to 8% during dynamic braking and cornering maneuvers [218]. The elevated center of gravity requires closer attention to wheel alignment specifications and monitoring for irregular wear patterns that indicate suspension of geometry changes under load. Vehicle length also may be affected, requiring longer-wheelbase vehicles.

The federal 2,000-lb. gross vehicle weight allowance for natural gas and battery-electric tractors under the FAST Act provisions enables fleets to operate closer to statutory axle ratings while protecting payload capacity [219]. This regulatory accommodation could result in operations within 500 to 1,000 lbs. of maximum legal axle limits. While this improves freight economics, it compresses engineering safety margins at the wheel end, where load capacity, fastener fatigue resistance, and bearing thermal limits converge relative to comparable diesel trucks rated at 80,000 lbs. However, many diesel trucks operate today well above 80,000 lbs., with weights ranging from 90,000 lbs. to 140,000 lbs. in very heavy-haul applications. Maintenance protocols must account for weight when assessing safety margins with increased inspection frequency and diagnostic monitoring.

Wheel-end maintenance on battery-electric and fuel cell vehicle requires enhanced protocols that account for increased component stresses from BEV truck operation. Inspection standards should include the need to document initial torque application using calibrated torque tools, confirm that fastener elongation remains within manufacturer tolerance specifications, and establish baseline hub temperatures using a non-contact infrared thermal camera for future comparison. This documentation creates an auditable maintenance record that supports both operational reliability and legal defensibility.

Schedules for zero-emission vehicles should include a commissioning inspection, a first service interval at 3,000 to 5,000 miles — when fastener seating occurs — and quarterly verifications thereafter. The torque application pattern matters: in stud-piloted systems, a star pattern ensures even load distribution, whereas hub-piloted designs require sequential pattern application per the manufacturer's specifications. Where available, integrated wheel-end monitoring technologies, such as SKF Insight systems, provide continuous monitoring via Bluetooth integration.

Where available, integrated wheel-end monitoring technologies provide continuous monitoring capability. As shown in Figure 70, modern systems, such as SKF Insight, integrate via Bluetooth to generate time-stamped data records that support predictive maintenance strategies and provide documentary evidence of heat and vibration.

---

## 11.2 Tires

Unlike RNG vehicles, which alter their wear patterns with tank weight, battery-electric vehicles fundamentally alter tire loading characteristics through both mass increase and torque delivery patterns. The additional 8,000 to 12,000 lbs. of battery mass, combined with instantaneous electric motor torque, increases tire contact patch stress by 30% to 50% during acceleration events. This substantially exceeds conventional diesel loading patterns, necessitating specialized tire construction.

Michelin developed high load (HL) tire technology specifically to address commercial BEV requirements. These tires feature reinforced construction with additional steel belt layers and higher-modulus bead compounds designed to support 27,000 to 30,000 lb. vehicle curb weights [221]. The HL specification, defined by standards from organizations such as ETRTO and TRA, increases load capacity by 9% to 12% at equivalent inflation pressures compared with conventional commercial tire specifications.

These reinforced tire constructions serve as prerequisites for maintaining operational availability and preserving retread casing yield above 70% through first service life on high-duty cycle routes approaching 70,000 to 80,000 lb. gross vehicle weights. Federal tire safety standards establish load rating and labeling requirements, but do not specifically address the accelerated wear mechanisms and loading patterns unique to BEV operations [215].

Brad Bayne, vice president of strategic initiatives for 4Gen, stated in Heavy Duty Trucking article, the following reasons for accelerated tire wear in BEVs [222].

Weight & Torque in Zero-Emission Trucks:
- BEVs are heavier than diesel, and additional torque is a significant factor.
- Torque varies by OEM, but our fleet ranges from 2,000 to 10,000 ft.-lbs., compared to about 1,600 ft.-lbs. on a typical diesel.

---

<!-- page: 98 -->
Messy Middle Powertrain Service & Maintenance

June 25, 2026 Page 98

- Because of this, BEVs can accelerate at the same rate, whether empty or fully loaded at 80,000 lbs. When drivers launch too aggressively from a stop, that extra torque directly increases tire wear.

Grade Performance & Powertrain Differences Between Diesel and Electric Trucks:
- On moderate grades (3% to 4%), BEVs and FCEVs can climb at twice the speed or more than an ICE truck. We do not encourage this behavior, but when it occurs, it causes additional wear.
- Most BEVs have just two gears. For example, Volvos shift from 1st to 2nd at around 20 mph, making their driveline performance markedly different from that of any diesel transmission.

BEV Duty Cycle Realities:
- BEVs are not yet a regional or long-haul replacement. They are primarily used in drayage and short-haul operations (100 to 250 miles), where constant stop-and-go driving is the norm.
- This operational profile magnifies both the torque advantage and the associated tire wear.

Research conducted by Oak Ridge National Laboratory highlights significant differences in rolling resistance between OEM tires and replacement tires, specifically in electric vehicle applications [223]. According to the findings, OEM tires exhibit rolling resistance coefficients that are approximately 23% lower than those found in replacement tires. See Figure 71. This notable reduction in rolling resistance is attributed to the advanced compound formulation and construction techniques employed during the development of OEM tires.

<!-- page: 99 -->
Messy Middle Powertrain Service & Maintenance

June 25, 2026 Page 99

Additionally, the Oak Ridge research documents the impact of tread wear on rolling resistance throughout the tire service life. As tires wear, their rolling resistance decreases; the study observed a roughly 20% reduction over a tire’s lifespan. More precisely, a 22% decrease in tread depth corresponds to an approximate 10% reduction in the rolling resistance coefficient. These findings underscore the importance of both tire selection and ongoing maintenance for optimizing energy efficiency in electric vehicle operations [223].

These findings create practical implications for fleet tire management. Unplanned tire replacements will increase fuel costs if the replacement tires have higher rolling resistance than the tires they replace. An increase in energy consumption from inappropriate tire selection directly impacts vehicle range. It may manifest in fleet telematics systems as apparent battery degradation when the actual cause is an increase in tire-induced rolling resistance. This diagnostic nuance requires systematic tire management to prevent misdiagnosing energy-consumption anomalies.

Tire particulate emissions scale proportionally with vehicle weight. Chemical by-products of tire wear, such as 6PPD-quinone, a tire rubber stabilizer degradation product, have been identified as toxins that affect sensitive fish species [224]. Regulatory initiatives and research from Emissions Analytics indicate potential regulatory and research oversight for non-exhaust particulate emissions from tire and brake wear [225]. While government regulations have not yet been enacted, fleets that practice elevated environmental stewardship establish tire documentation and maintenance protocols. These fleets also use tire pressure management systems (TPMS) or automatic tire inflation systems (ATIS) that can log data, including manufacturer identification, SKU numbers, lot codes, rotation histories with mileage and tread depth measurements, alignment data, and inflation pressure trends.

TPMS integrated with fleet telematics platforms have demonstrated an 85% reduction in tire-related roadside breakdowns, according to Continental Tires' ContiConnect Live field deployment data [226]. This addresses a critical fleet maintenance challenge, as NACFE research indicates that only 46% of tractor tires and 38% of trailer tires maintain target inflation pressure within ±5 psi during field inspections [227]. Contemporary TPMS implementations, such as Continental's ContiConnect Live, integrate with vehicle electronic control systems, providing real-time pressure, temperature, and mileage data to vehicle controllers and fleet telematics platforms [228]. Advanced system architecture uses cellular networks, Bluetooth, and RF wireless protocols to enable predictive maintenance scheduling based on pressure trends, temperature anomalies, and wear pattern analysis.

The operational value of ATIS and TPMS integration extends beyond breakdown prevention to include efficiency optimization through proper inflation pressure maintenance, extended tire service life through early detection of slow leaks and abnormal wear patterns, and improved safety through real-time monitoring of critical tire parameters during operations.

ATIS share the same primary objective as TPMS: eliminating tire underinflation to improve road safety, fuel efficiency, and tire longevity. However, ATIS offer distinct operational advantages. They automatically address tire leaks, reducing the severity of underinflation issues and providing maintenance teams with additional time to respond effectively.

One significant benefit of ATIS is the reduction in unnecessary alerts — ATIS generates 75% fewer alerts than TPMS, streamlining maintenance workflows and minimizing distractions. Furthermore, ATIS operate autonomously, requiring no human intervention to achieve its intended results, positioning it as a consistently superior solution for tire management in commercial fleet operations. [229].

Automated tire pressure management systems provide real-time inflation monitoring and adjustment. As shown in Figure 72, the Halo ATIS Automatic Tire Inflator System from Aperia maintains optimal tire pressure

The system continuously monitors tire pressure and automatically adjusts inflation pressure to manufacturer specifications, preventing under-inflation, which increases rolling resistance, and over-inflation, which accelerates wear. BEV tire maintenance requires rotation intervals to be shortened from conventional 6,000- to 8,000-mile cycles to 4,500- to 6,000-mile intervals, depending on duty cycle severity and instantaneous torque demands on driven axles. Wheel alignment inspection intervals traditionally set at 10,000 miles may need to be reduced to 7,500 to 8,000 miles due to asymmetric weight distribution from battery pack placement and its effects on suspension geometry under load. Daily Driver Vehicle Inspection Report protocols mandated under 49 CFR §396.11 should expand inspection criteria to include tread block cracking patterns and irregular wear characteristics specific to BEV instantaneous torque application [215].

## 11.3 Brakes

This section will look at brake system considerations for various powertrain platforms.

Natural Gas Platform Brake Systems

CNG fuel storage systems using elevated frame-rail or behind-cab tank mounting configurations add $50,000 to $70,000 to vehicle cost and extend the wheelbase by approximately 24” to accommodate 170+ diesel gallon equivalents (DGE) of storage capacity. The cylindrical Type 4 carbon fiber composite tanks are heavier and less flexible than conventional diesel saddle tanks, altering weight distribution and affecting vehicle dynamics during braking and acceleration. Tank mounting location and cargo weight distribution require engineering consideration to maintain optimal brake force distribution and vehicle stability.

---

<!-- page: 101 -->
Messy Middle Powertrain Service & Maintenance

June 25, 2026 Page 101

Regenerative Braking Systems for BEVs

In an article in Trucks, Parts, Service, (TPS) Andy Brown, product marketing manager of electromobility at Volvo, estimates that VNR Electric can recoup up to 15% of energy through regenerative braking when implemented correctly in optimal duty cycles [230]. Moreover, while regenerative braking is not an essential system in an electric vehicle — an electric truck can function solely on conventional brakes — the effort required to generate electricity also provides braking power by running the motor generators in inverse mode [230].

In the TPS article, Brown explained, "Because in a regen that's essentially what is happening; you are reversing the polarity inside the electric motor, so it acts as a generator and routes that energy [as electricity] back to the battery [230].” This generation mode substantially extends the service life of friction brake components by reducing the frequency and severity of friction brake engagement during regular deceleration events.

However, regenerative braking introduces failure modes not present in conventional pneumatic-only systems, including uneven friction material glazing from intermittent engagement, rotor surface corrosion from extended periods without thermal cycling, and control-system malfunctions when regenerative braking capacity is exceeded, necessitating emergency friction-brake application. Essentially sporadic and uneven brake use also can introduce brake issues [417]. Electric vehicles use regenerative braking to slow, which means friction brakes are used less frequently, allowing rust to build up on brake rotors and pads during extended periods of disuse [231].

Electro-Mechanical & Electro-Pneumatic Brake Architectures for BEVs

Regenerative braking is a key benefit of BEVs. Fleets train drivers on one-pedal driving, which is strong enough that in many cases, brakes are never used. Some drivers recover up to 20% of their daily energy use from regen alone. While there is not much formal data yet, many in the industry suspect regen also contributes to tire wear due to the way energy transfer and deceleration forces are applied [222].

Federal Motor Vehicle Safety Standard (FMVSS) No. 121 requires pneumatic foundation brake systems for commercial motor vehicles, creating adoption barriers for electromechanical brake (EMB) systems. Title 49 CFR § 393.42 mandates brakes act on all wheels of commercial motor vehicles, while 49 CFR § 393.49 requires power brake systems to operate via a single application valve that causes all service brakes to operate. These regulations were written specifically for pneumatic and hydraulic brake architectures and require regulatory exemptions before EMB systems can achieve widespread commercial deployment [232] [233].

FMCSA has recognized that brake-by-wire systems electronically activate all braking components and offer enhanced braking precision, with potential safety benefits through integration with antilock braking and electronic stability control systems. However, NHTSA's FMVSS 121 requirements for pneumatic foundation brakes prevents full adoption of EMB technology despite feasibility testing demonstrating viability [234].

Fuel Cell Electric Vehicle Brake System Architecture

Nikola Tre FCEV and other hydrogen Class 8 trucks are equipped with conventional pneumatic foundation brakes that meet current FMVSS 121 requirements, and integrate regenerative braking via the electric motor-generator. This dual-brake architecture ensures regulatory compliance under 49 CFR Part 393 while enabling energy recovery capabilities inherent to electric powertrains.

regulations. The electronic control enables optimized brake force distribution, improved anti-lock braking performance, and integration with vehicle stability control systems [236].

The EBS architecture enables more sophisticated regenerative-friction brake blending compared to purely pneumatic systems while maintaining compliance with existing federal safety regulations. The electronic control enables optimized brake force distribution, improved anti-lock braking performance, and integration with vehicle stability control systems.

Diesel and natural gas platforms retain pneumatic brake architecture that requires maintaining air compressors, air dryers, brake chambers, and brake shoes in accordance with conventional service schedules. Parts inventory requirements and technician skill sets remain aligned with established practices.

Battery electric and fuel-cell vehicles reduce dependence on pneumatic systems while introducing regenerative braking integration, electronic control system diagnostics, and software validation requirements [230]. This shift reduces friction material consumption — BEVs reduce brake dust emissions by 83% compared to conventional vehicles— but increases diagnostic complexity and technician training requirements [237] [418].

Service bay capabilities for BEV and FCEV brake systems must include diagnostic scan tools that can interrogate electronic brake control unit operating parameters, software update capability via over-the-air protocols or service-bay programming equipment, and technician training in electrical-mechanical integration troubleshooting methodologies [237]. The cost structure shifts from consumable parts expenditure toward diagnostic equipment investment and increased labor time for system validation and troubleshooting.

Fleet brake maintenance strategies must account for platform-specific requirements. Diesel and CNG platforms focus on friction material wear rates, air compressor reliability, and pneumatic system integrity [230]. BEV and FCEV platforms emphasize regenerative-system functionality validation, electronic-control-system software currency, and electromechanical component lifecycle management. Early-adopter fleets should anticipate

---

<!-- page: 104 -->
Messy Middle Powertrain Service & Maintenance

June 25, 2026 Page 104

reduced friction material costs, offset by increased diagnostic labor requirements and technician training investments, until regenerative brake technology and electromechanical architectures achieve regulatory standardization and widespread technician familiarity.

Technician certification programs for regenerative braking systems and advanced electric-vehicle diagnostics are available from multiple providers at varying cost levels. Bendix Commercial Vehicle Systems offers advanced technology training for $475 per attendee, while specialized online training in regenerative braking systems is available for $195 to $525 per course [238] [239].

Commercial-grade diagnostic platforms with BEV and FCEV system modules require a capital investment of $3,500 to $8,000 per service bay. These investments support the transition from consumable-focused maintenance to system-validation-focused maintenance, which characterizes zero-emission vehicle brake service [237]. The economic analysis indicates that friction material cost reductions of 30% to 40% are partially offset by diagnostic labor increases of 20% to 30% and by capital equipment requirements during the technology transition period spanning the first 2 to 3 years of fleet adoption of zero-emission vehicles.

Bendix Commercial Vehicle Systems highlighted its Global Scalable Brake Control (GSBC) compatibility with BEV powertrains and its ability to enhance driver-assistance safety systems at the 2024 Advanced Clean Transportation Expo in Las Vegas, Nevada [240] [241]. GSBC is Bendix's EBS technology. EBS, sometimes called brake-by-wire or electropneumatic braking system technology, uses electronic signals to control brake applications — as opposed to current antilock braking systems, where that control signal is pneumatic [242] [240].

The GSBC system enables vehicles equipped with regenerative braking to integrate regenerative and friction braking, optimizing energy recovery while maintaining the safety redundancy required under FMVSS 121 [243]. Seamless brake blending between regenerative and friction braking is essential for driver experience and system reliability. As illustrated in Figure 75, Bendix brake blending technology, for example, enables vehicles with regenerative braking to combine regenerative and friction braking seamlessly. The algorithm prioritizes regenerative energy recovery during initial deceleration phases when motor-generator capacity is available, then transitions to friction brake engagement as regenerative capacity is exceeded or thermal limits are approached [240].

## 12 INFRASTRUCTURE

Fleets that are running a mix of powertrains, have to consider changes that need to be made to the infrastructure of their depots to account for the unique needs of each of the powertrain solutions.

## 12.1 Technology Facility Design & Mixed-Fuel Operations

A fleet’s focus shifts from vehicles to facilities: when they begin infrastructure planning. This includes items such as air exchanges, detector placement, egress and standoff distances, spill containment, electrical clearances, interlocks, along with preventive maintenance schedules that keep it all operational. There is no single blueprint for a mixed-fuel depots because each energy source imposes a different hazard model and therefore requires a different set of architectural considerations and maintenance routines. Mihelic states, “When you start talking about infrastructure every technology has unique facility requirements. Safety codes, training, fire suppression, all different. There is no universal template for mixed-fuel operations [117].” This means that, going forward, the focus for all these fuel technologies will be on accurate, easily retrievable logs and data.

Each energy source requires its own infrastructure:
- Liquid fuels anchor one side of the design spectrum. Diesel and renewable diesel share familiar tankage, piping, and dispensing elements. The facility emphasis is secondary containment, overfill prevention, water intrusion control, and clean, inspectable suction lines.
- CNG/RNG facilities focus on compressed-gas awareness. Methane is lighter than air, so detection must be placed on the ceiling, ventilation must pull where the gas accumulates, and alarmed interlocks must drive dampers, fans, and equipment shutdown in ways technicians can test monthly. The bay ceiling geometry will affect the type of maintenance that can be performed. CNG/RNG are beginning to add complexity to the fuel infrastructure.
- Hydrogen operations require an advanced approach that builds upon the high-pressure safety standards of RNG but introduces strict thermal and protocol requirements. In medium- and heavy-duty dispensing applications, hydrogen must be precooled to -40°F (-40°C) and dispensed according to high-flow fueling profiles. Private hydrogen dispensing is not available at this time.
- While BEV charging introduces some additional complexity compared to CNG infrastructure, it does not reach a level that makes the installation of a charging system at a fleet facility unattainable. Design considerations for BEV charging shift the focus to electrical rooms, raceways, and network management within the facility.

The organizing virtue of diesel infrastructure is its maturity. Tanks, piping, dispensers, and meters are well understood, and the human systems around them, like driver habits, technician routines, and record-keeping, are embedded. RD is such a powerful lever in mixed-fuel depots because of its stability — it delivers immediate carbon-intensity reductions while preserving the physical plant and the workforce’s muscle memory.

Fleets running vehicles fueled by RD does not need to reinvent their regular diesel depot. However, there is for heightened discipline when the fleet starts using regular diesel, RD, and biodiesel in the same depot. While

---

<!-- page: 106 -->
Messy Middle Powertrain Service & Maintenance

June 25, 2026 Page 106

diesel and RD are drop-in alternatives, biofuel needs to be treated differently. In a mixed-fuel fleet, thorough documentation is essential because handling of biodiesel differs significantly from traditional diesel.

Biodiesel storage and handling require infrastructure modifications beyond standard diesel tank systems. As shown in Figure 76, biodiesel storage systems must accommodate coalescers, monitoring instrumentation, and drainage protocols specific to biofuel chemistry. An effective infrastructure maintenance program starts with standards-anchored procurement. Fleet should establish purchase orders procedures that explicitly require conformance to ASTM D975 for diesel and RD, and — if biodiesel will be used — ASTM D6751 for B100 and ASTM D7467 for blends in the B6 to B20 range. Each delivery requires a Certificate of Analysis (COA) bound into the delivery so that quality claims can be audited rather than guessed at.

> **Figure 76 — Biodiesel storage and handling system [83]**
> Type: Schematic / Technical Drawing
> Notes: Technical drawing showing a biodiesel storage system layout with numbered equipment callouts (1 through 59).

Material compatibility with the specific fuels must be maintained. In the tank farm, specify aluminum-oxide or glass-fiber coalescers must be sized to expected flow; and a corrective-action ladder must be defined that progresses from drain-down to biocide use to tank entry only when evidence demands it. On the shop floor, insist on ASTM-style sampling techniques and clear, easily repeatable seasonal standard operating procedures (SOPs). The Biodiesel Handling and Use Guide states, "Certain metals may affect the biodiesel by accelerating its oxidation process and creating fuel insolubles. Lead, tin, brass, bronze, and zinc significantly increase sediment formation in both B100 and B20. Galvanized metal and terne-coated sheet metal are not compatible with biodiesel at any blend level [83].”

CNG cylinders must comply with USDOT and International standards for visual inspection, hydrostatic testing, and mandatory retirement timelines. As shown in Figure 77, the inspection process focuses on cylinder condition, including damage assessment, corrosion evaluation, and verification of expiration dates. Storage cylinders must be visually inspected every 36 months or 36,000 miles, per NFPA 52. Cylinders have a finite service life of 15 to 20 years and must be retired at the end of life, with no requalification option [92].

Commissioning and routine PM should read as an evidence file. At commissioning, the evidence file should show that a technician verified meter accuracy against a traceable prover; documented suction line tightness and air-leak checks; confirmed overfill protection, interstitial monitoring, spill-containment function; and established baseline diesel particulate counts and water content from a representative draw. For monthly PMs, the technician must standardize the draw and test for free water; inspect vents, caps, and spill buckets; measure and log filter DP; check hose and nozzle seals; reconcile meters; and update the delivery-to-Certificate of Analysis (COA) chain for every drop. Quarterly, calibrate Automatic Tank Gauge (ATG) systems must be calibrated and tank bottoms sampled for microbial activity if water was detected; annually, must be verified per local code and re-proved at operating temperatures.

Hose material compatibility must be checked and dispenser elastomers rated for the fuel fleets consume, not the ones they are expecting to consume. When fleets shae dispensers between RD and biodiesel blends, a simple rule must be followed: label, train and log — because preventable cross-contamination remains the most common cause of avoidable downtime [83].

This disciplined approach to documentation transforms what might otherwise be disruptive about events such as audits or warranty defense procedures into routine practices. Well-organized records not only simplify

---

<!-- page: 108 -->
Messy Middle Powertrain Service & Maintenance

June 25, 2026 Page 108

compliance but also provide clear evidence for quality assurance, making regulatory inspection processes predictable and manageable.

Successful use of biodiesel in fleet operations requires attention to material compatibility, particularly for elastomeric seals, gaskets, and hoses. As shown in Figure 78, elastomer performance varies significantly when exposed to fresh, unoxidized methyl oleate (biodiesel). Materials such as natural rubber, butyl, and nitrile gaskets exhibit increased swelling or compatibility issues. In contrast, materials such as fluorosilicone, polypropylene, and polyurethane demonstrate superior resistance to swelling and compatibility issues. Fleets must verify that replacement fuel system components are explicitly approved for biodiesel service. Components rated only for petroleum diesel may fail prematurely in biodiesel systems. Procurement specifications should require material certifications confirming compatibility with ASTM D6751 (B100) or ASTM D7467 (B6-B20 blends).

Successful use of biodiesel in fleet operations also requires a proactive approach to cold-flow management. The cloud point and cold filter plugging point (CFPP) are critical factors that directly influence the risk of filter plugging during colder periods. These parameters are not merely technical details — they are central to maintaining fuel performance when temperatures drop.

To address seasonal variations, fleets must implement a blending plan that adjusts biodiesel concentrations throughout the year. For instance, using B20 during the summer months, shifting to B10 during the transitional season, and reducing to B5 in deep winter. Cloud-point margins based on local temperature minimums must be established and pre-winter filter changeover to eliminate any residual contamination before it solidifies and causes failures.

Effective water management is essential for biodiesel reliability. Technicians must conduct monthly checks for water accumulation at the bottom of tanks, perform semiannual sampling of tank bottoms, and carry out microbial tests whenever water is detected. These actions help prevent fuel degradation and equipment issues caused by water-related contamination.

During the first 60 to 90 days after a fuel transition — such as switching from petroleum diesel to renewable diesel or increasing biodiesel content — expect increased filter loading may occur as legacy deposits are released. Preparation includes stocking spare coalescers and filter elements and monitoring differential pressure to ensure filter replacements are based on actual evidence rather than a fixed schedule, with histories stored in a searchable repositor tied to asset IDs and dates. This targeted approach helps protect injectors, high-pressure pumps, and aftertreatment systems from unnecessary blame and maintenance costs.

When handling biodiesel blends in cold climates, fleets must follow the same protocols recommended for B100. Biodiesel blends must be stored at temperatures at least 5°F to 10°F (2.5°C to 5°C) above their cloud point, recognizing that cloud points vary among blends. Engineers need to design aboveground storage tanks and fuel lines to accommodate the cold-flow properties of biodiesel and local climate conditions. Ensure that pumps, lines, and dispensers are protected from cold and wind chill using appropriate heating and insulation equipment.

The same evidence base that protects fleets during audits and also shortens diagnosis. Proper maintenance schedules, advanced data collection, and differentiated fuel handling protocols, more than any single piece of hardware, are what turns the familiar diesel/RD plant into a modern, evidence-led infrastructure platform capable of shouldering the transition.

## 12.2 CNG /RNG

In an RNG maintenance and repair facility, classification determines the type of work permitted in each area, distinguishing minor garages (no fuel-system openings) from major ones (tank depressurization and fuel-system service). Each classification impacts required ventilation and detection measures, and accurate bay classification is critical for productivity and safety. The commissioning binder should include a clear summary detailing allowed activities, testing procedures for detection and exhaust, and accessible locations for isolation valves and venting.

Much of the maintenance for RNG facilities is condition-based, with preventive actions tied to compressor temperatures, vibration patterns, motor current, and dryer dew-point trends. Regular leak surveys and corrective actions become routine, reflecting a broader cultural shift in which compressed-gas safety and systematic leak detection are daily practices rather than rare occurrences.

---

<!-- page: 111 -->
Messy Middle Powertrain Service & Maintenance

June 25, 2026 Page 111

The Compressed Natural Gas Maintenance Facility Modification Handbook covers the five primary elements that must be considered when developing a CNG vehicle maintenance facility design to prevent the ignition of natural gas releases [84].

- Ventilation must provide sufficient air flow to reduce the concentration of the released gas, and at the same time, evacuate the gas from the structure.
- Paths of migration must be controlled to prevent the released gas from entering unprotected areas of the structure.
- Space heating must be designed in accordance with guidelines so that open flames or hot surfaces do not provide an ignition source.
- Electrical wiring and equipment must be installed in such a manner that they do not provide sources of ignition due to sparking. The equipment itself can be designed to be explosion-proof.
- Methane detection, control systems and alarms must provide defense against dangerous concentrations of natural gas by alerting personnel and turning off potential electrical ignition sources [84].

According to Recommended Practices for CNG Powered Refuse Trucks and the Supporting Facilities for Refueling and Maintenance, the base ventilation rate for all repair garages should be a minimum of 1 CFM/sq. ft. If mechanical ventilation is required, it must operate continuously except when it is either interlocked with a gas detection system or, for CNG applications only, electrically interlocked with the lighting circuit for CNG applications [244].

The report recommends keeping all documentation for maintenance performed for the life of the equipment. Fleets should set up recurring tasks with the proper frequencies. Conduct a monthly shop gas detection system compliance inspection. Conduct quarterly or semiannual calibration of the shop gas detection system for all sensors. Conduct an annual shop gas detection system calibration on all sensors, validating the functionality of the gas detection system [244].

Ari Silkey, general manager of Amazon Freight, stated, “We have been a long-term advocate of natural gas [CNG]. We installed fueling stations across our network, powered by RNG. It took investment, it took a long-term view to build scale of the technology [245].”

CNG fueling infrastructure offers two primary configurations to meet different operational requirements — time-fill and fast-fill stations. Time-fill stations fuel vehicles overnight, taking advantage of off-peak electricity rates and smaller, less expensive compression equipment, making them ideal for fleet operations with predictable return-to-base schedules. As shown in Figure 79, vehicles use the time-fill connection at the CNG refueling station in Oyster Bay, New York. Fast-fill stations, by contrast, rapidly fill vehicles using larger compressors and high-pressure gas storage systems, suitable for operations requiring quick turnaround.

As stated previously, natural gas (methane) is lighter than air and rises when released, creating specific hazard zones requiring precise facility design. As shown in Figure 80, upper ceiling areas should be considered natural gas collection zones because of the potential for gas to accumulate at the highest points of the facility. Detector placement must account for this physical reality: ceiling-mounted detectors paired with top-mounted exhaust ventilation ensure rapid leak identification. Base ventilation rate should be a minimum of 1 CFM per square foot, with monthly compliance inspections and mandatory quarterly or biannual sensor calibration [244].

Effective gas detection systems require rational interlock design escalating through predictable operational states. As shown in Figure 81, detection follows a tiered response. First, an alarm triggers notification; second, it forces mechanical ventilation to maximum; and third, it initiates equipment shutdown. This escalation allows operators to rehearse procedures and technicians to perform bump tests without facility downtime. Detection systems are an active safety infrastructure requiring maintenance rigor comparable to process equipment.

Once the detector is selected and he field-of-view range is known, fleet managers can begin the site layout to ensure complete coverage of the fueling area and your assets. As shown in the site layout in Figure 82, one lane has no overlap, while adding a fourth pole to the lower lane could provide complete coverage. Effective CNG parking facility design balances operational efficiency with safety through strategic vehicle spacing and integrated detection systems. The bow-tie parking layout positions are shown in Figure 82 with CNG refuse trucks in opposed rows with adequate separation distance to prevent cascade failures during thermal or pressure events. This staggered configuration isolates each vehicle within its own thermal and explosion-containment envelope, ensuring that activation of a pressure relief device (PRD) on one truck does not affect adjacent vehicles. Each truck remains a discrete hazard zone, managed independently.

At the equipment level, compressors, dryers, cascades, and priority-fill panels ensure uptime. Technicians should monitor compressor parameters, log dryer dew point, check panel sequencing, and document leak surveys with clear pass/fail actions. Parts inventory must reflect typical failures — such as filters, seals, valve kits, transmitters, dryer media, and relief devices — to avoid costly delays. Service contracts should specify response times, spare parts, and required records (dew-point logs, leak reports, trend data) to ensure clarity on costs and accountability. With these practices, stations operate reliably and transparently.

Natural gas system design relies on operating patterns and utility limit. Time-fill offers steady use with control, while fast-fill allows quick turns but strains compressors and dryers. A well-designed system matches capacity to fill needs, avoids overloading, and includes alerts for dew point or storage thresholds. Training should explain system logic, dew point effects, and relief functions, helping technicians spot problems early. In simple terms, successful maintenance depends on understanding the physics, closely monitoring systems, and validating performance through operational logs.

The pressure dew point indicates the temperature at which the water vapor begins to condense. In other words, the pressure dew point accurately describes the water content of compressed air. A low-pressure dew point always indicates a low water content of the compressed air. This is because warm, moist air has a higher moisture content than cold air, resulting in more water in the compressed air system.

Water and condensation are natural by-products of compressed air generation. The amount of water produced during compressed air generation depends on several factors — air temperature, humidity, compressor size, and the required pressure — which determine the water content of the compressed air. In any case, the water content of the compressed air must be kept as low as possible to avoid adverse effects on the compressed air system.

Atmospheric dew points and pressure dew points are different. The air's ability to carry water vapor depends on temperature. The warmer the air, the more water it can carry. It is therefore important to know that the atmospheric dew point must not be confused with the pressure dew point.

* Why does condensate form in the aftercooler of a compressor? In the compressor, the volume of the air being sucked in is reduced, and in the aftercooler, the compressed air temperature is lowered. Humidity is concentrated in small volumes and cannot be carried at low temperatures. As a result, condensate fails.
* What does the atmospheric dew point [°C] describe? The atmospheric dew point is the temperature to which air can be cooled without condensing the moisture it contains.
* What does the pressure dew point [°C] describe? The pressure dew point is the temperature at which compressed air can be cooled without condensing the moisture it contains.
* Why is the dew point so much lower than the pressure dew point? At the pressure dew point, the moisture is concentrated in a smaller volume [247].

The evidence-of-care trail turns good maintenance into defensible maintenance. Fleets need to keep a searchable ledger that ties every station asset and every vehicle cylinder to inspection dates, dew-point plots, vibration trends, leak-survey sheets, detector calibration certificates, and PRD setpoint verifications. Maintenance programs also should retain change-control records for any logic edits to compressors or priority panels and retain as-left parameters after work orders. Data is critical in all fueling and industrial settings; robust recordkeeping is critical to safety, compliance, and operational continuity, often requiring dedicated personnel to maintain accurate records.

Necessary routine inspections include the following.

* Conducted CNG inspections to verify the integrity and proper functioning of CNG components, such as the fuel storage cylinders, fuel lines, regulators, and other related equipment. These inspections are typically performed by trained professionals, such as certified technicians or inspectors, who have the knowledge and expertise in CNG systems.
* Examine various aspects of the CNG system to identify any potential hazards or issues that may compromise safety. Some of the key areas that are inspected include:
  * CNG cylinder inspection focuses on the condition of the CNG storage cylinders, checking for signs of damage, corrosion, or leaks. The cylinders are inspected to verify compliance with safety standards and to confirm they have not exceeded their recommended expiration date.
  * Fuel Lines and connections inspections verifies the condition of the fuel lines and connections, ensuring there are no leaks, loose fittings, or damaged parts that could lead to gas leaks.
  * Pressure regulation systems including the regulators and pressure relief devices, need to be checked to ensure they are functioning correctly and maintaining the appropriate pressure levels.
  * Ventilation and venting systems need to be inspected to ensure proper airflow and gas dispersion in case of a leak or emergency.
  * Safety device, such as emergency shut-off valves, pressure gauges, and leak detection systems, should be inspected to ensure they are in good working order.
  * Compliance with standards and regulations is part of the inspection process. These inspections verify compliance with local, regional, or national standards. This includes adherence to safety codes, installation guidelines, and maintenance requirements.

CNG inspections are crucial to ensure the safe operation of CNG vehicles and prevent potential accidents or incidents. Regular inspections, proper maintenance, and adherence to safety protocols help mitigate risks associated with CNG systems and ensure the continued use of CNG as a clean, sustainable fuel option [248].

Every combustible gas has an Upper Explosive Limit (UEL), the highest concentration at which it can ignite. As shown in Figure 83, the LEL-UEL range is the flammable region where ignition can occur. Above UEL, the mixture is too rich to burn due to insufficient oxygen. Natural gas UEL is approximately 15% by volume, propane is approximately 9.5%. Detection at 25% LEL provides early warning as gas concentrations rise into this dangerous zone. Understanding both LEL and UEL is essential: detection targets LEL (first threshold reached), while ventilation systems prevent concentrations from reaching any point within the flammable range.

## 12.3 Hydrogen Station Equipment Layer

The infancy, complexity, and the extreme care required to maintain a hydrogen fueling facility make it unlikely that most fleets will have their own fill station in the early days of hydrogen as a heavy-duty truck fuel. Most current medium- and heavy-duty truck deployments and demonstrations rely on light-duty (LD) fueling infrastructure, codes, and standards, while new, revised faster-flow concepts are introduced, evaluated, and implemented. Using LD-type infrastructure for medium- and heavy-duty trucks leads to slow or partial fueling, strains the infrastructure, and results in poor reliability and performance. This poor performance can disappoint and delay fleet deployments, hindering the potential adoption [250].

However, partnerships are being forged to create medium- and heavy-duty fueling options at freight corridors and ports, like the hydrogen fueling station at the Port of Oakland in Northern California.

In May 2024, at the FirstElement Hydrogen Fueling station in West Oakland, the Center for Transportation and the Environment (CTE) celebrated the official launch of NorCAL ZERO, a groundbreaking project bringing zero-emission freight transportation to the Bay Area and Central Valley. The project is a $53 million collaborative effort to deploy 30 units of Hyundai Motor Company's Class 8 XCIENT Fuel Cell electric trucks, the world’s first mass-produced, heavy-duty trucks powered by hydrogen [251].

Hydrogen fueling stations comprise interconnected subsystems that work together to ensure safe and efficient operation. These include bulk storage, multi-stage compression or cryo-pumping, high-pressure buffer storage, precooling trains, dispensers, and the safety-instrumented and supervisory control systems that manage and monitor the entire facility.

Each subsystem requires a specific PM strategy supported by measurable indicators. For example, valve-train inspection intervals should be tracked by both hours of operation and cycle counts. The performance of dryers or cold boxes should be monitored using dew point measurements or temperature changes (ΔT). Buffer-bank relief verification must be documented with both the setpoint and the verification date. Dispenser flowmeters and pressure transducers require calibration against traceable standards. At the same time, safety-instrumented systems (SIS) must undergo proof tests, with results mapped to a cause-and-effect table and recorded as clear pass or fail outcomes.

Maintenance logs must be kept for everything and organized with audit-resilient rigor. This ensures transparency and accountability, allowing maintenance teams and executives to monitor station health in real time. Maintenance effectiveness relies on visibility into these metrics; issues cannot be addressed if they are not visible or tracked.

Technicians should organize spare parts based on the station’s failure history. Standard components such as nozzle seal kits, heat exchanger fans and filters, temperature probes, transducers, valve pack cartridges, and dryer media must be readily available. By measuring, anticipating, and repairing typical degradations within the operational dwell window, technicians can minimize downtime and maintain consistent station performance.

High-performance hydrogen trucks requiring on-board storage at 70 MPa (10,150 psi) demand ultra-high-pressure dispenser systems. As shown in Figure 84, H70 trucks require hydrogen stations that dispense hydrogen at extreme pressure directly from high-pressure buffer storage. Gas-based systems employ multi-stage compressors and high-pressure accumulators, while cryo-based systems use liquid hydrogen vaporizers and high-pressure boosters. Station maintenance includes compressor vibration monitoring, booster seal integrity verification, and accumulator safety-relief checks.

This control architecture ensures vehicles receive hydrogen at rated specifications while protecting equipment from overpressure and thermal cycling damage.
