Robust processes complement safety culture. For hydrogen, the evidence-of-care trail should contain the detector calibration certificates; ventilation verification reports with measured airflow and interlock timing; and a safety instrumented system (SIS).

SIS is a dedicated control system designed to automatically prevent or mitigate hazardous events by taking the process to a safe state when predetermined conditions are violated and proof-test records mapped to the cause-and-effect matrix; dispenser trace libraries indexed by date, ambient condition, and vehicle class; metrology certificates for flow and pressure standards; and as-left parameters for any control change with an operator signature.

It is tempting to treat logs as a bureaucratic burden until the first contested claim arrives; hydrogen erases that temptation by making the logbook the station’s most valuable component after the precooler. The Hydrogen Safety Best Practices Guide and the NFPA Hydrogen Technologies Code have additional information [252] [253].

Safety culture in hydrogen maintenance facilities depends on clear visual communication systems that inform technicians, managers, and emergency responders of system status and required actions. As shown in Figure 88, proper signage for a hydrogen maintenance bay includes an alarm-condition matrix that identifies specific sensor triggers, corresponding actions, and required responses, paired with a visual beacon system that displays real-time facility status via color-coded lights. The alarm condition’s matrix documents what triggers each alarm state — hydrogen detection system faults, exhausted fans, curtain position errors, dew point exceedances, and system failures — along with specific actions required of technicians. A red light indicates facility shutdown with no entry permitted, red-flashing lights indicate alert condition requiring immediate investigation, a yellow light indicates maintenance-only condition with limited operations, and a green light indicates regular safe operation.

Visual beacon indicators mounted at facility entry points and key workstations ensure that all personnel immediately understand the operational status without requiring communication. Emergency response procedures, drilled to become second nature, establish clear escalation paths: an initial alarm activates notifications, a continued alarm forces ventilation to maximum, and a persistent fault triggers equipment shutdown. This integrated signage and beacon system transforms hydrogen maintenance from an unfamiliar hazard into a well-managed process, enabling technicians to see the variables, test them safely, and provide documented evidence of each action taken.

## 12.4 BEV Infrastructure

Reliable electrical and digital systems are essential to battery-electric vehicle charging infrastructure to meet National Electric Vehicle Infrastructure’s (NEVI) requirement that each charging port achieve an average annual uptime greater than 97 percent [215] [252]. Emerging reliability analyses show that software and communication failures, including loss of data connectivity to the back office, are a major contributor to charger downtime, which shifts maintenance priorities toward digital health monitoring as well as traditional electrical inspections [255] [256]. Effective deployments treat charging facilities as integrated systems, applying NEC Article 625 requirements for EV supply equipment and using OCPP-based monitoring and error-code reporting to detect and resolve data communication and backend failures quickly, with many operators targeting high backend availability and redundant connectivity to support dependable, code-compliant operations [257]. Site preparation lead times often can exceed vehicle delivery by 12 to 24 months. Utility interconnection may require 18 to 36 months from application to energization, with transformer procurement alone consuming 12 to

June 25, 2026 Page 121

---

<!-- page: 122 -->

Messy Middle Powertrain Service & Maintenance

24 months. Fleets should plan to initiate utility discussions 24 to 36 months before planned deployment to avoid stranded assets and missed emissions compliance targets.

When embarking on the journey into any new technology, it is essential to have vetted sources of information.

Fleet executives deploying BEV charging infrastructure face a critical procurement decision: building systems from component specifications or selecting pre-vetted, integration-tested hardware from qualified suppliers. The Electric Power Research Institute (EPRI) maintains a Vetted Product List (VPL) accessible through Southern California Edison (SCE) and other utility partners, which documents equipment models that have passed interoperability testing and demonstrate compliance with OCPP standards, UL certifications, and NEC Article 625 requirements. As shown in Figure 89, the EPRI VPL enables fleet procurement teams to filter by charger type (Level 2 AC, DC fast charge), power rating, connector standards (CCS1, CHAdeMO), and geographic deployment constraints. Each listed equipment model includes test validation dates, firmware versions, and known integration gaps with specific vehicle or backend platforms [258].

### 12.4.1 Infrastructure Planning

Transformer lead times have reached 12 to 24 months as of November 2025 — a 400% increase from pre-2022 baselines. This bottleneck stems from concentrated production of grain-oriented electrical steel (GOES), with Cleveland-Cliffs operating the sole US GOES mill [259]. Distribution transformer lead times, according to draft CalETC comments, now exceed EVSE equipment procurement (8 to 12 weeks) and switchgear availability (6 to 12 weeks) by an order of magnitude [260].

Except for the statutes and regulations cited, the Electric Vehicle Charging Analytics and Reporting Tool (EV-Chart) Data Format and Preparation Guidance Version 6.0. does not have the force and effect of law. They guidelines are not meant to be binding in any way. The guidelines suggest that the formal capacity assessment should quantify existing service capacity measured in kilovolt-amperes, require transformer upgrades with

June 25, 2026 Page 122

---

<!-- page: 123 -->

Messy Middle Powertrain Service & Maintenance

procurement timelines and cost allocation, provide switchgear and protection coordination requirements, provide a utility construction timeline with contractually binding milestone dates, and feature cost-sharing structures between fleet capital expenditure and utility make-ready programs [262].

Utility make-ready programs can reduce fleet capital expenditure by 30% to 50% through ratepayer-funded infrastructure investments. According to an article in Automotive Fleet, “Utility-side make-ready covers all the necessary system upgrades in front of the meter, such as transformer and distribution line upgrades, while customer-side make-ready encompasses costs for equipment generally owned by the fleet or charging services provider, such as panel, conduit, and charger installations [262].” California's Clean Energy Charging Equipment Make-Ready program funds utility-side infrastructure including service drops, transformer installations, and primary distribution upgrades [263]. However, these programs impose operational constraints: a minimum 97% uptime requirement that matches NEVI standards. These third-party access provisions convert private depots into semi-public facilities and impose usage-reporting obligations that create data-management overhead.

Battery energy storage systems (BESS) reduce required utility service capacity by 30% to 50% through load shifting and demand management, thereby compressing interconnection timelines from 24 months to 6 to 12 months by eliminating distribution system upgrade requirements [259]. BESS capital costs range from $400 to $600 per usable kilowatt-hour with additional maintenance complexity and NFPA 855 fire safety requirements [264]. Economic viability requires demand charges of $15 to $20 per kilowatt and time-of-use rate differentials exceeding 3:1 between peak and off-peak periods.

As shown in Figure 90, the BESS site layout must comply with NEC Article 625, including working clearance of at least 3’ in front of EVSE equipment, dedicated branch circuits for EVSE loads, continuous load derating to 125% of the EVSE continuous current rating, and ground-fault protection via GFCI for personnel protection or EGFP for facility-scale installations [265].

The 125% continuous load derating requirement applies to all conductors and overcurrent protective devices supplying EVSE loads. For a fleet depot with ten 150 kW DC fast chargers: base load equals 1,500 kW, continuous derating requires 1,500 kW × 1.25 = 1,875 kW equivalent service requirement, and transformer capacity needed is 2,000-2,250 kVA, allowing 6% to 7% growth margin [266] [267]. This requirement is frequently overlooked during initial electrical design, resulting in undersized service-entrance capacity that precludes future expansion without an expensive transformer replacement.

Physical protection from vehicle impact prevents 15% to 20% of charger downtime events. Bollards positioned 4' to 6' from EVSE enclosures must withstand 10,000-lb. impact forces per ASTM F3016 crash testing standards. Site lighting requires a minimum of 10 footcandles of horizontal illumination at 3' above grade, with a uniformity ratio not exceeding 4:1 for safety and operational visibility [261].

Mechanical, electrical, plumbing (MEP) design fees typically range from 8% to 12% of construction cost for standardized commercial facilities, with complex fleet depot projects commanding premium rates due to coordination requirements across multiple EVSE units and specialized electrical service sizing. Engineering design and permitting for iterative AHJ submittals, rework, and design verification activities account for 10% to 15% of electrical construction cost. Cost estimates are shown in Figure 91. Safe clearances are required as illustrated in Figure 92.

Electrical service sizing requires rigorous load analysis that accounts for simultaneous charging demand and duty-cycle effects. Conservative depot charging practice assumes 100% simultaneity during overnight windows when all vehicles return concurrently [269]. The 125% continuous-load derating requirement applies to all conductors and to overcurrent protection supplying EVSE loads, effectively increasing the service requirement from 100 A to 125 A.

When analyzing voltage drop, the technician must ensure that the terminal voltage at the EVSE remains within the equipment's operating tolerances. NEC recommendations limit voltage drops to 3% on feeder conductors and 5% total from service entrance to EVSE terminals [266]. Conductor sizing must simultaneously satisfy ampacity requirements under continuous-load conditions and voltage-drop constraints under maximum-load conditions.

Protection of coordination requires the technician to perform a selective coordination analysis, in order to demonstrate that only faulted circuits open during overcurrent or ground-fault conditions. Time-current curve analysis ensures adequate separation between upstream and downstream devices. Ground-fault protection at a 5-milliampere threshold provides personnel protection for Level 2 installations. Equipment ground-fault protection at higher thresholds prevents equipment damage while avoiding nuisance trips from capacitive charging currents.

Short-circuit analysis allows the technician to calculate available fault current at each distribution point to verify that protective devices can safely interrupt prospective fault currents. Arc flash hazard analysis establishes appropriate PPE requirements for maintenance activities on energized equipment [270].

## 12.4.2 Conductor Selection & Cable Management

Copper conductors remain preferred despite higher material costs because superior conductivity reduces voltage drop, and smaller conductor sizes simplify installation. Insulation temperature rating governs ampacity under continuous load, with 167°F rated (75°C) THHN/THWN-2 representing the minimum acceptable standard for EVSE applications.

Cable management for charging cables impacts the connector service life. Cable strain relief at EVSE terminations prevents conductor pullout under load. Retractable cable management systems from aftermarket suppliers, including Helix Linear Technologies, maintain cable organization. Cable replacement costs range from $2,000 to $5,000 per assembly for high-power DCFC applications.

Depot designs should prioritize dual CCS1/J3400 (NACS) capability to future-proof against the growing adoption of the J3400 connector, while planning for Megawatt Charging System (MCS) hardware as the heavy-duty standard is finalized. As summarized by the U.S. Joint Office of Energy and Transportation, CCS1 (SAE J1772 Combo 1) remains the required fast-charging connector on federally funded NEVI corridors while the J3400/NACS connector is being standardized for broad use, with MCS emerging as the high-power standard for heavy-duty vehicles.

Figure 93 illustrates these connector types — CCS1, J3400/NACS, and MCS — as the primary fast-charging interfaces for North American medium- and heavy-duty applications [271].

Primary connector standards dominate North American heavy-duty charging: CCS1 (SAE CCS1, five-pin bottom configuration for DC fast charging), CHAdeMO (circular nine-pin legacy standard with diminishing prevalence), and J3400 (NACS, still in development by CharIN for interoperability).

> **Figure 93 — Types of charging connectors [271]**
> Type: Photographs of connectors
> Axes: None
> Series: CCS1, CHAdeMO, J3400
> Values: CCS1 (SAE CCS1), CHAdeMO, J3400 (in development from NACS)
> Notes: Photographs showing connector pin configurations.

SAE J3271 Megawatt Charging System (MCS) standard, issued in March 2025, establishes protocols for 3.75 MW charging that enable ultra-rapid transit application charge times matching diesel refueling durations. Figure 94 shows the MCS connector schematic — a triangular pin arrangement with extended protective sheaths designed for 1,250 VDC at 3,000 A, utilizing liquid-cooled conductors (Configuration 3) and CAN FD/XL communication over twisted-pair wiring rather than CCS power-line communication. The connector evolved through three CharIN task group iterations (V1, V2, V3.2), with NREL validation testing up to 3,000 A under elevated ambient conditions. MCS architecture requires electrical service capacity planning accommodating 3.75 MW per dispenser, representing 5 to 10 times the current DCFC infrastructure capability.

Network operators reporting MCS roadmap deployments in the 2027 to 2028 timeframe suggest retrofit pathways through modular cabinet design and upgraded cooling infrastructure. Fleets should coordinate with utility partners during interconnection planning to reserve service capacity allocations that enable future MCS installation without a complete electrical system replacement.

CCS1 (SAE J1772) delivers a maximum of 350 kW at 1,000 VDC with 500 A continuous current, charging a 300 kWh battery to 80% in 69 minutes, and providing 350 kWh energy delivered per hour with 175 mi/hr range addition at 2 kWh/mi efficiency. CHAdeMO 3.0 increases the maximum charging rate to 400 kW with identical voltage and current, achieving a 60-minute charge time for a 300 kWh battery and a 400 kWh per hour delivery rate, supporting an additional 200 mi/hr range, representing an incremental improvement valued primarily for legacy vehicle compatibility.

NACS (J3400), an emerging North American standard, delivers 500 kW at a constant 1,000 VDC but increases current to 1,000 A, reducing the 300 kWh charge time to 48 minutes and enabling 500 kWh per hour delivery, adding 250 mi/hr of range. SAE J3271 Megawatt Charging System (MCS) represents the architectural leap forward: 3,750 kW maximum power at 1,260 VDC with 3,000 A current enables 6.4-minute charge time for 300 kWh battery, 11-minute charging for 500 kWh battery, and 17-minute charging for 800 kWh battery, delivering 3,750 kWh per hour and supporting 1,875 mi/hr range addition at equivalent efficiency.

Fleet procurement strategy must balance current market vehicle availability, weighted toward CCS1/NACS, against future MCS deployment timelines. While immediate North American deployments remain CCS1-centric, NACS market-share expansion among OEMs and announced MCS roadmaps for 2027 to 2028 operator networks suggest a long-term network architecture favoring NACS + MCS platforms. Infrastructure investments made today should establish technology migration pathways rather than single-standard lock-in scenarios.

Level 2 AC chargers and DC fast chargers operate under fundamentally different duty cycles, thermal loads, and maintenance requirements, requiring distinct operational protocols and spare management strategies. The performance gap reflects architectural complexity. Level 2 units deliver fixed power via rectified grid current with minimal active thermal management, whereas DCFC systems employ liquid cooling, active isolation monitoring, multiple power-conversion stages, and complex firmware that manages kW-to-MW power transitions.

Electrical safety testing anchors the annual protocol for all charger types, with ground continuity not exceeding 0.1 Ω for Class I equipment and insulation resistance testing requiring a minimum of 1 MΩ between conductors and ground. Component replacement schedules are tied directly to observed wear rather than arbitrary time intervals. Connector assemblies with contact scoring, housing cracks, or latch mechanism failures require

June 25, 2026 Page 128

---

<!-- page: 129 -->

Messy Middle Powertrain Service & Maintenance

immediate replacement. High-utilization depot installations typically cycle connector hardware every 12 to 24 months.

Uptime percentage, measured as the fraction of time chargers remain operational and available, establishes compliance reporting benchmarks, with mean time between failures (MTBF) for DCFC installations typically ranging from 2,000 to 4,000 hours under commercial fleet conditions. Equipment refresh cycles normally span 10 to 15 years for commercial EVSE installations, after which obsolescence or technological advancement justifies replacement.

Insulation resistance testing allows technicians to verify conductor insulation integrity, with a minimum acceptable value of 1 MΩ for new installations. Continuity testing allows technicians to confirm the integrity of the grounding conductor in accordance with NEC Article 250. Ground-fault protection testing injects controlled fault current to verify GFCI or EGFP devices trip within specified thresholds. Load testing applies actual charging loads to verify voltage regulation under maximum demand conditions [272].

Network connectivity testing allows technicians to verify that primary and backup transport paths achieve the required signal strength (RSSI ≥ -85 dBm), latency (< 100 ms), and throughput. OCPP handshaking tests confirm that EVSE successfully initiates a connection to the broker and maintains a persistent connection. Session authorization testing help technicians validate RFID card or mobile app credentials to successfully authorize charging sessions, with transaction latency below 2 seconds.

Weekly visual inspections form the foundation of effective station management, catching noticeable connector wear, cable damage, and environmental hazards before they escalate to failures (AMPPAL). These rapid walkthroughs verify that cables remain properly stowed; contact pins show no carbon tracking or corrosion, and user-facing components — including payment terminals and display screens — function correctly.

Professional maintenance intervals scale with charger complexity and site exposure. Level 2 AC units require comprehensive annual inspections covering pilot signal integrity verification, GFCI testing per OEM protocols, terminal torque checks, and firmware updates (AMPPAL). DC fast chargers require more frequent attention — quarterly or semiannual service cycles that include coolant system inspection, filter cleaning or replacement, isolation monitor validation, high-voltage interlock continuity confirmation, and busbar torque verification per manufacturer specifications (AMPPAL).

Figure 96 illustrates maintenance interval and MTBF differentials: Level 2 AC units require annual inspections (pilot signal integrity, GFCI testing, terminal torque) with 8,000 to 12,000-hour MTBF; DCFC units demand quarterly service cycles (coolant inspection, filter replacement, busbar torque verification) with 2,000 to 4,000-hour MTBF under commercial fleet duty. Connector assemblies exhibiting contact scoring, housing cracks, or latch mechanism failures require immediate replacement; high-utilization depot installations typically cycle connector hardware every 12 to 24 months.

## 13 MAINTENANCE STYLES

This section provides an overview of four maintenance philosophies as described by the Department of Defense (DoD) and NASA: reactive (run-to-failure) maintenance, preventive maintenance, predictive or condition-based maintenance (CBM) or CBM-plus maintenance, and prescriptive or reliability-centered maintenance [275] [47]. These frameworks provide authoritative manuals on the maintenance and management of high-tech assets. The DoD manual is a high-level overview of maintenance procedures, while the NASA manual can guide the fleet maintenance professional on the nuances of data collection. Those fleets using the prescriptive system potentially can analyze data from individual bearing vibration, temperature, and ultrasonic sensors to monitor turbocharger or wheel health for a particular asset or a fleet, for example.

To begin, excellent data management practices are required for these advanced maintenance frameworks to deliver results. Everyone involved in fleet maintenance should buy-in to acquiring complete, accurate data, service writing, and communication, or the chain of maintenance improvement will break.

## 13.1 Reactive Maintenance

Reactive maintenance, also called corrective or run-to-failure maintenance, is the most traditional approach. ISO 14224 defines corrective maintenance as maintenance carried out after fault detection to restore the system to its original state [274]. In commercial trucking, this approach dominated for decades because failures often could be tolerated until scheduled shop time or roadside breakdown. Figure 97 shows the reactive maintenance decision flow summarized in these steps: fault detection, criticality assessment (mission-critical vs. non-critical), safety impact evaluation, then repair urgency determination.

> Type: Boxed text / Diagram summary
> Axes: None
> Series: Basic philosophy, Cost
> Values: Basic philosophy: Allow machinery to run to failure. Repair or replace damaged equipment when obvious problems occur. Cost: $18/hp/yr
> Notes: Description states: This maintenance philosophy allows machinery to run to failure, providing for the repair or replacement of damaged equipment only when obvious problems occur. Studies have shown that the costs to operate in this fashion are about $18 per horsepower (hp) per year. The advantages of this approach are that it works well if equipment shutdowns do not affect production and if labor and material costs do not matter.

BEVs highlight the limitations of purely reactive strategies. High-voltage interlock loop (HVIL) connector issues, inverter coolant leaks, or traction motor bearing problems can disable vehicles, with severity ranging from simple connector reseating to complete powertrain replacement. OEMs increasingly control access to repairs through proprietary diagnostic tools and service authorization protocols Reactive maintenance remains unavoidable, but electrified fleets increasingly depends on access to OEM-controlled digital platforms and specialized high-voltage service equipment.

## 13.2 Preventive Maintenance

Preventive or time-based maintenance follows the recommended maintenance schedule and is traditional maintenance using a paper manual. This maintenance philosophy follows the manufacturer's recommended schedules. Cummins preventive maintenance contracts for diesel engines include general inspection, lubrication service, cooling system service, fuel system service, battery testing, and regular engine exercise. For renewable diesel, portable diesel analyzers combine particle counting, water detection, and viscosity measurement for in-field diagnostics. Figure 98 presents the preventive maintenance schedule template, comparing service intervals across diesel (oil/filter at 25,000 miles; coolant at 12,000 miles), renewable diesel (fuel analysis at 10,000 miles; DPF cleaning inspection at 20,000 miles), and BEV platforms (coolant service at 12 months; insulation resistance test every six months; connector torque verification annually). The advantage is predictable scheduling and resource allocation, though it may result in unnecessary servicing of components with remaining useful life.

When it is done correctly, studies have shown the costs of operating in this fashion to be about $13 per hp per year. The advantages of this approach are that it works well for equipment that does not run continuously, and with personnel who have enough knowledge, skills, and time to perform the preventive maintenance work.

## 13.3 Condition-Based Maintenance

Condition-based maintenance (CBM) shifts maintenance timing from fixed intervals to evidence-based interventions triggered by system conditions. ISO 14224 defines CBM as maintenance performed according to condition as monitored by sensors or external measurement [274]. NASA and DoD Instruction 4151.22, 2012 frameworks embed CBM within Reliability-Centered Maintenance, using integrated sensors and fault-code recording [275].

Figure 99 illustrates the CBM sensor integration architecture: NOx sensors (upstream/downstream of SCR) → turbocharger speed/temperature/pressure sensors → aftertreatment outlet temperature sensors → diagnostic algorithms → maintenance decision logic. Advanced ICE platforms require integrating multiple sensors to monitor combustion efficiency, turbocharger health, and aftertreatment performance. These sensor streams provide real-time health indices that trigger maintenance actions before failure.

> **Figure 99 — Predictive maintenance [47]**
> Type: Boxed text / Diagram summary
> Axes: None
> Series: Basic philosophy, Cost
> Values: Basic philosophy: Schedule maintenance activities when mechanical or operational conditions warrant. Repair or replace damaged equipment before obvious problems occur. Cost: $9/hp/yr
> Notes: Description states: This philosophy consists of scheduling maintenance activities only if and when mechanical or operational conditions warrant-by periodically monitoring the machinery for excessive vibration, temperature and/or lubrication degradation, or by observing any other unhealthy trends that occur over time. When the condition gets to a predetermined unacceptable level, the equipment is shut down to repair or replace damaged components so as to prevent a more costly failure from occurring. In other words, "Don't fix what is not broke." Studies have shown that when it is done correctly, the costs to operate in this fashion are about $9 per hp per year. Advantages of this approach are that it works very well if personnel have adequate knowledge, skills, and time to perform the predictive maintenance work, and that it allows equipment repairs to be scheduled in an orderly fashion. It also provides some lead-time to purchase materials for the necessary repairs, reducing the need for a high parts inventory. Since maintenance work is only performed when it is needed, there is likely to be an increase in production capacity.

Advanced ICE platforms require integrating multiple sensors to monitor combustion efficiency, turbocharger health, and aftertreatment performance. NOx sensors upstream and downstream of SCR catalysts directly measure engine-out emissions and catalyst performance, supporting optimized urea dosing and regulatory compliance [276]. Turbocharger systems use embedded temperature, pressure, and speed sensors to calculate airflow and pressure ratios, enabling real-time health monitoring and optimized overhaul scheduling.

In BEVs, CBM applications include monitoring cell-voltage imbalance (10 to 15 mV threshold), pack insulation-resistance degradation, and inverter temperature thresholds that trigger shop actions before outright failure. Robust fleet monitoring systems — most notably Tesla's remote diagnostics platform — track battery state of health across thousands of vehicles. Figure 100 presents the battery state of health (SOH) evaluation framework: data collection (vehicle mileage, drive cycles, ambient temperature) → data cleansing and outlier removal → ampere-hour integration (Ah counting) → open-circuit voltage correction → cubic spline fitting of capacity decay curve → SOH label generation. Nature's multimodal framework demonstrates that this integration enables accurate SOH prediction across diverse driving conditions and user behaviors.

## 13.4 Prescriptive Maintenance

Prescriptive maintenance (Figure 103) represents the most advanced stage of fleet maintenance maturity, where disciplined logging and real-time asset visibility enable systems to both predict failures and prescribe specific actions [47]. In a typical prescriptive architecture, continuous sensor streams — for example, vibration, temperature, pressure, and fuel-quality signals — feed a feature-extraction and anomaly-detection layer that supports machine-learning models such as Long Short-Term Memory (LSTM) networks and Random Forest classifiers to estimate failure probabilities [281] [282].

Those probabilistic forecasts drive a maintenance recommendation engine that optimizes timing, parts, and labor and automatically generates work orders in the fleet’s maintenance system, closing the loop from raw sensor data to executed tasks [47]. Industry studies report that AI-driven predictive and prescriptive maintenance can reduce unplanned downtime on the order of 30% to 50% while lowering overall maintenance and spare-parts inventory costs through more accurate, risk-based planning [283].

## 14 COMMUNICATION ARCHITECTURE

Communication from vehicles is an essential element of the repair process.

## 14.1 Vehicle Communication Architecture

Various communication channels can tell technicians what is going on with the vehicle and its components. This can provide a state of vehicle health and indicate where problems are occurring.

### 14.1.1 CAN

The Controller Area Network (CAN) provides foundational digital infrastructure for modern heavy-duty trucks, linking control modules for engines, brakes, transmissions, and telematics. The SAE J1939 standard establishes a universal communication protocol that ensures interoperability across OEM fleets. Figure 104 shows the J1939 backbone and stub architecture: a linear backbone bus (typically 2-wire CAN at 250 kbaud or 500 kbaud) with 120-ohm termination resistors at both ends, and stub branches connecting ECUs (Engine Control Module, Transmission Control Module, Body Controller, Telematics Gateway). Messages are coded with priority identifiers and CAN IDs, allowing multiple modules to transmit simultaneously without collision. Modern trucks are increasingly adopting 500 kbaud data rates for faster diagnostic communication.

June 25, 2026 Page 137

---

<!-- page: 138 -->

Messy Middle Powertrain Service & Maintenance

> Type: Network architecture diagram
> Axes: None

The ISO 11898 CAN standard and SAE J1939 establish universal communication protocols, ensuring interoperability across OEM fleets. Updates such as SAE J1939-22 CAN FD support high-bandwidth data exchange, whereas SAE J1939-91C mandates secure messaging to mitigate cyber threats. Industry innovations such as New Eagle’s Raptor Platform integrate legacy CAN 2.0 with CAN FD, bridging older truck architectures with next-gen systems [284]. For fleets, this means that diagnostic reliability, predictive maintenance, and secure OTA updates all depend on CAN integrity. For more on CAN, see Appendix C.

As a result of regulatory adoption of Unified Diagnostic Services (UDS), SAE J1979 has become a multi-part standard: J1979-2 for combustion engines and J1979-3 for zero-emission vehicles. Only one protocol is allowed per vehicle — either J1979-2 or J1979-3. Figure 105, SAE J1979-3 ZEV protocol mapping, maps the J1979-3 ZEV protocol coverage: traction drive motors and inverters, energy storage systems (battery pack, cell balancing, thermal management), high-voltage charging systems (onboard chargers, power distribution units), and thermal control systems supporting propulsion (coolant pumps, temperature sensors). The ZEV scope encompasses not only powertrain electronics but all systems whose failures impact recuperation and charging functionality.

Wired technology requires time to sense and transmit information. To enable a truck’s automatic braking system to react as quickly or better than a human, signal processing speeds require high speed digital protocols, such as automotive ethernet.

### 14.1.2 Beyond CAN

As fleets transition to software-defined vehicles (SDVs), the limitations of the CAN standard are becoming apparent. With a maximum data rate of 1 megabit per second (Mbps), CAN cannot efficiently support bandwidth-heavy features such as advanced driver assistance systems (ADAS), LiDAR, or real-time camera feeds. To address these limits, OEMs are adopting Automotive Ethernet, which delivers 10 gigabits per second (Gbps) and supports deterministic communication for safety-critical systems. Ethernet also allows zonal architectures, in which multiple ECUs are consolidated into centralized controllers, reducing wiring complexity and improving reliability. Research in SAE Technical Paper 2025-01-8138 confirms that Ethernet enables predictive maintenance, secure OTA updates, and higher levels of automation [285]. Legacy serial networks, such as SAE J1708/J1587, will remain in some vehicles for diagnostic purposes. At the same time, emerging protocols such as Controller Area Network Flexible Data Rate (CAN FD) and Vehicle-to-Everything (V2X) wireless communication will complement Ethernet backbones in hybrid architectures.

ISO 13400-4 specifies diagnostic communication over IP using an Ethernet-based high-speed data-link connector. According to Peter P. Zupka, “It is the same OBD connector you know, but we added new Ethernet pins—a four-wire Ethernet with Ethernet activation. 100BASE-TX is 100 Mbps compared to high-speed CAN at 1 Mbps on unshielded twisted pair” [425].

Cybersecurity is no longer optional in heavy-duty trucking as fleets shift to connected and software-defined architectures (SDAs). The ISO/SAE 21434 Road Vehicles — Cybersecurity Engineering standard defines lifecycle-wide processes, from design through decommissioning, for managing cybersecurity risks. The National Highway Traffic Safety Administration (NHTSA) Cybersecurity Best Practices recommend a layered defense approach that includes encryption, intrusion detection, and continuous monitoring of wired and wireless connections [286]. Vulnerabilities in SAE J1939, a protocol built on CAN, have been exploited to enable spoofing and denial-of-service (DoS) attacks [73]. To counter these risks, fleets are adopting intrusion detection systems (IDS), secure boot processes, and encrypted OTA updates to preserve that software integrity is preserved.

Collaborative efforts such as CALSTART’s 2025 Cybersecurity Working Group, led by Auburn University with support from the Federal Transit Administration (FTA), are piloting secure CAN routers and vehicular Wi-Fi firewalls [287]. On the shop floor, the Technology & Maintenance Council (TMC) Recommended Practices now include cybersecurity workflows that ensure technicians use authenticated sessions and validated tools [288]. For fleets, cybersecurity has become a daily operational requirement, just as crucial to uptime as brake inspections or oil changes.

## 14.2 Automated Driving Systems

Automated Driving Systems (ADS) are advancing rapidly under both regulatory and industry leadership. The FMCSA Automated Vehicles Comprehensive Plan and the 2023 ANPRM on ADS represent a significant policy shift, moving from requiring human drivers at all times to enabling SAE Levels 4 and 5 autonomy [289] [290]. At ACT Expo 2025, TRATON provided detailed lessons from Level 4 truck deployments in Europe and the US, particularly for freeway “exit-to-exit” operations [291]. These deployments rely on sensor-heavy platforms, often with five or more LiDARs such as the Hesai AT128, which delivers 1.53 million points per second [293]. The DIN/SAE Spec 91471 sets calibration requirements with 0.01° angular precision and millimeter-level accuracy

June 25, 2026 Page 139

---

<!-- page: 140 -->

Messy Middle Powertrain Service & Maintenance

[292]. Meanwhile, FMCSA’s Tech-Celerate Now program found that forward-collision warning systems can reduce crashes by 29%, underscoring ADS’s safety potential. Together, these advances define both a regulatory and technical pathway for autonomous freight [294].

ADAS and LiDAR technologies introduce new precision requirements for heavy-duty truck service. The DIN/SAE Spec 91471 defines standardized testing with 0.01° angular precision and millimeter-level distance accuracy, ensuring reliable calibration [295]. Placement choices — grille, windshield, or roof — each present tradeoffs in cooling, protection, and field of view, as noted by Innoviz Technologies [296]. The FMCSA Tech-Celerate Now program found that forward-collision prevention, lane-keeping, and blind-spot detection reduce crashes by up to 29%, highlighting their safety benefits [294]. Misaligned cameras are a critical risk: even a 0.6° deviation can delay braking by 60%, reinforcing the need for precision [297]. Tools such as the TEXA ADAS Truck Kit align with TMC calibration standards, helping technicians maintain sub-millimeter tolerances. In practice, ADAS calibration elevates technician duties from mechanical adjustments to digital precision engineering [298].

Presentations at ACT Expo 2025 highlighted how peripheral systems are reshaping trucking into a software-defined, electrified ecosystem. Cummins’ modular LFP battery packs deliver 104 kWh per pack at 840V with mix-and-match flexibility, validated by NREL’s HD field evaluations covering 5.6 million fleet miles [299] [300]. The ZF TraXon 2 Hybrid and BorgWarner iM-575 electrified driveline systems extend drivetrain efficiency and sustainability [301] [302]. Cybersecurity is embedded in new peripherals, such as New Eagle’s Raptor CAN platform, which bridges legacy and modern CAN and implements J1939-91C. Shops also are evolving with ETAS AI workshop assistants that provide real-time diagnostic guidance [303]. Autonomous-vehicle peripherals, such as the Daimler eCascadia sensor bar and Kalmar yard tractors, demonstrate the convergence of AI, electrification, and automation. These peripherals redefine uptime, safety, and the technician role.

## 15 PARTS

Parts performance is a significant factor in uptime. Part identification, sourcing, receiving, traceability, warranty management and core management should be treated as a controlled operating system. Where fleet risk is highest — serialized emissions hardware, safety items, high-pressure fuel components, and high-voltage components — this approach ensures that fleets are in compliance and that liability is controlled. That discipline aligns with the carrier's obligation to systematically inspect, repair, and maintain vehicles and with the requirement that added equipment not degrade safe operation [304] [305].

The parts function should be designed around three outcomes: correct part and revision the first time, predictable availability, defensible documentation for warranty, compliance, and audits.

## 15.1 ICE Parts

Advanced ICE vehicles remain central to fleet operations, and the parts program must support more extended durability and warranty periods for emissions-related systems. EPA’s heavy-duty standards rulemaking and the durability framework in eCFR—40 CFR §1036.104 — extend the useful-life expectations and reinforce that emissions-related components, calibrations, and supporting hardware must remain compliant and warrantable over a longer operating interval than that for which many legacy parts programs were designed [306]. The aftermarket consequence is simple: serialized aftertreatment modules and their supporting sensors/connectors should be treated as controlled items, with provenance captured at receipt and again at installation, so that warranty recovery and traceability are maintained throughout the asset’s service life (not reconstructed later).

June 25, 2026 Page 140

---

<!-- page: 141 -->

Messy Middle Powertrain Service & Maintenance

A fleet parts program for ICE vehicles should be designed to minimize downtime by eliminating wrong-part events and preventing uncontrolled substitutions that lead to repeat failures. The functional sourcing workflow is not complicated: configured and warranty-sensitive items default to the OEM channels. At the same time, approved aftermarket pathways support the high-velocity mechanical layer — but only in the categories where equivalence is stable, and verification is practical. OEM portals matter is not convenience; but for configuration control and revision accuracy for emissions-critical parts that are expensive to get wrong [307] [308] [309].

Vehicle Maintenance Reporting Standard (VRMS) integrates parts catalogs, warranty databases, technical service bulletins, and wiring diagrams into a unified platform that accelerates diagnostics and ensures accurate part identification for complex commercial trucks. Figure 106 shows the Daimler Truck Excelerator parts dashboard interface with VMRS lookup functionality, allowing technicians to enter VIN (Vehicle Identification Number) and instantly retrieve complete build specifications: engine model and serial number, transmission type, axle ratios, GVWR, emission certification, and as-built options.

For alternative fuel vehicles, VMRS proves especially critical because battery pack specifications (chemistry, capacity, voltage), hydrogen storage pressure (350 bar vs. 700 bar), and charging system hardware (AC Level 2 vs. DC fast charge capability) vary widely even within the same truck model, and installing incorrect replacement parts (wrong voltage battery module, incompatible charge controller) can cause catastrophic failures or void warranties. The system integrates with parts distributors (VIPAR, Fleetpride, HD Supply) to verify parts availability, cross-reference OEM part numbers to aftermarket equivalents, and flag superseded parts that have been replaced by updated designs addressing field failures.

The heavy-duty truck aftermarket provides cost-effective alternatives to OEM parts, with major distributors like VIPAR Heavy Duty, Fleetpride, and HD Supply offering cross-referenced components that meet or exceed original specifications at 20% to 40% lower prices.

Figure 107 presents an excerpt from VIPAR’s catalog showing aftermarket availability for alternative fuel vehicle components including battery disconnect switches ($150 to $300 vs. $400 to $600 OEM), high-voltage cable harnesses ($800 to $1,500 vs. $2,000 to $3,500 OEM), coolant pumps for battery thermal management ($300 to $500 vs. $700 to $1,200 OEM), and hydrogen pressure regulators ($1,200 to $2,000 vs. $3,000 to $4,500 OEM).

Critical considerations for aftermarket adoption include warranty implications — some OEMs void powertrain warranties if non-OEM parts are installed in high-voltage systems — certification requirements — SAE J2929 certification for hydrogen components ensures crashworthiness standards are met — and liability concerns — using uncertified parts in high-voltage systems creates potential electrocution hazards and fire risks.

Fleet policies typically restrict aftermarket parts in safety-critical systems (battery disconnects, hydrogen pressure vessels, high-voltage contactors) while allowing them in auxiliary systems (HVAC compressors, 12V accessories, lighting), balancing cost savings against risk exposure.

Fuel innovation alters parts demand curves, and fleets should measure these shifts rather than assume the provided numbers match their fleet’s profile. DOE’s Co-Optima work supports the operational concept that fuel properties can influence particulate formation and combustion behavior, which can move soot loading and aftertreatment service cadence, meaning your demand for DPF service hardware, gaskets, clamps, and certain sensing elements may shift over time [311]. CARB’s testing of low-emission diesel or renewable diesel similarly demonstrates that emissions outcomes can differ from those of petroleum diesel under controlled conditions, which is relevant to long-term stocking assumptions for aftertreatment service parts [79]. Fleets should adjust min/max only after it has observed a sustained change in its duty-cycle data.

Natural gas and dual-fuel ICE platforms introduce a second layer of aftermarket constraint: the fuel system Bill of Materials (BOM) is non-interchangeable and standards-bound, meaning that versions of the same parts do not fit a newer version of the same engine, and vice versa. The Cummins X15N engine is a clear example of a heavy-duty natural gas engine family whose fuel-system components (injectors, regulators, seals) are not “diesel equivalents,” and inventory masters need to reflect that reality [312]. That constraint should be expressed in the parts program as controlled categories and certification and rating capture.

Warranty enforcement and core rules are where ICE fleets either recover predictable value or eat avoidable cost. Cummins’ OTC warranty administrative requirements are a practical example of how warranty coverage for specific emissions components can be conditioned on on-engine diagnosis and adherence to published troubleshooting steps [25]. Core economics follows the same pattern: if a shop damages or modifies cores, credit may be denied; Horton’s published core guidance makes this explicit [41]. The parts process response should be explicit and controlled, and everyone from the technicians on the floor to the budget makers should understand parts hygiene.

Core return programs incentivize repair and remanufacturing by providing partial refunds when failed components are returned to suppliers, supporting circular-economy principles while reducing fleet costs [313]. Figure 108 details Horton’s core return policy for engine cooling fans, clutches, and related components,

June 25, 2026 Page 142

<!-- page: 143 -->

Messy Middle Powertrain Service & Maintenance

specifying acceptable core condition (no cracked housings, bearings present, shafts not bent), return timeframes typically 30 to 90 days from replacement purchase, and core charges ranging from a few hundred dollars upward depending on component complexity [41]. For alternative-fuel and electric vehicles, core return programs increasingly apply to high-value components such as traction motors, battery modules and packs, fuel cell stacks, and high-voltage inverters, where core charges can run into thousands of dollars per unit [426].

## 15.2 RD & Biodiesel Parts

Diesel remains the baseline technology for North American freight, and the diesel parts program should be treated as the template for disciplined sourcing and documentation. EPA’s heavy-duty standards framework and 40 CFR §1036.104 reinforce longer durability expectations, which increases the cost of wrong-revision installs and weak evidence capture for emissions-related data [314].

Sourcing architecture for diesel should be explicitly split. The compliance-preserving inside ring comprises OEM portals and dealer systems that support VIN-verified selection and configuration matching for emissions- and serialized components [308] [309]. Surrounding that, approved aftermarket networks and local dealer coverage exist to keep the high-speed layer moving — chassis, electrical hardware, consumables, and the small parts that routinely stop bays [110] [315] [316] [317]. This remains stable only if the fleet defines which families are OEM-required, which are approved aftermarket, and which are exception-only, and exercises control over those choices.

Warranty discipline in diesel aftertreatment is another absolute. Cummins’ OTC requirement is the clearest example: specific emissions components and electronics may require authorized diagnosis and adherence to published troubleshooting steps for warranty coverage to apply [25].

The parts ordering process should be followed only after diagnostic evidence and authorization on the work order are provided, before issuing a replacement part.

Remanufactured and core products are direct-margin levers, but only when the fleet runs them as asset recovery. Caterpillar’s remanufactured framing is a clean reference for the economics — remanufactured parts are designed to provide like-new performance at reduced cost, conditioned on core return [318]. Horton’s core acceptance guidance anchors the operational reality: modified or damaged cores can lose credit entirely [41]. Mature fleets assign ownership, define staging rules, tag cores at removal, and reconcile credits monthly so “core value” is not left to chance.

June 25, 2026 Page 143

---

<!-- page: 144 -->

Messy Middle Powertrain Service & Maintenance

Inventory design should be standardized and repeatable, not a shop-specific habit. TMC-recommended practices provide an industry governance backbone for purchasing, inventory control, and parts-room discipline — especially valuable in multi-shop environments where inconsistency can lead to downtime [288]. VMRS supports consistent coding, so the fleet can see the trend of what is failing and adjust stocking based on evidence rather than anecdotes [319].

Renewable diesel adds a parts-planning nuance that should be handled empirically. testing by UC Riverside indicates emissions differences under controlled conditions, which can influence aftertreatment loading patterns and service demand for related [79]. The correct execution is to monitor fleet-specific signals (regen frequency, service events, sensor-related derates) and tune stocking to the parts that drive downtime in that operating profile without assuming uniform outcomes across regions and duty cycles.

## 15.3 RNG Parts

RNG/CNG fleets succeed when the parts program is designed to account for the reality that high-pressure fuel systems are standards-driven and substitution-intolerant. Even when the base engine architecture resembles a diesel engine, the fuel-system components are distinct, certified, and noninterchangeable. The Cummins X15N engine is a current example of a heavy-duty natural gas engine platform that requires methane-rated components in the fuel train; this requirement must be reflected in parts master data and controlled category rules [312]. The operational consequence is that the natural gas BOM must be managed as its own controlled ecosystem, with injectors, regulators, seals, valves, and sensors sourced and handled under explicit governance.

NFPA 52 is the core code reference point for vehicular natural gas fuel systems. For containers, CSA/ANSI NGV2 is the relevant standard anchor for CNG fuel containers and cylinder lifecycle management [427]. In practical fleet operations, cylinder inspections, PRD checks, and documentation should be embedded in PM and asset records as controlled events, as these items are treated as having an expiration date.

The goal is to prevent improvisation during a fueling failure: standardize the interface families by station and vehicle configuration and store spares and seals at the trucks' fueling locations.

Vendor documentation is part of enforcement because it contains ratings and compatibility information. Parker’s CNG vehicle and fueling products catalog is a practical reference for rated components and fueling hardware that should be controlled, handled cleanly, and retained in the parts library to support correct substitution decisions [428]. DOE’s AFDC Inspector Study Guide is a useful standardized training reference for consistent inspection language and practices [320].

When referencing inspection practices, technicians should use the Inspector Study Guide exclusively. The 2008 AFDC PDF cannot be relied on for compliance purposes, as it may not reflect current standards or procedures. For specific inspection criteria, a free Digital Inspection Manual can be downloaded directly from the cylinder manufacturer. For example, Hexagon Agility Support provides updated product manuals. The manufacturer's latest documentation should be reviewed to ensure accurate and compliant inspections [321].

To obtain up-to-date study materials for certification, technicians must enroll in a current CSA-aligned course, such as those offered by NGVi or similar organizations. These courses provide the most recent materials and training aligned with certification standards.

Because certified high-pressure components can have meaningful lead times, CNG and RNG inventory strategy generally requires a higher level of strategic stocking than diesel for the “critical spares” subset — regulators, PRDs, certified seals, and interface parts that immobilize vehicles if unavailable. That does not mean blanket

June 25, 2026 Page 144

---

<!-- page: 145 -->

Messy Middle Powertrain Service & Maintenance

overstock; it means controlled min/max parts stock tied to lead time, safety-criticality, and utilization, and enforced receiving and issue discipline to prevent controlled spares from disappearing.

## 15.4 BEV Parts

BEV aftermarket readiness differs because what stop trucks are often thermal-management and high-voltage interconnect components rather than traditional consumables. The parts program should explicitly define which HV and thermal items are stocked as bay-protection spares (connectors, seals, service disconnect-related parts, selected fuses/relays where serviceable, coolant valves/pumps, sensors, and platform-specific harness segments) and should bind issuance of controlled HV components to qualified work orders so the fleet can defend safety and warranty posture. OSHA’s electrical work practices and electrical safety guidance provide the baseline compliance environment, which matters because parts issuance is inseparable from safe work execution in HV systems [322].

The warranty vs. post-warranty split should be treated as a design input because OEM support boundaries determine the extent of a fleet’s in-house access to parts and diagnostics. From the perspective of battery service practices, warranty and post-warranty periods, are extremely vitalas is knowing why and how to interpret battery pack PID data (based on chemistry). “When considering battery pack level replacement vs. internal component replacement, there are many things to consider within warranty vs. post-warranty service periods [159].” In part that means the fleet should negotiate, in writing, which service parts will be released, what diagnostic access will be provided, and what post-warranty repair options exist for packs and power electronics before the fleet scales the platform.

BEV parts programs also fail when fleets underinvest in the diagnostic toolchain required to generate defensible data for warranty and root-cause closure. OEM tools may be necessary but are not always sufficient for fleet operational needs. Procurement processes should standardize the enabling tool classes (HV meter capability, insulation test capability where required, and shop procedures that ensure readings are recorded consistently) and to treat those tools as controlled assets — calibrated, issued, tracked, and consistently used.

The supplier ecosystem becomes part of the parts plan because platform variability limits cross-compatibility Charging infrastructure suppliers also belong in the parts directory because charging failures immobilize trucks; the parts program should define the specific “charge-stopper” consumables and spares (connectors, cables, contactors where applicable, pedestal components, seals) and ensure a defined procurement path [323] [324] [325] [326].

## 15.5 Hydrogen Fuel Cell & Hydrogen ICE Parts

Hydrogen aftermarket readiness must be treated as a safety-critical parts discipline, given constrained supplier availability and potentially long lead times. The parts program should explicitly define controlled categories: high-pressure tanks, valves, regulators, fittings, sensors, seals, and fueling interface components must be sourced through qualified channels, with ratings and documentation retained in the part record. In hydrogen service, “close enough” substitution is unacceptable; the process must be designed to prevent it.

Fueling interface compatibility and protocols directly influence which parts families are acceptable. SAE J2601 and the heavy-duty extension J2601/5 define hydrogen fueling protocols. However, they are not parts catalogs; they shape the operating envelope that fueling hardware must support, so they should be included in procurement constraints for interface components (SAE J2601; SAE J2601/5). DOE’s heavy-duty hydrogen

June 25, 2026 Page 145

---

<!-- page: 146 -->

Messy Middle Powertrain Service & Maintenance

dispensing nozzle and dispenser project documentation is also a practical context for why heavy-duty dispensing components can be long-lead and why standardization is still developing [328].

On the vehicle side, the hydrogen “parts universe” includes both storage and plumbing families and balance-of-plant uptime drivers. The fleet’s supplier directory should explicitly include suppliers of storage and cylinder ecosystems and high-pressure fluid systems as part of the approved sourcing map.

Hydrogen fuel systems require specialized fittings, valves, and tubing designed to prevent embrittlement, ensure leak-tight seals at 350 to 700 bar pressures, and maintain material compatibility with H₂’s small molecular size, which can permeate conventional metals.

Figure 109 showcases Swagelok’s hydrogen-specific hardware lineup including 316L stainless steel tube fittings (resistant to hydrogen embrittlement), spring-loaded check valves (preventing reverse flow that could create detonation hazards), pressure relief devices (PRDs) calibrated for H₂ service (venting at 125% of maximum allowable working pressure) and electropolished tubing (smooth interior surface minimizing turbulence and particle contamination). Critical specifications are fittings rated for 1,000 bar (14,500 psi) to provide safety margin above 700 bar tank pressure, ASME B31.12 compliance for hydrogen piping systems, and helium leak testing to 1×10⁻⁶ mbar·L/s (detecting leaks 1,000× smaller than conventional leak detection thresholds).

For technicians, understanding hydrogen-specific hardware is essential because using conventional brass or carbon steel fittings in H₂ systems can cause catastrophic failures. Hydrogen embrittlement reduces material strength by 50% to 80%, enabling cracks that propagate to explosive ruptures. Installation requires specialized training: Swagelok-certified technicians learn proper tube cutting (preventing burrs), precise fitting assembly (correct number of turns to achieve seal), and torque specifications (preventing over-tightening that damages threads) [329] [330] [429].

Durability targets and validation cycles underscore the importance of the spare components’ strategy in the near term. DOE’s technical targets provide authoritative guidance on durability expectations for transportation fuel cell systems and stacks, supporting strategic spares planning as supply chains continue to develop [430]. In practice, if balance-of-plant components become long-lead items, the fleet must explicitly decide whether to carry spares as uptime insurance or to accept extended downtime as the norm.

Hydrogen ICE keeps some familiar mechanical architecture but not a familiar parts risk profile: seals, regulators, and material compatibility are hydrogen-specific, and substitution errors can create leak paths and safety

June 25, 2026 Page 146

<!-- page: 147 -->

Messy Middle Powertrain Service & Maintenance

hazards. Therefore, hydrogen ICE fuel-system components should be controlled with the same rigor as fuel-cell high-pressure components.

## 16 TECHNICIAN TRAINING

This section represents the culmination of this paper’s intent, which is to assist in finding a floor that technicians and maintenance experts can rely on going forward through the Messy Middle. There is no generational knowledge for these technologies as many are nearly first generational. Those aspiring technicians and technical experts forged today will be paving the path toward greater efficiency and utility.

Not only do aspiring technicians and maintenance experts need to understand the foundations of advanced technology to maintain assets, but maintenance managers and fleet decision-makers also should understand these foundations to make appropriate decisions for the business.

For those interested in a career in heavy-duty vehicle technologies but with no experience, this section is a guide through the process of gaining experience. Practicing will be difficult for those who do not already have trucks working in family business. The next best choice is to learn in an academic environment.

## 16.1 Foundation & Apprenticeship Pathway

The first 24 months of a technician's career function as the formation period for safety behavior, mechanical discipline, and regulatory compliance. This is an important time in a tech’s career. Workforce studies consistently show that early-career technicians account for a disproportionate share of preventable injuries, maintenance errors, and early attrition.

From the first day of employment, technicians in the foundation pathway are expected to operate within active shop environments under supervision, performing repetitive but safety-critical work. Preventive maintenance, inspections, lubrication, and tire services are not treated as transitional tasks but as deliberate practice for building procedural memory and hazard recognition. Federal safety guidance emphasizes that practical training must focus on the "recognition, avoidance, abatement, and prevention of workplace hazards," rather than abstract rule familiarity, particularly for workers with limited exposure to industrial work environments [331]. Fleets that accelerate inexperienced technicians into advanced diagnostic or energized work without this conditioning increase the probability of arc flash incidents, crush injuries, environmental violations, and unnecessary failed repairs.

Mandatory safety and environmental certifications are therefore imposed at the outset of the foundation period and cannot be deferred due to staffing constraints. OSHA outreach training at the 10-hour general industry level establishes baseline competency in electrical safety awareness, lockout/tagout concepts, and expectations for personal protective equipment. It is widely adopted as a minimum fleet requirement within the first 90 days of hire [331]. In parallel, EPA Section 609 Motor Vehicle Air Conditioning Certification is a federal legal requirement for any technician who services mobile A/C systems or handles regulated refrigerants for compensation, with typical certification costs ranging from $24 to $29 through approved providers [32]. Absent this credential, routine maintenance activities expose fleets to direct regulatory non-compliance.

Formal technical education during this phase is governed by cost discipline and outcome equivalence rather than institutional branding. Public community college programs remain the lowest-risk entry point for structured diesel and heavy-equipment training, with certificate programs nationally ranging from approximately $1,000 to

June 25, 2026 Page 147

---

<!-- page: 148 -->

Messy Middle Powertrain Service & Maintenance

$8,000, compared to associate degree pathways that may reach $10,000 to $40,000 depending on state residency and duration [333] [334].

By contrast, private technical institutes like Universal Technical Institute cost upwards of $40,000; UTI’s impressively tooled and resourced facilities are used to train their students [335]. Also, UTI claims “graduates of the program are prepared to take ASE examinations,” and can substitute their training for one of the two years of experience required to take the exams to become ASE certified [336].”

Once recruiting, onboarding, and administrative details are accounted for, fleets that subsidize early tooling and certification costs consistently reduce first-year turnover, thereby lowering total labor cost rather than increasing it. Gen Z workforce data indicates that practical investments are critical for retention, as the average tenure for these new entrants is just 1.1 years without clear career support from the employer [337]. Entry-level technicians are required to procure OSHA-compliant PPE and basic tooling, with 2025 estimates placing professional starter toolkits between $4,000 and $6,000 before any advanced diagnostic equipment is introduced [338]. This rapid, one-year attrition rate suggests fleets should support budding technicians after their educational phase. Increasingly, progressive fleets and dealers offer tool stipends, starter tool sets, or formal tool-assistance programs to reduce the financial barrier of building a professional toolbox.

Replacing a skilled employee can entail significant direct and indirect costs [430] [431]. Those costs quickly compound when young technicians exit before their second year. Work-based learning during the foundation pathway is formalized through apprenticeship structures that differ by jurisdiction but share standard accountability requirements. In the US, Registered Apprenticeships administered through the Department of Labor integrate paid employment with structured technical instruction, resulting in approximately 680,000 active apprentices across a range of disciplines in 2024, a 114% increase since 2014 across all industries [339]. Transportation and logistics programs constitute a subset of this growth, including diesel mechanic pathways. In Canada, technician authority is governed through provincial apprenticeship systems aligned to the Red Seal Program, which requires multi-year documentation of on-the-job hours before journeyperson certification [340].

Compensation expectations during the foundation period must be anchored to verified labor data rather than anecdotal market claims. Nationally, union membership materially affects earnings: union workers earned a verified median of $1,337 per week in 2024, compared with $1,138 for non-union workers, representing a 17.5% wage premium [341]. Regional purchasing power further modifies real wages, as Bureau of Economic Analysis Regional Price Parities range from the mid-$80,000’s to more than $110,000 meaning identical nominal pay yields substantially different living standards [433].

By the end of the 24-month foundational period, technicians can get professional certifications that verify their basic knowledge competency and alongside their verified work experience. The US requires 4,000 supervised work hours, Canadian Red Seal candidates must earn 6,000 hours for [342] [343].

## 16.2 Diesel & Traditional Internal Combustion Engine Specialization

Diesel and traditional internal combustion engine specialization remain the backbone of North American fleet maintenance and the primary employment pathway for technicians. Zero-emission heavy-duty trucks still account for only about 0.4% of new U.S. heavy-duty truck registrations in 2024 — 1,103 vehicles out of roughly 277,000 — underscoring the continued centrality of diesel skills [344].

Verified wage and employment data reinforce the economic durability of diesel specialization. The U.S. Bureau of Labor Statistics (BLS) reported a median annual wage of $60,640 for diesel service technicians and mechanics

June 25, 2026 Page 148

---

<!-- page: 149 -->

Messy Middle Powertrain Service & Maintenance

in May 2024, with employment projected to remain stable due to replacement demand of older techs in the industry, despite evolving technology [80]. Similarly, the Government of Canada Job Bank reports a national median wage of $35.00 CAD per hour for heavy-duty mechanics as of late 2025, reflecting strong demand across transport and industrial sectors [345]. These figures underscore that while propulsion technologies advance, the core economic value of skilled heavy-duty maintenance remains robust across North America.

The BLS tracks employment, wages, and workforce demographics for bus and truck mechanics, providing insights into labor market conditions affecting fleet maintenance operations. Figure 110 presents BLS occupational data for SOC code 49-3031 (Bus and Truck Mechanics and Diesel Engine Specialists), showing national employment of 299,600 technicians as of May 2024, median hourly wage of $24.15 ($50,230 annually), and projected growth of 4% from 2023 to 2033 (slower than average due to increasing vehicle reliability and telematics reducing repair needs). The data reveals regional wage variations: California averages $29.50/hour ($61,360/year), while Mississippi averages $19.20/hour ($39,936/year), reflecting cost-of-living differences and union penetration rates [80].

Pay distribution analysis reveals significant wage dispersion within the heavy-duty technician occupation, driven by specialization, certification level, geographic location, and employer type (independent shops vs. fleet maintenance vs. dealership service). Figure 111 presents BLS wage percentile data for bus and truck mechanics: the 10th percentile, typically entry-level lube technicians or tire changer with no certifications, earns $15.50/hour ($32,240/year), the 25th percentile, with 2 to 3 years’ experience and basic ASE certifications, earns $19.25/hour ($40,040/year); the 50th percentile , journeyman technicians with multiple ASE certifications and 5+ years’ experience, earns $24.15/hour ($50,230/year; the 75th percentile, comprised of senior technicians with master ASE certification or alternative fuel specializations, earns $30.25/hour ($62,920/year; and 90th percentile, comprised of shop foremen, diagnostic specialists, or OEM field service engineers earns $37.50/hour ($78,000/year).

For alternative fuel vehicles, certification premiums shift these distributions upward: BEV certification adds $3 to $5/hour, FCEV specialization adds $5 to $8/hour and combined multi-fuel expertise (diesel + BEV + FCEV + CNG) commands $10 to $15/hour premiums over diesel-only technicians. Fleet managers budgeting for alternative fuel adoption must account for these wage premiums: a 10-truck BEV fleet requiring one certified technician adds $6,000 to $10,000 in annual labor costs compared to diesel maintenance.

The cost story is not just the technician’s labor rate, but the maintenance billing rate which can exceed $200/hr. in 2026. A 2025 guide to truck repair costs highlights the findings on fleet costs shown in Figure 112 [431].

Pay distribution analysis reveals significant wage disparity within the heavy-duty technician ranks. ASE Medium/Heavy Truck certification series (T-Series) provides a nationally recognized framework for validating proficiency in diesel engines, drivetrain, brakes, suspension and steering, electrical and electronic systems, HVAC, and preventive maintenance [346]. Achievement of ASE Master Medium/Heavy Truck status signals breadth rather than specialization. Fleets and OEMs frequently use it as a gatekeeping requirement for advanced diagnostics and factory-sponsored training. Advanced diagnostic capability is further formalized through ASE L2 certification for electronically controlled diesel engines, reflecting the increasing prevalence of emissions controls, electronic fuel systems, and networked vehicle architectures.

A specialization will include the need for specialized tooling, software, and infrastructure requirements for the fleet and the technician. Technicians at this level routinely interface with OEM diagnostic platforms such as Cummins INSITE, Volvo/Mack Diagnostic and Programming Software, (or, for example with offroad equipment, Caterpillar Electronic Technician), access to which is typically governed through dealer agreements or paid subscriptions [347] [348]. Therefore, it should be expected that fleets help a technician with tool access and training to prevent misdiagnoses, improper repairs, and warranty chargebacks.

Safety governance in diesel specialization extends beyond basic hazard awareness to include exposure management and procedural control. Diesel maintenance environments pose documented risks from high-pressure fuel systems, rotating assemblies, thermal hazards, and exhaust emissions. OSHA guidance identifies diesel exhaust as a workplace contaminant requiring ventilation and exposure controls, particularly in enclosed maintenance facilities [349]. In addition, technicians routinely interact with fuel and aftertreatment systems operating at high temperatures and pressures, making adherence to safety imperative.
