---

<!-- page: 152 -->

Messy Middle Powertrain Service & Maintenance

Completion of the diesel specialization phase is not defined by time served but by verified capability and risk readiness. Technicians completing this phase are expected to demonstrate documented proficiency in emissions diagnostics, electronic engine controls, drivetrain systems, and regulatory-compliant repair documentation. This level of competence, attention to detail, and experience in effective written and verbal service write ups and communication is a prerequisite for working on battery-electric or hydrogen platforms, where the technical risks are compounded.

## 16.3 BEV Specialization

Battery-electric vehicle specialization is a conditional, infrastructure-dependent extension of the diesel foundation rather than a universal replacement pathway. Despite increasing regulatory attention and capital investment, verified national data show that zero-emission heavy-duty vehicles remain a small minority of the active fleet, with deployment concentrated in port drayage, urban delivery, and hub-to-hub operations rather than long-haul corridors [350]. Fleet service manual governance manuals treat BEV specialization as a regional accelerator, authorized only where vehicle procurement, charging infrastructure, and utility coordination are contractually established. Advancing technicians into BEV roles without these conditions results in stranded training investments and exposes fleets to safety and utilization risks without operational benefits.

The defining characteristic of BEV specialization is the introduction of high-voltage electrical energy as a primary hazard, thereby fundamentally delineating the skill and safety boundaries of this pathway. National safety standards explicitly require that only "qualified persons" perform work on or near exposed energized conductors, with retraining intervals not to exceed three years under NFPA 70E [270].

Training and credentialing for BEV specialization currently relies on a combination of industry certifications and OEM-specific programs because there is no fully mature, universal credentialing framework. ASE offers the L3 Hybrid/Electric Vehicle Specialist certification as a means of validating advanced knowledge of electric drivetrains, energy storage systems, regenerative braking, and power electronics, with a verified exam fee of $118 plus a $34 registration fee [351]. However, ASE certification alone does not authorize high-voltage work; it must be paired with NFPA-aligned safety training and OEM platform instruction. FSM governance, therefore, treats ASE L3 as a competency indicator rather than a standalone license to perform energized work.

OEMs provide training that will only support a specific product, but they don’t provide the deeper education that is needed to learn the broader operation, diagnostics, and failure modes of electrification technologies.

“Skills gaps that our company has experienced for the past few decades, as a part of managers and technicians transitioning into the electrification space are significant. From our perspective, fleet technicians and managers will need to receive significant education and training to fully and successfully transition into the electrified vehicle space; there is no question. Fleet techs need solid background in electrical systems and a working knowledge of electronics. Technicians need to start retraining now,” according to Quarto [159].

BEV specialization also introduces new facility-level constraints that directly affect technician utilization. Depot electrification requires coordination with utilities, electrical contractors, and permitting authorities, often extending timelines beyond vehicle delivery schedules. Federal guidance under the National Zero-Emission Freight Corridor Strategy outlined a phased deployment, progressing from hub locations to multi-state corridors through 2040, underscoring the non-uniform availability of charging [416]. NACFE field studies demonstrate that successful BEV deployment occurs within specific depot archetypes where electrical capacity, duty cycles, and dwell times are aligned, rather than through blanket fleet conversion [416].

June 25, 2026 Page 152

---

<!-- page: 153 -->

Messy Middle Powertrain Service & Maintenance

Economic outcomes for BEV-specialized technicians vary widely by region and are not yet supported by stable national wage premiums. While job postings in specific markets advertise higher hourly rates for electric or advanced diagnostics roles, these figures remain market samples rather than statistically verified differentials and must be treated cautiously. FSM wage governance requires fleets to benchmark BEV compensation locally, using the BLS’s Occupational Employment and Wage Statistics as a baseline, adjusted for regional cost of living and union coverage, rather than extrapolating from early-adopter markets [352]. Any claimed wage premium not tied to a collective bargaining agreement or verified survey is classified as provisional and subject to periodic review.

Operational work during the BEV specialization phase centers on commissioning, inspection, and controlled diagnostics rather than component-level repair. Technicians are expected to perform verified de-energization, insulation resistance testing, battery state-of-health evaluation, thermal management checks, and fault isolation using OEM diagnostic platforms. These tasks require disciplined documentation because high-voltage incidents carry heightened regulatory and liability exposure.

Completion of the BEV specialization phase is defined by demonstrated capability to work safely and effectively within electrified environments, not by the volume of BEV assets serviced. Technicians entering this phase must also retain diesel competency while adding verifiable high-voltage qualifications, ensuring fleet flexibility during mixed-powertrain operations. FSM governance explicitly rejects the notion of irreversible "BEV-only" career tracks at this stage, recognizing that diesel and electric platforms will coexist for decades and that workforce resilience depends on cross-platform competence rather than early specialization.

## 16.4 RNG/CNG Specialization

CNG and RNG are deployed at commercial scale in refuse, transit, return-to-base freight, airport ground support, and vocational fleet applications throughout the US, supported by established OEM vehicle platforms and permanent fueling infrastructure [353]. Federal alternative fuel deployment data identify natural gas as one of the most widely adopted alternative transportation fuels currently in service, with sustained medium- and heavy-duty fleet utilization rather than pilot-stage deployment [353].

Training programs preparing technicians for inspection certification typically identify 7 to 14 hours of structured instruction as a baseline for experienced technicians prior to an examination, followed by a formal evaluation of inspection competence [1]. Inspection training covers cylinder construction types, allowable damage criteria, mounting integrity, pressure relief device configuration, and post-incident inspection triggers [1].

Advanced natural gas technician training extends beyond inspection into platform-specific diagnostics and system integration. Heavy-duty natural gas engines designed for long-haul and high-load applications require understanding ignition strategies, fuel delivery architecture, electronic controls, and emissions system integration [434]. The X15N natural gas engine is positioned for applications historically dominated by diesel, altering service expectations and diagnostic workflows for natural gas technicians.

Public labor statistics do not classify CNG or RNG technicians as a distinct occupation. Compensation benchmarks therefore rely on diesel service technician categories for national wage baselines [354]. Within this framework, natural gas specialization functions as a safety-critical capability rather than a separately quantified wage premium [354].

## 16.5 Hydrogen & Fuel-Cell Specialization

Hydrogen fuel-cell specialist roles represent a distinct advanced specialization within organizations deploying this technology. These roles are distinguished by the presence of high-pressure gaseous fuel, hydrogen-specific safety hazards, cryogenics, electrification, and technical details that do not exist in conventional diesel operations. Personnel operating in these roles are responsible not only for vehicle systems, but for maintaining safe interaction between vehicles, fueling stations, maintenance facilities, and emergency response actions.

Code of Practice for Hydrogen Fueled Vehicles and Maintenance Workshops requires that personnel engaged in hydrogen vehicle operation and maintenance receive training, instruction, and supervision commensurate with the risks associated with hydrogen fuel, and that they demonstrate competence in safely carrying out assigned tasks, including emergency handling and use of safety devices. Recently, Hong Kong has employed hydrogen in an initiative to become carbon neutral by 2050 and is therefore training many hydrogen specialists. Many fundamental technicians’ tasks can be outlined in their Code of Practice For Hydrogen Filling Stations manual [355].

Hydrogen specialization also carries electrical safety implications that extend beyond fuel handling. Fuel-cell vehicles incorporate high-voltage balance-of-plant systems, power electronics, and electrically actuated safety components that require documented electrical safety training. Emergency response documentation for heavy-duty fuel-cell vehicles illustrates the level of knowledge necessary to approach an incapacitated vehicle [356].

OEM emergency response guidance further underscores the consequences of insufficient training. The Nikola Tre Emergency Response Guide repeatedly cautions responders against contacting or disabling high-voltage components without appropriate training and personal protective equipment, emphasizing the risk of serious injury when energized systems are handled improperly [435].

From an OEM and fleet economics perspective, hydrogen training is treated as an enabling investment rather than a discretionary expense. Cummins’ hydrogen engine program states: “Technician training and service location retooling can cover multiple fuel types — [and] will lower total cost of ownership for the end user” [196]. This framing reflects the recognition that hydrogen viability depends on workforce readiness and service infrastructure capability as much as on vehicle technology itself.

In certain facility configurations, hydrogen maintenance operations also may fall under OSHA Process Safety Management requirements. Under 29 CFR 1910.119, hydrogen is classified as a highly hazardous chemical with a threshold quantity of 10,000 lbs. Facilities exceeding this threshold are required to implement formal process hazard analyses, management of change procedures, and documented training programs tied to system modifications and operating conditions [357]. While many fleet depots operate below this threshold, hub-scale hydrogen storage and fueling installations may trigger full PSM applicability, further elevating training and documentation requirements for affected personnel.

Taken together, these requirements establish hydrogen fuel-cell specialization as a training-intensive role category defined by safety-critical competence, regulatory alignment, and controlled authorization. Completion of this pathway is therefore measured not by years of service, but by demonstrated ability to operate within hydrogen safety envelopes, fueling protocols, and emergency response frameworks. Personnel functioning successfully at this level provide the human control layer that enables hydrogen vehicle deployments to proceed safely, legally, and at scale within a constrained but expanding hydrogen ecosystem.

## 16.6 Engineering & Technologist Progression

A pathway should be thought of as role reclassification rather than a linear promotion, reflecting a shift in scope from individual vehicle repair to analytical oversight of assets, depots, and supporting systems, and project management. These roles include research and development engineer, sales engineer, field service engineer, and eventually engineer.

The BLS reports that engineering technologists classified under SOC 17-3029 earn a mean annual wage of $75,290, with 66,200 positions nationwide. The BLS states a mechanical engineering technologist median pay is $68,730, while the median pay of an electronic engineering technician is $77,180 per year [358] [359].

Geographic distribution of engineering technologists reveals regional concentrations of technical talent that support commercial truck maintenance, with implications for alternative fuel vehicle deployment strategies and workforce development priorities. Figure 113 maps BLS data for SOC code 17-3029 (Engineering Technologists and Technicians, Except Drafters), showing state-by-state employment levels and location quotients that indicate specialization. California leads with 42,800 engineering technologists (location quotient 1.23), followed by Texas (38,200, LQ 1.15) and Michigan (14,600, LQ 1.82) — indicating high specialization relative to state size. These concentrations correlate with automotive manufacturing, aerospace, and defense industries that develop advanced vehicle technologies.

For alternative fuel adoption, states with high engineering technologist concentrations offer advantages: an established technical workforce capable of supporting BEV/FCEV maintenance training, proximity to OEM engineering support for troubleshooting complex failures, and community college programs aligned with industry needs. Conversely, states with low concentrations (Mississippi: 890 technologists, Montana: 510) face workforce challenges that may slow alternative fuel adoption as fleets struggle to recruit certified technicians.

The map informs infrastructure investment decisions. California, Texas, and Michigan justify heavy investment in technologist training programs given large addressable markets, while rural states may require regional service hub models where fleets transport vehicles 100 to 200 miles for specialized maintenance.

This distinction is operationally significant. Engineering technologists are not defined primarily by their hands-on repair skills, but by responsibility for analysis, documentation, system behavior, commissioning support, and feedback into engineering and operational decision-making. Entry into engineering technologist roles, therefore, represents a transition into a different occupational domain rather than an incremental advancement within the same classification.

National wage data reinforce this occupational distinction. BLS reports that engineering technologists (SOC 17-3029) earn a mean annual wage of $75,290, with 66,200 positions nationwide. Example wages are shown in Figure 114. The wage distribution spans from a 10th percentile of $42,900 annually to a 90th percentile of $109,190 annually. By contrast, bus and truck mechanics and diesel engine specialists (SOC 49-3031) report a mean annual wage of $59,920, with a 90th percentile of $70,470. These figures provide a defensible external anchor for compensation philosophy, aligning engineering technologist pay bands more closely with engineering and technical-specialist roles than with bay-level mechanic classifications [361].

Federal safety regulations illustrate this expectation clearly. OSHA's electric-power standard (29 CFR 1910.269) defines a "qualified person" as "one knowledgeable in the construction and operation of the electric power generation, transmission, and distribution equipment involved, along with the associated hazards," and clarifies that "the employee must have the training required by 1910.269(a)(2)(ii) to be considered a qualified person."

Messy Middle Powertrain Service & Maintenance

This definition aligns with a policy that prohibits lateral entry into engineering technologist roles without documented field grounding and formal training. The role is explicitly designed to meet "qualified person" expectations, not merely generalized technician competency [362].

In the industry, educational pathways are to develop competency. In practice, many engineering technologists build capability through part-time associate- or bachelor-level engineering technology programs while remaining employed. Community college engineering technology programs commonly require 60 to 70 credit hours and are structured to accommodate working adults completing coursework over three to four years on a part-time basis. Cost structures and timelines vary by institution, but tuition schedules consistently demonstrate that these programs are designed to coexist with employment rather than replace it.

Governance aligns engineering technologist roles with decision authority commensurate with risk. Where technologists are accountable for commissioning, system modification, or performance validation, they are also expected to maintain the documentation, training currency, and analytical rigor necessary to support those decisions. This balance between authority and accountability is central to the pathway's design.

The engineering technologist and engineering technician pathway formalizes a transition from task-level execution to system-level stewardship. It recognizes that modern fleet operations depend on roles capable of integrating mechanical knowledge with analytical reasoning, regulatory literacy, and operational economics. By grounding this pathway in federal occupational classifications, defensible wage benchmarks, documented safety expectations, and realistic educational models, the organization ensures that progression into engineering technologist roles is structured, transparent, and aligned with enterprise-level risk and performance objectives.

## 17 CONCLUSIONS

Successful, safe freight operations require equipment to operate when needed, and keeping trucks, trailers, drivers and loads continuously moving invariably requires attention to equipment maintenance and repair. Vehicle complexity has increased since the first true freight trucks hit the roads in the early 1900s, but the industry has seen an explosion of technology choices since 2020 that include more efficient versions of diesel engines with improved emission reductions, new, alternative fuels in various forms and blends of renewable diesels and renewable natural gases, and new technologies like battery electric and hydrogen fuel cells. The transportation industry is even starting to embrace a future that may include autonomous vehicles. Artificial intelligence is beginning to factor into improving trucking operations including maintenance and repair. NACFE has labeled this period the Messy Middle reflecting an overabundance of viable technology choices in a rapidly evolving and increasingly competitive freight marketplace.

Uptime is crucial to fleet economics, as the saying goes, “if the wheels ain’t turnin’, fleets ain’t earnin’.” Keeping trucks running requires well-trained technicians supported with appropriate tools in shop facilities capable of dealing with the breadth of technologies. Truck and trailer manufacturers are critical to rapidly diagnosing problems, helping provide solutions, troubleshooting tools, data on failure modes, training in new technologies, on-going auditing and certification of skills and equipment, and in many cases, providing timely parts supply for proprietary systems.

Fleets that operate their own shops need to encourage hiring, training, and motivating technicians. The shortage of adequately trained technicians receives much less attention than potential shortages of drivers, but is equally concerning to successful operations. Fleets that use third party service support need to press their providers to stay ahead of the technology demands.

Messy Middle Powertrain Service & Maintenance

Encompassing a year of interviews with industry experts and extensive literature research, this report is a guidebook for OEMs, fleets, drivers, dealerships, shops and technicians on the breadth of complexity facing truck and trailer maintenance and service in the Messy Middle. The report highlights the growing resources available to help the industry improve technician training and help prepare service center operations to better serve fleet needs.

Findings in the report, as seen in Figure 115, are:

1. Maintenance costs are a significant factor in investing in new trucks. Those costs also often include facility infrastructure maintenance, special equipment and training for technicians, and on-going auditing and record keeping.
2. The skills of the technicians and the tools available to them will continue to be key to high uptime. Intelligent OEM service tools and fault-diagnosis systems will be critical to reducing downtime.
3. Service of natural gas, hydrogen, battery electric, and bio-fuel systems demand greater attention to safety and equipment protocols than traditional diesels.
4. Training technicians and keeping that training current will need to be a priority for service managers, including tracking and auditing skills and certifications. The pace of technology change will require continuous training and recertifications.
5. Computers will likely be the first tools needed to diagnose and service vehicles. They also will need to be kept current with appropriate software licensing and regular hardware updating that will need to be factored into costs.
6. OEM warranties will become even more critical as the complexity of vehicles increases with new technologies.
7. For the foreseeable future, standards and procedures will be rapidly evolving for as fleets gain experience and provide feedback to OEMs. Staying aware of changing standards and staying compliant may be an additional labor cost to factor in investment planning.
8. Behind the fence fueling infrastructure maintenance will be as challenging and may be necessary to keep trucks running. Public infrastructure maintenance or lack thereof may also factor into vehicle uptime.
9. Predictive maintenance enhanced by artificial intelligence systems will greatly aid technicians.
10. Specialization in new technologies is inevitable for service technicians as the complexity of vehicles continues to grow. This may require additional staffing to cover all the critical new technologies to keep fleets moving.

## APPENDIX A – SCHOOLS

### Community Colleges (ROI Leaders)

1. El Camino College: El Camino’s CDL program is a four- to six-week training track that costs approximately $2,500. With a reported placement rate near 85%, it offers one of the fastest pathways into trucking careers.
2. Fresno City College: With a verified tuition of $2,530, Fresno City College offers one of the most affordable diesel programs in California. Students complete a 55-unit curriculum that blends classroom theory with shop-based training on late-model engines.
3. Northeast Community College: This Nebraska program offers dual concentrations in truck and agricultural diesel technology. More than 1,000 hours of laboratory work and a 360-hour internship make it widely applicable and employer-connected.
4. Rio Hondo College: Rio Hondo is a national leader in BEV and alternative fuel technician training, with contracts from Volvo, Tesla, and Rivian. The program integrates diesel, hybrid, and high-voltage systems and serves as a workforce pipeline for California’s clean truck sector.
5. San Bernardino Valley College: SBVC partnered with Volvo on the LIGHTS project, becoming the first US college to graduate battery-electric truck technicians. Its diesel curriculum emphasizes high-voltage safety and hands-on BEV diagnostics, preparing students for advanced fleet service roles.
6. Santa Ana College: The Santa Ana diesel and heavy equipment program covers engines, powertrains, electrical systems, and welding. It serves both entry-level students and incumbent workers seeking to upgrade technical skills (Santa Ana College – Diesel and Heavy Equipment Technology).

### Private Technical Institutes

1. Arizona Automotive Institute: AAI’s 13-month diesel heavy truck program blends theory with hands-on training. Tuition ranges from $25,000 to $30,000, and the program is designed for rapid workforce entry.
2. Lincoln Tech operates six diesel campuses, featuring ASE-certified instructors and dedicated diesel shops. Its 83-week associate degree program includes truck service management and technical diagnostics (Lincoln Tech – Diesel Technology
3. Pennsylvania College of Technology: Penn College offers a two-year A.A.S. in diesel technology, with a verified placement rate of 97%. Graduates earn an average starting salary of $52,000, supported by extensive laboratories and modern equipment.
4. Universal Technical Institute (UTI) operates 11 campuses that offer diesel training and Manufacturer-Specific Advanced Training (MSAT) partnerships. Tuition averages $40,000 to $45,000, and one-year placement rates are reported at 80% to 85%.
5. University of Northwestern Ohio (UNOH) integrates its diesel program with the Mack/Volvo DATE pipeline, providing students with direct access to OEM dealer networks. Its curriculum spans powertrain, electronics, and emissions systems.

June 25, 2026
NACFE

Messy Middle Powertrain Service & Maintenance

6. WyoTech: WyoTech’s nine-month diesel program is 60% hands-on and draws national recruiters. Employer fairs regularly feature more than 90 companies interviewing students on-site.

### OEM & Dealer Programs

1. Caterpillar ThinkBIG: ThinkBIG is a two-year associate program with alternating dealer and classroom rotations. Supported by CAT dealers, students gain paid internships, tools, and guaranteed job pathways.
2. Cummins TAP: The Cummins Technical Apprenticeship Program combines paid work with classroom instruction. Graduates earn Cummins product certifications while employed by sponsoring dealers (Cummins Technician Apprentice Program).
3. Daimler/Detroit Service Training Center: Daimler operates an expanded facility in Detroit that offers advanced courses. Its curriculum now includes ePowertrain training to support the deployment of electric Freightliner Cascadias (DTNA Detroit Service Training Center).
4. Freightliner Finish First: This 12-week program is designed to funnel graduates into Freightliner dealerships. Students train on Cascadia and Western Star trucks with factory diagnostic systems.
5. Mack/Volvo DATE: The Diesel Advanced Technology Education program provides 500+ hours of OEM-specific training. Graduates earn Mack and Volvo certifications and are placed directly into the dealer network.
6. TEC Equipment: TEC Equipment, Volvo’s most prominent West Coast dealer, was the first to earn BEV certification. It provides specialized high-voltage training for technicians to support Volvo’s VNR Electric rollout (Volvo Trucks North America).
7. Truck Center Companies: This Freightliner/Western Star dealer group operates two training centers: a 25,000 sq. ft. facility in Omaha and a 13,000 sq. ft. facility in Waterloo. Both provide specialized instruction in diagnostics, engines, and transmissions (Truck Center Companies Training Center).

## Additional Programs

### Community Colleges

1. Central Community College (NE): Diesel tech with Caterpillar dealer scholarships.
2. College of Western Idaho: ASE-certified heavy truck technician program.
3. Iowa Western CC: 34-week diesel diploma.
4. Lewis-Clark State College: Diesel technology associate degree.
5. Merced College: Diesel program emphasizing 70% lab time.
6. Palomar College: Diesel mechanics training serving San Diego fleets.

# APPENDIX C – CONTROLLER AREA NETWORK (CAN) PRIMER

## Overview

Modern heavy-duty trucks use multiple Controller Area Network (CAN) data links to coordinate engines, transmissions, brakes, and auxiliary systems. The ISO 11898 CAN standard defines the physical and data link layers, while SAE J1939 provides the heavy-duty messaging framework. Legacy modules still may rely on SAE J1708/J1587 serial protocols, creating a mixed-network environment in many fleets [363]. Diagnostics, therefore, require identifying which link is active, verifying the baud rate (250 vs. 500 kbps), and choosing the correct adapter channel. Secure, high-speed variants such as SAE J1939-22 CAN FD and SAE J1939-91C Secure Messaging are increasingly prevalent, necessitating updated tools and adapters.

## Quick Start Workflow

To access the network, connect an RP1210 adapter, such as the Nexiq USB Link, to the 9-pin Deutsch DLC and to your PC. Launch your sniffer or diagnostic software (Device Manager, DAVIE4, Tech Tool), then select the correct protocol/channel. Channel 1 typically carries J1939 traffic (e.g., SA 0 = Engine, SA 3 = Transmission, SA 11 = ABS), while Channel 2 often hosts J1708/J1587 MIDs such as MID 130 (Transmission) or MID 136 (Brakes) [364] [365]. If no traffic appears, confirm power/grounds and check for ~60 Ω across CAN-H/L, which indicates proper termination [366].

## OEM-Specific Practices

**PACCAR MX (2016–2021):** Some builds route critical signals on CAN Channel 3. In these cases, the VIN reads, but the diagnostic menus in DAVIE4 remain greyed out. The solution is a CAN3→CAN1 crossover adapter that routes scan tool traffic [367] [368].

**Volvo VNL/VNR (including BEV):** Uses multiple links — DL1 for J1939 backbone, DL2 for SAE J2284 high-speed diagnostics at 500 kbps, and DL4 for legacy J1587. ECU programming in Premium Tech Tool requires a connection to DL2; failure to do so results in session errors [369].

**Navistar:** Employs separate Drivetrain J1939, Body J1939, and Engine private J1939 networks, making termination and stub checks essential [366].

## Tools and Adapters

Most fleet shops standardize on Nexiq RP1210 adapters, with eTechnician software now covering PACCAR and Detroit applications through MY 2024 [370]. A backup adapter, such as the DG Technologies DPA XL, is recommended for compatibility edge cases. As Clean Truck Check (HD I/M) expands OBD-based compliance, fleets should maintain a repeatable diagnostic workflow: sniff the link, identify the backbone, then run the correct OEM software [371]. Documenting link settings and baud rates in repair orders is recommended to ensure reproducibility.

## Shop-Floor Checklist

* Verify DLC power/grounds and ~60 Ω baseline.
* Sniff frames to confirm active link and baud.
* Toggle 250 vs. 500 kbps if no traffic.
* Select the correct backbone (e.g., Volvo DL2 for programming).
* Match adapter session to channel; use RP1210 compliance.
* For PACCAR MY2016–2021, add CAN3→CAN1 if VIN reads but tests are greyed.
* Record network settings in the RO to streamline repeat work.

## Training and Technician Use

Field tutorials confirm standard addressing: “Source Address 0 = Engine #1,” “SA 3 = Transmission,” and legacy modules communicating via J1708 MIDs [372]. These embedded examples reinforce dual-stack practice and make the primer suitable for technicians onboarding. By combining Tier-1 standards with OEM-specifics and aftermarket adapter support, the appendix equips technicians to manage mixed-protocol networks and ensures compliance with both diagnostic and regulatory requirements.

## Volvo OEM Architecture: What to Expect on VNL/VNR (and BEV)

Volvo’s bodybuilder documentation spells out multiple data links: J1939 backbones for vehicle/powertrain, legacy J1587/J1708 for specific diagnostics and data reporting, and a dedicated diagnostic link defined by SAE J2284 used by the service tool for programming (Volvo Trucks North America, “Body Builder Instructions — Electrical/Electronic Control Unit (Section 3),” 2025). Specifically, “Backbone 2 (J2284) is a dedicated diagnostic data link for powertrain control modules with a signal transfer speed of 500 kbit/ used by the service tool to reprogram the VMCU, TGW, and other control units” (Volvo Trucks North America, “Body Builder — Section 0, General Guidelines,” 2024).

Volvo’s VECU/MID144 materials also emphasize using the Serial Communication Port with the manufacturer's scan tool for deeper testing and note that additional data becomes available when a diagnostic tool is connected — consistent with your Channel-by-Channel approach (Volvo, “Vehicle Electronic Control Unit (MID 144) Diagnostic Trouble Code Guide”).

**Actionable for Tech Tool:** If Tech Tool cannot program an ECU, verify you are sessioned on the J2284 diagnostic backbone and network prerequisites; Volvo’s PTT FAQ provides error code guidance and workflow checks (Volvo Trucks, “Premium Tech Tool: Frequently Asked Questions”)

# APPENDIX D – HIGH VOLTAGE SAFETY CHECKS BEFORE WORK

## Technician Work Procedures (BEV or FCEV Shop)

**Scope.** Qualified EV technicians only. Follow the shop HV SOP, OEM service manual, and LOTO. Use CAT-rated equipment and PPE in accordance with NFPA 70E / OSHA Electrical Work Practices.

### Intake & Triage

Verify concern, retrieve DTCs, and capture BMS, inverter, and thermal data. Electric truck maintenance experience indicates that traction batteries primarily require periodic condition inspections rather than frequent physical maintenance (Battery-Electric Trucks: Maintenance 101, Heavy Duty Trucking).

If safety-critical (isolation fault or thermal event), move to a secure bay and disable HV.

### HV Disable (DMM-Based) and Absence of Voltage

Apply LOTO and remove the MSD in accordance with OEM procedures. GM upfitter guidance outlines a manual, hands-on method for de-energizing high-voltage systems on EV models (GM Upfitter – UI Bulletin 198c, High Voltage Disabling EV Models).

Perform a live-dead-live absence-of-voltage verification consistent with electrical testing safety guidance (Fluke – Electrical Testing Safety: Preparing for Absence of Voltage Testing). Gloves, meters, and leads must all be rated at or above the maximum system voltage (Fluke – Electrical Safety and High-Voltage Measurement Basics).

### HVIL Diagnostics

Measure HVIL voltage and current using the OEM tool or a TDS. Tesla high-voltage interlock loop service bulletins describe a BMS-driven low-current loop and connector conditions that can cause intermittent HVIL faults (Tesla – Service Bulletin SB-13-44-003, HVIL Connector Upgrade; NHTSA HVIL Technical Bulletin).

Use a breakout box or OEM-approved adapter for testing; do not pierce insulation or back-probe unsupported circuits.

### Isolation Verification and Insulation Resistance

Conduct insulation-resistance (megohmmeter) testing on high-voltage subsystems before re-energization and compare measured values to OEM limits. One common approach is to use a 1 kV insulation tester in the Fluke 1535/1537 class (Fluke 1535/1537 Insulation Testers).

NHTSA technical guidance on BEV and fuel-cell isolation notes that if the pack bus voltages read zero. At the same time, the BMS continues to report internal isolation faults after guided tests, and traction battery replacement may be required (NHTSA – Failure Modes and Effects Analysis for Hydrogen Fuel Cell Vehicles).

### DC Current Measurement (Clamp)

Use a Hall-effect DC clamp for non-intrusive current measurement. Clamp-meter application guidance describes selecting the DC range, zeroing the sensor, and clamping a single conductor for accurate readings (Fluke – How to Measure Current with a Clamp Meter).

For accessory clamps with millivolt-per-amp output, set the DMM to an appropriate DC millivolt range and apply the probe’s scaling factor (Fluke – How to Measure Current with a Clamp Accessory). Automotive AC/DC probes in the Keysight 1146B class support measurements of 0.1 A to 100 A without opening the circuit (Keysight – 1146B AC/DC Current Probe).

June 25, 2026
NACFE

<!-- page: 198 -->

Messy Middle Powertrain Service & Maintenance

### Thermal and Fluid Service Anchors

Verify thermal loops (battery, axle, power electronics) for flow, leaks, and hose condition, and service coolant at OEM intervals. Volvo heavy-duty service schedules and dealer summaries call out BEV-specific inspections and coolant checks in addition to standard chassis intervals (Volvo Truck Service Schedule Overview).

Inspect axle fluids per OEM schedule; confirm that vents and seals are intact and leak-free.

### Re-enable, Road Test, and Documentation

Re-enable the high-voltage system per the OEM scan tool procedure after absence-of-voltage verification and reassembly. GM upfitter documentation provides specific high-voltage enabling steps for Bolt EV/EUV and similar platforms (GM Upfitter – High Voltage Enabling Bolt EV/EUV).

Road-test with data capture (state-of-charge change, thermal behavior, regenerative-braking behavior, and any recurring DTCs). Attach meter screenshots, HVIL readings, insulation-resistance results, and torque or closure confirmations to the work order, and note any parts replaced under service bulletins or recalls (DOE Fuel Cell Balance of Plant Reliability Testbed).
